# PHASE 13: AI-Era Full Stack Skills

> Phase 13 is not a replacement for Phases 1â€“12. It is the layer that sits on top of them. Every
> topic here assumes you can already build a typed HTTP API, model a domain, write migrations,
> ship a frontend with state management, reason about latency and cost, and debug production.
> What Phase 13 adds is a new class of dependency â€” a probabilistic one â€” and a new class of
> failure mode that is not reproducible by re-running the request.
>
> The through-line: **the model is a component with a prompt, a schema, a latency budget, a cost
> budget, and a distribution of outputs. Treat it like any other untrusted dependency and the
> hard parts become ordinary engineering problems.**

---

## 13.1 What changed, and what did not

### 13.1.1 The new surface area of a full-stack job

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** "AI-era full stack" means the surface area of your job gained a new dependency class:
a language model reached over the network, billed per token, non-deterministic, and capable of
producing output that is syntactically valid and semantically wrong. The job did not become
"prompt engineering." It became **software engineering around an unreliable oracle**.

**Mechanics.** Concretely, the new surface area is a set of responsibilities that had no analogue in
a traditional CRUD stack:

1. **Model selection and routing.** No single model is right for every call. You now choose per
   endpoint: a small fast model for classification and routing, a large model for synthesis and
   code, an embedding model for retrieval, a small model for reranking.
2. **Token budgeting.** Every prompt is a budget you defend. System prompt, retrieved context,
   conversation history, tool schemas, and the completion all compete for a fixed context window.
3. **Output contracts.** You cannot rely on "it returns JSON sometimes." You pin a schema, validate,
   and retry or degrade.
4. **Evaluation.** Traditional tests assert exact equality against a known answer. Model output has
   no single correct answer, so you need golden datasets, rubric scoring, and LLM-judge.
5. **Guardrails.** Input that reaches a model becomes prompt-injection surface. Output that leaves a
   model becomes a rendering and policy surface. Actions a model can take become an authorization
   surface.
6. **Cost and latency accounting.** Token spend is a per-feature COGS line. Latency is
   multi-second and streaming is the default expectation for chat-shaped UX.
7. **Observability that includes the prompt.** Traditional traces show `handler -> db`. AI traces
   must also show the resolved prompt, the model, the token counts, the retrieved chunk IDs, and the
   validation verdict.
8. **Data governance.** Prompts and completions are often user content stored in your database. That
   makes retention, redaction, and subject-access requests an engineering concern.
9. **Human-in-the-loop surfaces.** Because the model is unreliable, product surfaces must support
   "draft, review, approve." Building those UIs is now a core full-stack skill.
10. **Vendor abstraction.** You will want to swap providers. That requires a normalization layer and
    an eval set that proves the swap did not regress.

**The realistic shape of the work.** In a mature team, an AI feature is roughly:

```
frontend (streaming UI, optimistic states, review/approve affordance)
   |
app server (session, authz, rate limit, cost meter)
   |
orchestration layer (route â†’ prompt â†’ retrieve â†’ assemble â†’ call â†’ validate â†’ repair)
   |
   â”œâ”€â”€ prompt registry (versioned, typed, testable)
   â”œâ”€â”€ retrieval service (index, embed, search, rerank)
   â”œâ”€â”€ tool layer (schemas, authorization, confirmation, audit)
   â””â”€â”€ providers (adapter per vendor, one internal contract)
   |
infra (queues, caches, vector store, relational store, secret manager)
```

**When to use / when not to.** Use these skills when the product genuinely needs model capability
(summarization, extraction, classification of messy text, semantic search, natural-language
interfaces). Do not build an agent framework because a feature ships in two weeks and the
implementation is 400 lines of deterministic code. 13.6.2 covers this in detail.

**Trade-offs.** Every AI capability you add is a new thing to be on call for. A deterministic
function fails in one way. A model-backed feature fails in a distribution of ways, some of which
you cannot reproduce from the logs because you did not log enough.

**Production considerations.**
- Feature flags around every AI capability, with a kill switch that does not require a deploy.
- A fallback path that is not the model. If summarization is unavailable, show the first 200 words.
- Budget enforcement at the edge: per-user, per-tenant, per-day token caps.
- Separate SLIs for the model call and the surrounding system. "p95 latency" for a chat endpoint
  is meaningless if it is dominated by queueing.

**Common mistakes.**
- Treating the model as a function call with no error handling.
- Building the whole feature before writing the eval set, so you have no definition of "better."
- Logging prompts nowhere, so the first bad report is unreproducible.
- Assuming output shape stability without a schema and a validator.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Walk me through how you'd ship an AI feature end to end. What's the first thing you write?"
- "How do you know your prompt change didn't make things worse?"
- "What's the most surprising thing about working with models in production?"

---

### 13.1.2 Which parts of your existing fundamentals matter more now, not less

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** AI features amplify fundamentals. They do not replace them. The engineers who do well
here are the ones who already had strong opinions about interfaces, data modelling, and failure
handling.

**Mechanics.** What matters more:

| Fundamental | Why it matters more with models |
| --- | --- |
| **Interface design** | You now design the interface between *your system and an unreliable component*. Narrow, typed, well-documented interfaces are what let you swap vendors and add fallbacks. |
| **Data modelling** | Conversation state, tool calls, retrieved chunks, and eval runs are all structured data with lifecycles. Most "AI problems" are really data-modelling problems. |
| **Idempotency and retries** | Model calls are slow and flaky. Retry semantics, deduplication, and exactly-once effects on the *side effect* (the tool call) matter enormously. |
| **Backpressure and queues** | Long-running generation must not hold an HTTP request open for 30 seconds. Queueing and concurrency limits are core, not optional. |
| **Caching** | Prompt caching and semantic caching are the single biggest cost lever. You need cache-key design skills. |
| **AuthN/AuthZ** | Tool calling moves authorization into the model loop. If you authorize at the wrong layer you get privilege escalation. |
| **Observability** | Distributed tracing, structured logs, and cardinality control all apply, with the addition of prompt/version/token fields. |
| **Testing discipline** | You need deterministic unit tests for the deterministic 90% of the code, plus a separate non-deterministic eval harness. |
| **Cost engineering** | You now own a COGS line you can change by editing a string. |
| **Frontend fundamentals** | Streaming, incremental rendering, optimistic UI, and partial-failure UX are now core, not polish. |

What matters slightly *less*: hand-rolled low-level optimizations where a managed service is
cheaper and faster to run; exotic database features if you have no time to learn the operational
footprint; and building bespoke infrastructure when the managed path is boring and well-trodden.

**The key insight:** model calls are I/O to an unreliable, expensive, slow remote service. Every
skill you have for talking to an unreliable, expensive, slow remote service now applies directly.
Treat a model call like a call to a third-party API in a language you don't control, where the
response is nondeterministic and the errors are semantic rather than syntactic.

**When to use / when not to.** When designing any AI integration, write down the deterministic
portion (retrieval, filtering, authorization, persistence, validation) as ordinary tested code, and
isolate the nondeterministic portion behind one narrow seam. If you find yourself unable to write a
unit test for a piece of logic, that logic probably belongs on the deterministic side of the seam.

**Trade-offs.** Over-investing in prompt cleverness while under-investing in schema validation and
fallbacks is the most common failure. The prompt gets 100% of the attention in design review; the
validator gets none. Reverse that.

**Production considerations.**
- Every AI code path needs a stated, tested fallback that does not call a model.
- Keep the number of network hops per user action small. Each hop is a chance to be slow.
- Version the model, the prompt, and the schema together as one "recipe" identifier.

**Common mistakes.**
- Skipping the database and holding conversation state in memory or in the client.
- Treating the model call as synchronous inside a request handler that already does six other slow
  things.
- No schema validation because "the model is good enough."

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Which of your existing skills transferred directly to AI work?"
- "How do you test the parts of an AI feature that aren't deterministic?"

---

### 13.1.3 Where AI genuinely does not help

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** There is a large and important class of problem where a model is the wrong tool.
Recognizing it is a senior skill, and interviewers test for it explicitly.

**Mechanics.** Categories where a model adds nothing or subtracts value:

1. **Anything with a single correct answer derivable from the data you already have.** Totals,
   aggregations, joins, filtering, sorting, pagination, permission checks. Use SQL.
2. **Exact-match transformations of structured data.** Renaming fields, mapping one schema to
   another, reformatting dates, filling in a template. Use a mapping function.
3. **Logic with invariants you must be able to prove.** Pricing calculations, tax, scheduling with
   hard constraints, financial reconciliation. A model cannot give you an audit trail.
4. **Anything where latency budget is under ~100ms end to end.** A network round trip to a model is
   tens to hundreds of milliseconds before you've computed anything.
5. **Anything where a wrong answer is unacceptable and the input is verifiable.** Use a verifier.
   Model + verifier beats model alone; but if verification is trivial, just use the verifier.
6. **Secret handling, cryptographic operations, key derivation, protocol framing.** Never.
7. **Bulk data processing over millions of rows where a template or a program suffices.**
8. **Anything that must be byte-reproducible for compliance or for a customer contract.**
9. **Security-critical authorization decisions.** The authorization graph is not a language problem.
10. **Low-latency, high-volume classification where a trained discriminative model (or a regex) is
    both better and 1000x cheaper.** A 3B model on a good GPU is far cheaper per call than a large
    frontier model over the network.

**The cost-shape argument.** This is the argument that actually persuades product teams. A frontier
model call costs orders of magnitude more than an ordinary database query, both in dollars and in
wall-clock time. If a deterministic implementation is 200 lines, it will be *more* correct, *more*
debuggable, *more* testable, and *cheaper* than any prompt that tries to coax the same behaviour out
of a model. Use the model for the parts that are genuinely fuzzy: extracting structure from messy
human text, summarizing, paraphrasing, semantic matching, natural-language interface translation.

**When to use / when not to.** The rule of thumb: **if you can describe the correct behaviour as a
specification with test cases, write the specification.** If you can only describe it by example,
with judgement calls and edge cases you cannot enumerate, that is where the model belongs.

**Trade-offs.** Sometimes the deterministic version is genuinely hard: complex parsing of arbitrary
documents, or classification over a taxonomy that keeps growing. In that case the honest answer is
often a hybrid â€” deterministic preprocessing to narrow the input, model for the fuzzy judgment,
deterministic post-validation for the invariants you cannot compromise on.

**Production considerations.**
- Write the "AI or not" decision into the design doc, with the cost estimate and the fallback.
- If you choose no model, you still need the observability, the schema, and the tests â€” you just
  swap the inference engine for a function.

**Common mistakes.**
- Using an LLM to generate a SQL query where a parameterized query builder would be safer and
  faster. (If you must, do it with a schema-grounded read-only user, a read-only connection, a
  statement timeout, and a row limit. See 13.6.6.)
- Using an LLM for classification without measuring whether a 100-line logistic regression on
  embeddings does the same job at a hundredth the cost.
- Using an LLM to reformat data you already have in a structured form.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Tell me about a feature where you decided *not* to use AI. What tipped it?"
- "How do you decide between a model call and a lookup table?"

## 13.2 LLM API Integration

### 13.2.1 The shape of a provider API: messages, roles, stop reasons, usage

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Every mainstream chat-style model API is the same shape with different names. The
portable mental model is: an array of **messages**, each with a **role** and **content**, sent to a
**model**, returning **content blocks**, a **stop reason**, and **usage** metadata.

**Mechanics.** The four things you must understand:

1. **Roles.** The portable set is `system` (behavioral instructions, often repeated), `user`
   (human input), `assistant` (model output, and â€” crucially â€” prior turns are replayed as
   `assistant` messages), and `tool` (the result of executing a tool the assistant requested).
   Some APIs distinguish a "developer" role from "system"; some allow multiple consecutive tool
   result blocks for parallel calls.
2. **Content blocks.** Modern APIs return a *list of typed blocks* rather than a single string: text
   blocks, tool-use blocks, and in multimodal cases image/document blocks. Code defensively against
   a list even when you only expect text, because the moment you enable tools the shape changes.
3. **Stop reasons.** The most important field nobody reads. Distinguish:
   - `end_turn` / `stop` â€” the model finished naturally.
   - `max_tokens` / `length` â€” **truncated**. This is not an error, it is a *silent correctness
     failure*, and you must handle it explicitly.
   - `tool_use` / `tool_calls` â€” the model wants a tool executed.
   - `stop_sequence` â€” a custom stop string was hit.
   - provider error statuses.
   Truncation is the single most common cause of "the model ignores the second half of my
   instructions." If `stop_reason === length`, the right response is *not* to retry identically. It
   is to either raise the limit, shrink the prompt, or split the task.
4. **Usage.** Input tokens, output tokens, and increasingly **cached input tokens**. Cache write /
   cache read counts are what your prompt-caching bill is computed from. Log these on every call.

```ts
// The normalized response shape your whole app should program against.
export type Role = "system" | "user" | "assistant" | "tool";

export type ContentBlock =
  | { type: "text"; text: string }
  | { type: "tool_use"; id: string; name: string; input: unknown }
  | { type: "tool_result"; tool_use_id: string; content: string; is_error?: boolean };

export interface ChatMessage {
  role: Role;
  content: string | ContentBlock[];
  name?: string;      // for tool results
  tool_use_id?: string;
}

export type StopReason =
  | "end_turn"
  | "max_tokens"
  | "tool_use"
  | "stop_sequence"
  | "refusal"
  | "error";

export interface Usage {
  inputTokens: number;
  outputTokens: number;
  cachedInputTokens: number;
}

export interface ModelResponse {
  id: string;
  model: string;
  content: ContentBlock[];
  stopReason: StopReason;
  stopSequence: string | null;
  usage: Usage;
}
```

**The request envelope, portably:**

```ts
export interface ChatRequest {
  model: string;
  messages: ChatMessage[];
  maxOutputTokens?: number;
  temperature?: number;
  topP?: number;
  stopSequences?: string[];
  system?: string;              // hoisted system prompt, kept separate on purpose
  tools?: ToolSpec[];           // see 13.5
  responseSchema?: JSONSchema;  // see 13.2.4
  stream?: boolean;
  metadata?: Record<string, string>;
}
```

**Provider quirks you will hit.** Naming differs (`max_tokens` vs `max_completion_tokens`,
`input_tokens` vs `prompt_tokens`). Some providers reject `temperature` together with a fixed
reasoning mode. Some support a `system` field on the request and *also* accept a leading `system`
message â€” pick one, not both, or you will get duplicate instructions and doubled token cost. Some
cache only above a minimum prefix length. Some count tool schemas toward the cached prefix and some
don't. All of these are adapter concerns, which is why you write an adapter.

**When to use / when not to.** Use the normalized shape everywhere in your application. Never let a
vendor field name escape the adapter directory. Do not build a lowest-common-denominator abstraction
so lossy that you lose access to features you need (reasoning traces, caching controls, fine-grained
tool result streaming) â€” build a core interface plus escape hatches.

**Trade-offs.** A thin adapter over two vendors is a day of work and pays for itself the first time
you switch. A full abstraction over ten vendors with feature flags per capability is a month of work
and often never pays off. Two or three vendors is the sweet spot for most teams.

**Production considerations.**
- Never put vendor field names in database columns. Store the raw response *blob* for debugging
  plus your normalized fields for logic.
- Record `stop_reason` on every call. Alert on a spike in `max_tokens`.
- Budget output tokens explicitly per call site. An unbounded `maxOutputTokens` on a public
  endpoint is a denial-of-wallet.

**Common mistakes.**
- Ignoring `stop_reason === "max_tokens"` and shipping a truncated JSON blob to the parser.
- Mixing `system` role messages with the request-level `system` field.
- Not tracking `stop_sequence`, so a stray delimiter in the output silently ends generation.
- Assuming `usage` is free of charge for cached tokens.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "What is a stop reason and why do you care about each value?"
- "How would you abstract over two providers without losing features?"

---

### 13.2.2 OpenAI, Anthropic, and open-weight endpoints: the portable subset

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Three families of endpoints exist: (a) hosted proprietary models behind a vendor
HTTP API, (b) self-hosted or third-party-hosted **open-weight** models served through an
OpenAI-compatible or vendor-native surface, and (c) local runtimes on your own hardware. The
**portable subset** is the set of features that behave the same across all three.

**Mechanics.** The portable subset you can rely on:

- `messages: [{role, content}]` with roles `system` / `user` / `assistant` (and `tool` where
  supported).
- `model` as a string identifier (opaque to you â€” treat as a config value, never a constant).
- `max_output_tokens` (required by most; always set it).
- `temperature` and `top_p` (though some reasoning-tuned models restrict them).
- Streaming over SSE with a delta-shaped event stream.
- Token usage reported in the final event.
- Text in, text out.

The non-portable parts, which need capability flags:

| Feature | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **Prompt caching / prefix caching** | Exposed differently per vendor; some automatic, some explicit cache breakpoints, some minimum-length gated. |
| **Structured output / JSON schema mode** | Native in some, emulated by prompt+validator in others, unsupported in some open-weight runtimes. |
| **Tool calling** | Widely supported, but parallel-call support, strict schema enforcement, and tool-result streaming differ. |
| **Extended reasoning / thinking** | Vendor-specific control, token cost, and streaming event shape. |
| **Citations / grounding metadata** | Vendor-specific. |
| **Logprobs** | Available on some, not others; extremely useful for calibration work. |
| **Vision / document input** | Format details (base64 vs URL vs file id, page ranges) differ. |
| **Batch API** | Separate async endpoint at some vendors, absent at others. |
| **Fine-tuning** | Available at hosted vendors; locally you train yourself. |

Design implication: **capability detection, not just field mapping.**

```ts
export interface ProviderCapabilities {
  streaming: boolean;
  toolCalling: "none" | "basic" | "strict";
  parallelToolCalls: boolean;
  jsonSchemaMode: "native" | "prompted" | "none";
  promptCaching: "automatic" | "explicit" | "none";
  cachedTokenReporting: boolean;
  logprobs: boolean;
  reasoningMode: boolean;
  vision: boolean;
  documentInput: boolean;
  batch: boolean;
  seedForDeterminism: boolean;
}

export interface ProviderAdapter {
  readonly id: string;
  readonly capabilities: ProviderCapabilities;
  complete(req: ChatRequest, opts: RequestOptions): Promise<ModelResponse>;
  stream(req: ChatRequest, opts: RequestOptions): AsyncIterable<StreamEvent>;
}
```

**Open-weight specifics.** Open-weight models are typically served through a runtime that exposes an
OpenAI-compatible `/chat/completions` surface. Practical differences to plan for:

- **Tool calling quality varies by model.** Some open-weight models need very explicit schemas and
  few-shot examples; some emit malformed arguments. Always validate arguments before executing.
- **JSON schema enforcement is often absent.** You get "prompted JSON" â€” so you must parse
  defensively and repair.
- **Context windows and max output are model properties**, not API properties, and long-context
  behaviour is uneven. A model advertising 128k may degrade badly past 32k.
- **Tokenizer differences** mean token counts, and therefore costs and cache hits, are not
  comparable across models. Never compare "prompt length in tokens" across vendors as if the
  tokenizer were the same.
- **Quantization changes behaviour.** A quantized small model may be fast and cheap and noticeably
  worse on structured output.
- **Serving cost is real.** GPUs, batching, autoscaling, cold starts, and a whole new on-call
  surface. Do not self-host to save API fees unless your volume and privacy constraints justify it.

**When to use / when not to.** Use hosted proprietary endpoints as the default for product features:
they are the strongest and the fastest to ship. Consider open-weight when you have a hard data
residency requirement, an extremely high steady volume that makes self-hosting cheaper, a need for
weights you can fine-tune on your own hardware, or a latency requirement (same-region serving) you
cannot meet from a distant endpoint.

**Trade-offs.** Open-weight buys control and privacy and costs you quality, operational burden, and
engineering time. Hosted buys quality and speed and costs you data residency, per-token price at
scale, and a dependency you don't control. Most teams run both: a hosted frontier model for the
hard path, a self-hosted small model for the cheap high-volume path (see 13.7.4).

**Production considerations.**
- Keep model IDs in configuration, per environment, with a documented promotion process.
- Run your eval suite against every model you route to. A model swap is a regression event until
  proven otherwise by evals.
- Gate behind feature flags so a provider outage has a fallback model, not a dead product.
- Watch for silent API behaviour changes: response shape, default parameters, and content filtering
  all shift without notice.

**Common mistakes.**
- Hard-coding a model name in source instead of config.
- Assuming OpenAI-compatible means OpenAI-behaved.
- Self-hosting a model for a low-volume workload and discovering the GPU bill exceeds the API bill.
- No fallback model, so a provider incident is your incident.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Why would you self-host an open-weight model?"
- "What do you do when you want to switch providers?"
- "How do you know a cheaper model is good enough?"

---

### 13.2.3 Messages vs completions, and why the abstraction leaks

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Two API families coexist: the older **completions** API (a raw text continuation
endpoint, and its chat-shaped variants) and the **messages** API (a turn-structured, multimodal,
tool-aware endpoint). The chat API is the one to build on. The abstraction still leaks, and knowing
where it leaks is the skill.

**Mechanics.** Why the abstraction leaks:

1. **Prompt templates don't map to messages.** The classic single-template prompt with
   `### Instruction` / `### Input` / `### Output` markers maps onto a message list only awkwardly. You
   either preserve the template (better for open-weight models trained on that shape) or split it
   (better for caching and for role semantics). Decide per provider.
2. **Conversation history has two natural shapes** â€” a linear array of turns, or a
   *summarized-prefix-plus-recent-window* structure. Only the first maps to "just send the array."
3. **Multi-turn `assistant` replay is not free.** Every resend re-transmits prior assistant output.
   This is what makes prompt caching valuable (13.7.2) and what makes naive history unbounded.
4. **Prefill / continuation** â€” some completion endpoints let you prime the assistant's response.
   Message APIs generally do not. If your prompt relies on prefilling, you are not portable.
5. **Stop sequences and forced continuations** â€” classic completion technique, less relevant but not
   identical in message APIs.
6. **Logit bias and grammar constraints** â€” available in some forms, not others.
7. **Message-level content types** (images, documents) have no representation in a text template.
8. **The "turn" is a server concept, not a client one.** The server maintains no state; you
   resend everything. "Conversation state" is entirely your responsibility â€” see 13.2.8.

```ts
// Flat template form (open-weight friendly, cache-unfriendly, brittle)
const flat = [
  "You are a triage assistant for a payments platform.",
  "",
  "### Policy",
  POLICY_TEXT,
  "",
  "### Examples",
  fewShot.map(e => `### Input\n${e.input}\n### Output\n${e.output}`).join("\n\n"),
  "",
  "### Input",
  userText,
  "",
  "### Output",
].join("\n");

// Turn form (hosted-friendly, cache-friendly, role-correct)
const turns: ChatMessage[] = [
  { role: "system", content: SYSTEM },
  { role: "user", content: `Policy:\n${POLICY_TEXT}\n\nQuestion: ${userText}` },
];
```

**When to use / when not to.** Use messages everywhere unless you are serving an open-weight model
that was instruction-tuned on a flat template and measurably does worse on the message form. Then,
and only then, keep a flat-template path in the adapter. Do not build a template abstraction that
both paths share â€” they have genuinely different token economics and different failure modes.

**Trade-offs.** Template prompts are easy to diff and easy for humans to read, and they keep
few-shot examples adjacent to the instruction in a fixed order. Message prompts give you role
semantics the model was trained to respect, and let you keep a stable prefix for caching. Pick
based on measurement.

**Production considerations.**
- Keep the prompt-building function pure: `(input) => messages`. No I/O, no clock, no randomness.
  Then it is trivially unit-testable and cacheable.
- Store the rendered prompt (or a stable hash + the recipe id) on every request record.
- Be deliberate about where the volatile part sits â€” everything before it is cacheable.

**Common mistakes.**
- Appending the user question after a huge few-shot block, so the entire prompt misses cache.
- Interleaving system messages mid-conversation, which some providers reject and others handle
  inconsistently.
- Treating a message-list as the conversation record without validating it on load (a message with a
  missing parent tool_use_id will break replay).

**Interviewer probes.**
- "When would you use a flat completion prompt instead of messages?"
- "How does conversation state actually work â€” where does it live?"

---

### 13.2.4 Structured output: JSON schema, tool results, and validation loops

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Structured output is the discipline of making a model return data you can parse
without post-hoc heroics. The spectrum runs from "ask nicely in the prompt" to "the provider
constrains decoding to satisfy a JSON schema."

**Mechanics â€” four levels of enforcement, weakest to strongest:**

**Level 1 â€” Prompted JSON.** You describe the shape in the prompt and hope.
- Failure modes: prose preamble, markdown fences, trailing commentary, wrong key names, correct
  keys with wrong types, `null` where you need a value, nested arrays collapsed into strings.
- Mitigation: still validate; never trust.

**Level 2 â€” Native schema mode.** The provider accepts a JSON Schema and constrains generation so the
output satisfies it. This is the right default when available.
- Know the schema-feature subset: most implementations support object/array/string/number/boolean/
  null, `required`, `additionalProperties: false`, enums, and nesting. Support for `$ref`,
  `oneOf`, complex `pattern`, `minimum`, and `format` varies. Keep your schema inside the intersection
  and test it at the edge.
- Optional fields: schemas that force every optional key to be present change your code. Decide
  whether absent means "null" or "missing."

**Level 3 â€” Tool-use as a structured channel.** Define exactly one tool whose parameters *are* your
output schema. The model emits a tool call instead of text; the arguments are your object. This
works on providers without native schema mode and gives you a natural place to hang metadata.
- Trade-off: you're abusing the tool channel; some models get confused; you must handle the case
  where the model insists on narrating instead of calling.

**Level 4 â€” Constrained decoding / grammar.** The sampler is constrained so only token sequences
valid under the grammar can be produced. Strongest guarantee; availability is narrower.

**Level 5 â€” The validation-repair loop.** Whatever the level, you verify, and on failure you repair.
This is not optional in production; it is the part people forget.

```ts
import { z } from "zod";

export const TicketSchema = z.object({
  title: z.string().min(3).max(120),
  severity: z.enum(["sev1", "sev2", "sev3", "sev4"]),
  component: z.enum(["api", "web", "billing", "infra", "other"]),
  summary: z.string().min(10).max(600),
  duplicateOf: z.string().nullable(),
});
export type Ticket = z.infer<typeof TicketSchema>;

const JSON_SCHEMA_FOR_TICKET = {
  type: "object",
  additionalProperties: false,
  required: ["title", "severity", "component", "summary", "duplicateOf"],
  properties: {
    title: { type: "string" },
    severity: { type: "string", enum: ["sev1", "sev2", "sev3", "sev4"] },
    component: { type: "string", enum: ["api", "web", "billing", "infra", "other"] },
    summary: { type: "string" },
    duplicateOf: { anyOf: [{ type: "string" }, { type: "null" }] },
  },
} as const;
```

**The repair loop.** Critical design decisions: how many attempts, what changes each attempt, and
when to give up and degrade.

```ts
export interface ExtractOptions {
  schema: z.ZodTypeAny;
  jsonSchema?: Record<string, unknown>;
  maxRepairs?: number;      // default 1
  maxOutputTokens?: number;
  model?: string;
}

export class ValidationFailure extends Error {
  constructor(
    public readonly issues: z.ZodIssue[],
    public readonly raw: string,
    public readonly attempts: number,
  ) {
    super(`output failed validation after ${attempts} attempt(s): ${raw.slice(0, 400)}`);
    this.name = "ValidationFailure";
  }
}

export async function extractStructured<T>(
  buildRequest: (extraInstruction: string | null) => ChatRequest,
  opts: ExtractOptions,
): Promise<{ value: T; raw: string; usage: Usage }> {
  const maxRepairs = opts.maxRepairs ?? 1;
  let extraInstruction: string | null = null;
  let usage: Usage = { inputTokens: 0, outputTokens: 0, cachedInputTokens: 0 };
  let lastRaw = "";
  let lastIssues: z.ZodIssue[] = [];

  for (let attempt = 0; attempt <= maxRepairs; attempt++) {
    const req = buildRequest(extraInstruction);
    if (opts.jsonSchema) req.responseSchema = opts.jsonSchema;
    if (opts.maxOutputTokens) req.maxOutputTokens = opts.maxOutputTokens;

    const res = await client.complete(req, { timeoutMs: 30_000 });
    usage = addUsage(usage, res.usage);

    if (res.stopReason === "refusal") {
      throw new RefusalError(res.content);
    }
    if (res.stopReason === "max_tokens") {
      // Retrying identically cannot help. Surface it distinctly so callers can split or shrink.
      throw new TruncationError(res.usage.outputTokens);
    }

    lastRaw = textOf(res.content);
    const parsed = tryParseJson(lastRaw);
    const checked = parsed.ok ? opts.schema.safeParse(parsed.value) : null;

    if (checked?.success) {
      return { value: checked.data as T, raw: lastRaw, usage };
    }
    lastIssues = checked?.error.issues ?? [
      { code: "custom", path: [], message: parsed.ok ? "unparseable" : "invalid JSON" } as z.ZodIssue,
    ];
    extraInstruction = buildRepairInstruction(lastRaw, lastIssues);
  }

  throw new ValidationFailure(lastIssues, lastRaw, maxRepairs + 1);
}

function buildRepairInstruction(raw: string, issues: z.ZodIssue[]): string {
  const shape = issues
    .slice(0, 8)
    .map((i) => `- ${i.path.join(".") || "<root>"}: ${i.message}`)
    .join("\n");
  return [
    "Your previous response did not satisfy the required schema.",
    "Problems found:",
    shape,
    "Return ONLY the corrected JSON object. No prose, no code fences, no explanation.",
    "Do not change values that were already valid unless the problem list requires it.",
  ].join("\n");
}

// Tolerant parse: strips fences, finds the outermost balanced object, repairs common slips.
export function tryParseJson(raw: string): { ok: true; value: unknown } | { ok: false; error: string } {
  const cleaned = raw
    .replace(/^\s*```(?:json)?\s*/i, "")
    .replace(/\s*```\s*$/, "")
    .trim();
  try {
    return { ok: true, value: JSON.parse(cleaned) };
  } catch { /* fall through */ }

  const start = cleaned.indexOf("{");
  if (start === -1) return { ok: false, error: "no object found" };
  let depth = 0, inStr = false, esc = false;
  for (let i = start; i < cleaned.length; i++) {
    const ch = cleaned[i];
    if (esc) { esc = false; continue; }
    if (ch === "\\") { esc = true; continue; }
    if (ch === '"') { inStr = !inStr; continue; }
    if (inStr) continue;
    if (ch === "{") depth++;
    else if (ch === "}") {
      depth--;
      if (depth === 0) {
        const candidate = cleaned.slice(start, i + 1).replace(/,\s*([}\]])/g, "$1");
        try {
          return { ok: true, value: JSON.parse(candidate) };
        } catch (e) {
          return { ok: false, error: String(e) };
        }
      }
    }
  }
  return { ok: false, error: "unbalanced object" };
}
```

**The single-tool trick.** When native schema mode is unavailable but tool calling works:

```ts
export function schemaAsTool(name: string, schema: Record<string, unknown>): ToolSpec {
  return {
    name,
    description: "Return the structured result. Call this exactly once. Do not write prose.",
    input_schema: schema,
  };
}

// Force the channel: one tool, tool_choice required, and treat the tool call as the payload.
export async function extractViaTool<T>(
  name: string, jsonSchema: Record<string, unknown>, zodSchema: z.ZodTypeAny,
  req: ChatRequest,
): Promise<T> {
  const res = await client.complete({
    ...req,
    tools: [schemaAsTool(name, jsonSchema)],
    toolChoice: { type: "tool", name },
  });
  const call = res.content.find((b) => b.type === "tool_use");
  if (!call || call.name !== name) throw new ValidationFailure([], textOf(res.content), 1);
  const parsed = zodSchema.safeParse(call.input);
  if (!parsed.success) throw new ValidationFailure(parsed.error.issues, JSON.stringify(call.input), 1);
  return parsed.data as T;
}
```

**When to use / when not to.** Use native schema mode when available; use the single-tool trick when
not; use prompted JSON only for open models where nothing better exists, and only behind a repair
loop plus a metric on repair rate. Do not use structured output where the actual deliverable is
prose â€” a summary should be prose, and forcing it into JSON costs quality and tokens.

**Trade-offs.** Schema mode guarantees shape, not truth. A perfectly valid JSON object with an
invented `duplicateOf` id is still wrong. Structural validity buys you a parseable failure mode, not
correctness. That distinction is the whole point.

**Production considerations.**
- Emit a `schema_validation_failed` metric with the failing path, not the full content.
- Keep schemas versioned alongside prompts; a schema change is a prompt change.
- Cap `maxOutputTokens` from the *schema size*, not habit. A big schema with a long string field
  needs a big budget or you get truncation.
- If you accept user-supplied schema, validate the schema itself before sending it.

**Common mistakes.**
- No `additionalProperties: false`, so the model adds invented fields that your zod `.strict()`
  rejects downstream.
- Repair loop that re-sends the whole original prompt including the bad output, doubling cost each
  round. Send the original request plus a *short* instruction; include the raw output only if it is
  small.
- Retrying on truncation with identical parameters.
- Parsing with `JSON.parse` directly on model text.
- Trusting `null` for a field that has no meaningful null.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you guarantee the model returns valid JSON?"
- "What do you do when the output is structurally valid but semantically wrong?"
- "Tell me about a time a repair loop made things worse."

---

### 13.2.5 Streaming responses and Server-Sent Events in the UI

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Streaming delivers model output incrementally so the user sees tokens appear rather
than waiting. On the web the standard transport is **Server-Sent Events** (SSE): a long-lived
HTTP response with `text/event-stream`, one-directional, over plain HTTP, with automatic browser
reconnection support.

**Mechanics â€” the full path:**

1. Client POSTs to your server (you need POST for a request body; EventSource only does GET). Two
   common solutions: fetch + ReadableStream reader parsed by hand, or a POST-to-GET indirection where
   the client POSTs to create a stream id and then EventSources it. Prefer fetch + reader; it is
   simpler, supports headers, and avoids the indirection.
2. Server calls the provider's streaming endpoint and **re-emits** its events in your own protocol.
   Do not leak the provider's raw event format to the browser; you will regret it when you change
   providers.
3. Browser parses the stream, appends to state, and re-renders.

```ts
// ---------- server: SSE endpoint ----------
export const handler = async (req: Request): Promise<Response> => {
  const { messages, conversationId, recipeId } = await req.json();

  const encoder = new TextEncoder();
  const stream = new ReadableStream<Uint8Array>({
    async start(controller) {
      const send = (event: string, data: unknown) => {
        controller.enqueue(
          encoder.encode(`event: ${event}\ndata: ${JSON.stringify(data)}\n\n`),
        );
      };
      const abort = new AbortController();
      req.signal.addEventListener("abort", () => abort.abort());

      try {
        send("meta", { conversationId, messageId: crypto.randomUUID() });

        const result = await runTurn({ messages, conversationId, recipeId, signal: abort.signal });

        for await (const ev of result.stream) {
          switch (ev.type) {
            case "text_delta": send("delta", { text: ev.text }); break;
            case "tool_call": send("tool", { id: ev.id, name: ev.name }); break;
            case "tool_input_delta": send("tool_delta", { id: ev.id, json: ev.json }); break;
            case "usage": send("usage", ev.usage); break;
          }
        }
        send("done", { stopReason: result.stopReason, messageId: result.messageId });
      } catch (err) {
        send("error", { code: classify(err), message: publicMessage(err) });
      } finally {
        controller.close();
      }
    },
  });

  return new Response(stream, {
    headers: {
      "content-type": "text/event-stream; charset=utf-8",
      "cache-control": "no-cache, no-transform",
      connection: "keep-alive",
      "x-accel-buffering": "no",   // defeat proxy buffering (nginx)
    },
  });
};
```

```ts
// ---------- client: fetch + reader ----------
export async function* streamChat(body: unknown, signal: AbortSignal): AsyncGenerator<ServerEvent> {
  const res = await fetch("/api/chat", {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "content-type": "application/json", accept: "text/event-stream" },
    body: JSON.stringify(body),
    signal,
  });
  if (!res.ok || !res.body) throw new Error(`chat failed: ${res.status}`);
  if (!res.headers.get("content-type")?.includes("text/event-stream")) {
    throw new Error(`expected SSE, got ${res.headers.get("content-type")}`);
  }

  const reader = res.body.pipeThrough(new TextDecoderStream()).getReader();
  let buf = "";
  let event = "message";
  try {
    for (;;) {
      const { value, done } = await reader.read();
      if (done) break;
      buf += value;
      // SSE frames are separated by a blank line.
      let idx: number;
      while ((idx = buf.indexOf("\n\n")) !== -1) {
        const frame = buf.slice(0, idx);
        buf = buf.slice(idx + 2);
        let data = "";
        for (const line of frame.split("\n")) {
          if (line.startsWith("event:")) event = line.slice(6).trim();
          else if (line.startsWith("data:")) data += line.slice(5).trim();
        }
        if (data) yield { type: event, data: JSON.parse(data) } as ServerEvent;
        event = "message";
      }
    }
  } finally {
    reader.releaseLock();
  }
}
```

```ts
// ---------- client: state + render ----------
export function useChatStream(conversationId: string) {
  const [messages, setMessages] = useState<UiMessage[]>([]);
  const [status, setStatus] = useState<"idle" | "streaming" | "error" | "aborted">("idle");
  const abortRef = useRef<AbortController | null>(null);

  const send = useCallback(async (text: string) => {
    abortRef.current?.abort();
    const ac = new AbortController();
    abortRef.current = ac;

    const userMsg: UiMessage = { id: crypto.randomUUID(), role: "user", content: text };
    const aiMsg: UiMessage = { id: crypto.randomUUID(), role: "assistant", content: "", partial: true };
    setMessages((m) => [...m, userMsg, aiMsg]);
    setStatus("streaming");

    try {
      for await (const ev of streamChat(
        { messages: [{ role: "user", content: text }], conversationId }, ac.signal)) {
        if (ev.type === "delta") {
          setMessages((m) => m.map((x) => (x.id === aiMsg.id ? { ...x, content: x.content + ev.data.text } : x)));
        } else if (ev.type === "done") {
          setMessages((m) => m.map((x) => (x.id === aiMsg.id ? { ...x, partial: false } : x)));
          setStatus("idle");
        } else if (ev.type === "error") {
          setStatus("error");
          setMessages((m) => m.map((x) => (x.id === aiMsg.id
            ? { ...x, content: `${x.content}\n\n[unable to complete: ${ev.data.message}]`, partial: false, failed: true }
            : x)));
        }
      }
    } catch (e) {
      if ((e as Error).name === "AbortError") {
        setStatus("aborted");
        // Partial text stays on screen. Do not delete the user's work.
        setMessages((m) => m.map((x) => (x.id === aiMsg.id ? { ...x, partial: false, aborted: true } : x)));
      } else {
        setStatus("error");
      }
    }
  }, [conversationId]);

  const stop = useCallback(() => abortRef.current?.abort(), []);

  return { messages, status, send, stop };
}
```

**Reasoning/thinking traces.** If the model emits thinking blocks, decide explicitly: stream them to
the UI (transparent, but noisy and can leak reasoning that isn't user-facing), or keep them in
transcripts only. Either way they must not be replayed into future turns as assistant *text* unless
the API requires it.

**When to use / when not to.** Stream for anything a user reads as prose: chat, explanations,
long-form generation, code. Do not stream for structured extraction â€” partial JSON is not useful and
increases client complexity for nothing. Stream token-level text, but send *semantic* events for
everything else (tool started, section complete, citation added).

**Trade-offs.** Streaming cuts perceived latency dramatically â€” often 3â€“5x perceived speed â€” without
changing server-side latency at all. Costs: more complex clients, partial-render bugs, harder
testing, more edge cases (abort mid-stream, reconnect mid-stream, double-send), and load balancers
that buffer (which makes it silently non-streaming). Also: streaming prevents easy "retry the whole
thing" â€” you need per-message retry semantics instead.

**Production considerations.**
- Disable proxy/CDN buffering (`no-transform`, `X-Accel-Buffering: no`) or you will get all-at-once
  delivery in production and all-at-once-looking-streaming in dev.
- Heartbeat comments (`: ping\n\n`) every 15â€“30s to defeat idle timeouts.
- Persist the assistant message **once at the end** (or incrementally with a debounce) rather than
  on every delta. On abort, persist the partial text and mark it partial.
- Never expose provider error bodies verbatim to the client.
- Sanitize rendered markdown; model output is untrusted HTML source. Render with a strict allowlist,
  no raw HTML, and safe link protocols.

**Common mistakes.**
- Deleting partial output on abort. Users are furious. Keep it and mark it incomplete.
- Client-side `JSON.parse` on a half-arrived event because you didn't split on `\n\n`.
- Rendering `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` on model output.
- Buffering the whole response in your framework because the handler returns a string instead of a
  stream.
- No stop button, so the only cancel is closing the tab.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Why SSE and not WebSockets?"
- "What happens when the user hits stop halfway through?"
- "How do you test a streaming endpoint?"

---

---
### 13.2.6 Cancellation, timeouts, and partial results

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Cancellation is the ability to stop work in flight â€” both stopping the user's wait
and stopping the provider from continuing to bill you. Partial results are the honest artifact of a
cancelled or truncated generation: you got some output, and pretending otherwise is a lie.

**Mechanics.** Three layers must cooperate:

1. **Browser â†’ server.** `AbortController` on the `fetch`. The UI must keep the partial text and
   offer retry-from-here.
2. **Server â†’ provider.** Forward the signal. Both major hosted APIs accept an abort signal on the
   request. If your provider is behind a stream, aborting closes the socket, which stops generation.
   Everything you buffered is still yours.
3. **Server â†’ downstream side effects.** This is the part people forget. If the model was
   mid-way through an agentic loop that had already written to the database, aborting the stream does
   **not** roll those back. You need an explicit transaction boundary or a compensation step.

```ts
export class Deadline {
  readonly at: number;
  private timer: NodeJS.Timeout | null = null;
  private controller = new AbortController();

  constructor(private readonly budgetMs: number) {
    this.at = Date.now() + budgetMs;
  }
  get signal() { return this.controller.signal; }
  get remainingMs() { return Math.max(0, this.at - Date.now()); }
  get expired() { return this.remainingMs === 0; }

  arm() {
    this.timer = setTimeout(() => this.controller.abort(new DeadlineExceeded(this.budgetMs)), this.budgetMs);
    return this;
  }
  dispose() { if (this.timer) clearTimeout(this.timer); }
}

export interface ModelCallOptions {
  timeoutMs?: number;        // whole call
  connectTimeoutMs?: number; // time to first byte; a separate and useful budget
  signal?: AbortSignal;
  onFirstByte?: () => void;
}

export type CallFailure =
  | { kind: "timeout"; phase: "connect" | "first_byte" | "total"; ms: number }
  | { kind: "cancelled"; by: "user" | "deadline" }
  | { kind: "refused" }
  | { kind: "truncated"; outputTokens: number }
  | { kind: "provider_error"; status: number; retryable: boolean }
  | { kind: "validation"; issues: unknown }
  | { kind: "network" };

export class ModelCallError extends Error {
  constructor(public readonly failure: CallFailure) {
    super(`model call failed: ${failure.kind}`);
    this.name = "ModelCallError";
  }
}

export async function callWithDeadlines<T>(
  fn: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>,
  opts: ModelCallOptions = {},
): Promise<T> {
  const dl = new Deadline(opts.timeoutMs ?? 60_000);
  const connectTimer = setTimeout(() => dl.controller.abort(
    new ModelCallError({ kind: "timeout", phase: "first_byte", ms: opts.timeoutMs ?? 60_000 }),
  ), opts.connectTimeoutMs ?? 15_000);

  const onOuterAbort = () => dl.controller.abort(new ModelCallError({ kind: "cancelled", by: "user" }));
  opts.signal?.addEventListener("abort", onOuterAbort, { once: true });

  try {
    return await fn(dl.signal);
  } catch (e) {
    if (dl.controller.signal.aborted && dl.controller.signal.reason instanceof ModelCallError) {
      throw dl.controller.signal.reason;
    }
    throw new ModelCallError({ kind: "network" });
  } finally {
    clearTimeout(connectTimer);
    opts.signal?.removeEventListener("abort", onOuterAbort);
    dl.dispose();
  }
}
```

**Persisting partial results correctly.** The invariant: an assistant message on disk is either
`complete` or explicitly `partial`, and partial text is never re-fed to the model as if it were a
finished turn (unless you deliberately mark it as such â€” some designs do, for "continue" flows).

```ts
export async function persistAssistantMessage(
  conversationId: string,
  text: string,
  meta: { stopReason: StopReason; usage: Usage; recipeId: string; latencyMs: number },
): Promise<string> {
  const id = crypto.randomUUID();
  const complete = meta.stopReason === "end_turn" || meta.stopReason === "stop_sequence";
  await db.query(
    `INSERT INTO messages (id, conversation_id, role, content, status, stop_reason,
                           input_tokens, output_tokens, cached_tokens, recipe_id, latency_ms)
     VALUES ($1,$2,'assistant',$3,$4,$5,$6,$7,$8,$9,$10)`,
    [id, conversationId, text, complete ? "complete" : "partial",
     meta.stopReason, meta.usage.inputTokens, meta.usage.outputTokens,
     meta.usage.cachedInputTokens, meta.recipeId, meta.latencyMs],
  );
  return id;
}
```

**Retry semantics for cancelled work.** Distinguish:
- **User cancelled** â†’ do not auto-retry. Save partial, offer "continue" or "discard."
- **Deadline exceeded before any output** â†’ safe to retry with the same input; nothing was spent.
- **Deadline exceeded mid-stream** â†’ do not auto-retry transparently; the user has seen text.
  Offer resume.
- **Truncation (`max_tokens`)** â†’ retry only with a different strategy (raise limit, chunk the task,
  ask for a summary of partial work). Never identical.

**When to use / when not to.** Always set a deadline; there is no call site where an unbounded model
call is correct. Set a *separate, tighter* time-to-first-byte budget, because "no first byte in 15
seconds" is the user's actual patience limit while "still generating a 2000-word answer at 40
seconds" is fine if tokens are already flowing.

**Trade-offs.** Long budgets protect quality on hard tasks and destroy interactive UX. The right
move is usually two tiers: a fast path with a 3â€“5s budget that returns a partial or a fallback, and
an async path for the full answer with a generous budget and a push channel. Ship the fast path to
the browser and the slow path to a queue (13.7.5).

**Production considerations.**
- Charge tokens on cancelled calls. They were generated and billed.
- Track `abandon_rate` per feature; it is a quality signal users don't articulate.
- If you auto-continue truncated generations, cap total continuations or you'll loop forever.
- Cancellation should propagate into the retrieval layer too â€” no point fetching 20 chunks if the
  user already stopped.

**Common mistakes.**
- `try/finally` that closes the stream but never aborts the provider request, so you keep paying.
- Persisting a truncated response as `complete`, then replaying it and confusing the model.
- Treating abort as an error in the UI (red text, scary toast) instead of a normal outcome.
- Retrying a cancelled call automatically, producing duplicate side effects.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you make sure cancelling actually stops the cost?"
- "What do you store when a response is cut off halfway?"

---

### 13.2.7 Retries and rate limits for model calls

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Retrying a model call is *mostly* wrong by default. Naive exponential backoff on
every error causes duplicated work, duplicated charges, and duplicated side effects. Correct retry
is selective, idempotent, budgeted, and aware of what the call was *for*.

**Mechanics â€” classify before retrying:**

| Failure | Retry? | Strategy |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 429 rate limit | Yes | Honor `retry-after`; else exponential backoff with full jitter; respect token-bucket limits you observe |
| 5xx / transient network | Yes | Backoff with jitter, capped attempts (2â€“3), short connect timeout |
| Timeout, nothing produced | Yes | One retry, possibly on a faster model |
| Timeout mid-stream, partial text | Not transparently | Surface for user |
| 4xx malformed request | No | Bug. Fix the request. |
| Context length exceeded | No | Shrink context and retry once, deliberately |
| Refusal | No | Handle as a policy outcome, route or transform |
| Truncation | Not identically | Change the strategy |
| Validation failure | Yes, once | Repair loop (13.2.4) |
| Tool execution failure | Depends | Depends whether the tool is idempotent |

```ts
export interface RetryPolicy {
  maxAttempts: number;
  baseDelayMs: number;
  maxDelayMs: number;
  jitter: "full" | "equal" | "none";
  retryOn: (failure: CallFailure) => boolean;
  respectRetryAfter: boolean;
}

export const defaultPolicy: RetryPolicy = {
  maxAttempts: 3,
  baseDelayMs: 400,
  maxDelayMs: 8_000,
  jitter: "full",
  retryOn: (f) =>
    f.kind === "provider_error" ? f.retryable :
    f.kind === "network" :
    f.kind === "timeout" && f.phase === "connect",
  respectRetryAfter: true,
};

export const sleep = (ms: number, signal?: AbortSignal) =>
  new Promise<void>((resolve, reject) => {
    const t = setTimeout(resolve, ms);
    signal?.addEventListener("abort", () => { clearTimeout(t); reject(signal.reason); }, { once: true });
  });

export function delayFor(attempt: number, p: RetryPolicy, retryAfterMs?: number): number {
  if (retryAfterMs != null && p.respectRetryAfter) return Math.min(retryAfterMs, 60_000);
  const exp = Math.min(p.maxDelayMs, p.baseDelayMs * 2 ** attempt);
  if (p.jitter === "full") return Math.floor(Math.random() * exp);
  if (p.jitter === "equal") return Math.floor(exp / 2 + Math.random() * (exp / 2));
  return exp;
}

export class TokenBucket {
  private tokens: number;
  private last = Date.now();
  constructor(private capacity: number, private refillPerSec: number) {
    this.tokens = capacity;
  }
  async take(n = 1, signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<void> {
    for (;;) {
      const now = Date.now();
      this.tokens = Math.min(this.capacity, this.tokens + ((now - this.last) / 1000) * this.refillPerSec);
      this.last = now;
      if (this.tokens >= n) { this.tokens -= n; return; }
      const waitMs = ((n - this.tokens) / this.refillPerSec) * 1000;
      await sleep(Math.max(10, waitMs), signal);
    }
  }
}
```

**Concurrency control.** A semaphore is usually more effective than retry for rate limits: it stops
you from sending the 200th request instead of failing and re-sending it.

```ts
export class Semaphore {
  private active = 0;
  private queue: (() => void)[] = [];
  constructor(private readonly limit: number) {}
  async acquire(signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<() => void> {
    if (signal?.aborted) throw signal.reason;
    if (this.active < this.limit) {
      this.active++;
      return () => this.release();
    }
    await new Promise<void>((resolve, reject) => {
      const entry = () => { this.active++; resolve(); };
      this.queue.push(entry);
      signal?.addEventListener("abort", () => {
        const i = this.queue.indexOf(entry);
        if (i >= 0) { this.queue.splice(i, 1); reject(signal.reason); }
      }, { once: true });
    });
    return () => this.release();
  }
  private release() {
    this.active--;
    this.queue.shift()?.();
  }
}
```

**Global budgets, not just per-request.** Three layers, each with its own counter: a global
concurrency semaphore, a per-tenant token bucket, and a hard daily spend cap with a kill switch.

```ts
export class BudgetGuard {
  constructor(
    private readonly redis: RedisLike,
    private readonly dailyCapTokens: number,
    private readonly tenantCaps: Map<string, number> = new Map(),
  ) {}

  async check(tenant: string, estimatedTokens: number): Promise<void> {
    const day = new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10);
    const gKey = `budget:global:${day}`;
    const tKey = `budget:tenant:${tenant}:${day}`;
    const cap = this.tenantCaps.get(tenant) ?? Infinity;

    // One atomic transaction: incrby both, compare both against caps.
    const ok = await this.redis.multi()
      .incrby(gKey, estimatedTokens)
      .incrby(tKey, estimatedTokens)
      .exec();
    const globalUsed = Number(ok[0]);
    const tenantUsed = Number(ok[1]);
    if (globalUsed > this.dailyCapTokens || tenantUsed > cap) {
      await this.redis.multi().decrby(gKey, estimatedTokens).decrby(tKey, estimatedTokens).exec();
      throw new BudgetExceededError({ globalUsed, tenantUsed, cap });
    }
  }
}
```

**When to use / when not to.** Retry connection-level failures and 429/5xx, always with jitter,
always with a cap. Do not retry validation failures more than once, do not retry refusals, do not
retry non-idempotent tool calls unless the tool has an idempotency key.

**Trade-offs.** Retries increase tail latency under load, which is exactly when you are already
struggling. Prefer admission control (concurrency limits) + fast fallback over aggressive retries.
Every retry costs money. Every retry can duplicate a side effect. Both are real.

**Production considerations.**
- Cap retries per *user action*, not per layer, or your effective multiplier becomes 3 Ã— 3 = 9.
- Track retry rate per model and per error class; a rising 429 rate is a capacity problem, not a
  retry-policy problem.
- Circuit breaker: after N consecutive failures of a provider, fail fast to the fallback model.
- Respect `retry-after`; providers are telling you the truth.

**Common mistakes.**
- Retrying on 4xx (except 429) and burning quota on requests that will never work.
- No jitter, so every client retries in lockstep and you create a thundering herd.
- Retrying the *whole* turn including side-effecting tools.
- Measuring latency including retries but billing only the successful attempt.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Which errors do you retry and which do you not?"
- "How do you handle a rate limit without making latency worse?"

---

### 13.2.8 Conversation state: who owns it, and where it is stored

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Providers are stateless: every request contains the full history. "Conversation
memory" is therefore something *you* build. The design question is who owns the canonical state and
how it maps onto the provider's stateless replay.

**Mechanics.** The correct model is a **canonical store** (your relational DB) plus a **derived
view** (the message list you send). From the canonical store you *project* a sendable history using:
windowing, summarization, salience selection, or some combination.

```ts
export interface ConversationStore {
  append(msg: NewStoredMessage): Promise<StoredMessage>;
  history(conversationId: string): Promise<StoredMessage[]>;
  summary(conversationId: string): Promise<SummaryRecord | null>;
  putSummary(conversationId: string, s: SummaryRecord): Promise<void>;
  branch(conversationId: string, fromMessageId: string): Promise<string>;
}

export interface StoredMessage {
  id: string;
  conversationId: string;
  parentId: string | null;
  role: Role;
  content: ContentBlock[];
  status: "complete" | "partial" | "failed";
  createdAt: string;
  recipeId?: string;
  usage?: Usage;
  stopReason?: StopReason;
}
```

**History projection.** The strategy matters more than the storage.

```ts
export interface HistoryBudget {
  maxMessages: number;
  maxChars: number;
  maxToolResults: number;
  keepSystemIntact: boolean;
}

export interface ProjectedHistory {
  messages: ChatMessage[];
  notes: { summarized: number; truncatedToolResults: number; droppedMessages: number };
}

export function projectHistory(
  msgs: StoredMessage[],
  summary: SummaryRecord | null,
  budget: HistoryBudget,
): ProjectedHistory {
  const out: ChatMessage[] = [];
  const notes = { summarized: 0, truncatedToolResults: 0, droppedMessages: 0 };
  if (summary) out.push({ role: "system", content: summary.text });

  const system = msgs.filter((m) => m.role === "system");
  const rest = msgs.filter((m) => m.role !== "system");
  if (budget.keepSystemIntact) out.push(...system.map(toWire));

  // Tool results are big and lossy to keep; cap and truncate the oldest first.
  const recent = rest.slice(-budget.maxMessages);
  let chars = recent.reduce((n, m) => n + measure(m), 0);
  let toolResultsKept = 0;
  const kept: ChatMessage[] = [];

  for (let i = recent.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    const m = recent[i];
    const size = measure(m);
    const isToolResult = m.role === "tool";
    const overChars = chars + size > budget.maxChars;
    const overTools = isToolResult && toolResultsKept >= budget.maxToolResults;
    if (overChars || overTools) {
      notes.droppedMessages++;
      if (isToolResult) {
        kept.unshift(truncateToolResult(m));
        notes.truncatedToolResults++;
        chars += 80;
      }
      continue;
    }
    kept.unshift(toWire(m));
    chars += size;
    if (isToolResult) toolResultsKept++;
  }
  out.push(...kept);
  return { messages: out, notes };
}

const truncateToolResult = (m: StoredMessage): ChatMessage => ({
  role: "tool",
  tool_use_id: m.tool_use_id!,
  content: "[large tool result omitted to fit context; re-query the tool if this detail is required]",
});
```

**Summarization as a compaction.** Trigger on token count, summarize the oldest span, replace it
with one synthetic `system`-role summary message, and keep the invariant that summaries are
**cumulative** â€” each new summary must absorb the previous one.

```ts
export async function compactIfNeeded(
  store: ConversationStore,
  conversationId: string,
  budget: HistoryBudget,
): Promise<boolean> {
  const msgs = await store.history(conversationId);
  const total = msgs.reduce((n, m) => n + measure(m), 0);
  if (total <= budget.maxChars * 0.7) return false;

  const keepTail = Math.floor(msgs.length * 0.35);
  const head = msgs.slice(0, msgs.length - keepTail);
  if (head.length < 6) return false;

  const prior = await store.summary(conversationId);
  const res = await client.complete({
    model: CHEAP_MODEL,
    maxOutputTokens: 600,
    system: SUMMARIZER_SYSTEM,
    messages: [
      ...(prior ? [{ role: "system" as const, content: `Existing summary:\n${prior.text}` }] : []),
      ...head.map(toWire),
    ],
  });

  await store.putSummary(conversationId, {
    text: textOf(res.content),
    coversMessageIds: head.map((m) => m.id),
    createdAt: new Date().toISOString(),
    recipeId: "conversation.summary.v3",
  });
  return true;
}
```

**Why the client must not own the state.** If the browser holds the only copy of the history:
- The user loses the conversation on refresh or device change.
- You cannot compact or summarize, because summarization is a server-side model call.
- You cannot enforce retention/deletion policy.
- Two tabs produce divergent histories.
- A prompt-injected client can send arbitrary history.

Send only the *latest user input* (plus conversation id) from the client; the server loads history.

**Branching and editing.** Give conversations a tree, not a list, from day one if the product has
"edit and resend," "regenerate," or "branch" affordances. `parentId` on messages makes this trivial
later and painful to retrofit. Regeneration is: append a new assistant message with the same parent,
mark the old one `superseded`, don't delete it (it's often in the user's saved history).

**When to use / when not to.** Always keep the canonical history server-side. Window-only (no
summarization) is fine until you see context-limit errors or cost complaints. Summarize when
conversations routinely exceed the window. Branch when users can edit; it's cheaper to support from
the start.

**Trade-offs.** Full history replay is the highest-fidelity and highest-cost option. Summarization
loses detail and can accumulate drift ("the summarizer misremembered a preference and every future
turn is wrong"). Mitigate by keeping the summary factual and entity-focused, and by making the
summary regenerable from the original messages when the original is still retained.

**Production considerations.**
- Retention policy: conversations can be the most sensitive data you hold. Encrypt, scope access,
  support deletion.
- Per-conversation token accounting so you can find the expensive ones.
- Store the exact `messages` array sent on each request (or a hash + recipe id) so bad turns are
  reproducible.
- Prompt caching works because the prefix is stable; keep system + tools at the top and put history
  after, in append order.

**Common mistakes.**
- Client-owned history.
- Summarizing non-cumulatively, so context slowly loses earlier facts.
- Keeping full tool-result blobs in history forever, blowing the window and the budget.
- No `parentId`, making regenerate/branch a rewrite later.
- Truncating a `tool` message without keeping the `tool_use_id` pairing, which breaks replay.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Where does conversation history live and who owns it?"
- "How do you handle a conversation longer than the context window?"
- "How would you implement 'edit and resend'?"

---

### 13.2.9 Multimodal input: images, audio, and documents

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Multimodal input is user-supplied images, audio, video, or documents fed to the model
as typed content blocks rather than as extracted text. The engineering is mostly in the *front door*
(upload, validation, conversion) and in the *back door* (how the extracted content is stored and
retrieved).

**Mechanics â€” images.** Two transport modes: base64 inline in the request (simple, but inflates your
request size by ~33% and counts tokens against a per-image budget) or a provider file/URL reference
(cheaper on the wire, requires lifecycle management and an accessible URL). Provider limits on
dimensions, file size, and count are real; validate before sending.

```ts
export interface ImageRef {
  kind: "inline_base64" | "file_id" | "url";
  mediaType: "image/png" | "image/jpeg" | "image/webp" | "image/gif";
  data?: string;       // base64, no data: prefix
  fileId?: string;
  url?: string;
}

export async function normalizeImage(input: Blob | Buffer, fileId?: string): Promise<ImageRef> {
  const mediaType = detectMediaType(input) as ImageRef["mediaType"];
  if (input.byteLength > 5 * 1024 * 1024) throw new ImageTooLarge();
  const { width, height } = await probeDimensions(input);
  if (width < 32 || height < 32) throw new ImageTooSmall();
  if (Math.max(width, height) > 4096) {
    return { kind: fileId ? "file_id" : "inline_base64", mediaType, fileId, data: downscaleBase64(input, 1568) };
  }
  if (fileId) return { kind: "file_id", mediaType, fileId };
  return { kind: "inline_base64", mediaType, data: toBase64(input) };
}

// Long edge ~1568px and high-quality JPEG is a widely used sweet spot:
// it preserves legibility of small UI text while cutting tokens several-fold.
export function downscaleBase64(buf: Buffer, longEdge: number): string {
  const out = resizeToLongEdge(buf, longEdge);
  return toBase64(out);
}
```

**The document two-pipeline pattern.** Documents have two distinct uses, and conflating them causes
you to do the expensive thing twice or the wrong thing once:

1. **Direct multimodal comprehension** â€” send pages as images (or the native document block) to
   answer questions about layout, tables, charts, and visual structure.
2. **Extraction into your RAG corpus** â€” parse to text, chunk, embed, index so the document becomes
   retrievable by *other* users' queries.

```ts
export interface ParsedDocument {
  docId: string;
  text: string;
  pages: { pageNumber: number; text: string }[];
  tables: { pageNumber: number; markdown: string; csv: string }[];
  figures: { pageNumber: number; caption: string; description: string }[];
  headings: { level: number; text: string; pageNumber: number }[];
}

export async function ingestDocument(file: Buffer, meta: DocMeta): Promise<IndexedDoc> {
  const parsed = await parsePdf(file);            // layout-aware, not naive text extraction
  const docId = `${meta.tenantId}:${meta.sha256}`;

  const chunks = chunkByStructure(parsed);          // 13.4.3
  const embedded = await embedInBatches(chunks.map((c) => c.text));
  await indexChunks(docId, chunks, embedded);
  await upsertDocumentMeta({ docId, ...meta, pageCount: parsed.pages.length });

  return { docId, chunkCount: chunks.length, hasTables: parsed.tables.length > 0 };
}
```

**Audio.** Two distinct uses: (a) *transcription* â€” a dedicated speech-to-text endpoint or model,
which is a normal API call with normal retry/caching semantics; (b) *native audio understanding* â€”
feeding audio directly to a multimodal model. For almost all products, transcription is the right
choice: it's cheaper, it's searchable, and you can run your RAG pipeline over the transcript. Native
audio understanding is for tasks where prosody/tone/timbre carry the signal (call triage sentiment,
voice QA) and it is worth the premium.

```ts
export interface TranscriptSegment { startMs: number; endMs: number; text: string; speaker?: string; }

// Store segments, not one blob: you need timestamps for citation ("at 12:04 they said X").
export async function transcribeAndStore(audioUrl: string, meetingId: string) {
  const res = await stt.transcribe({ audioUrl, diarize: true, languageHint: "en" });
  const segments = res.segments.map((s) => ({
    startMs: s.startMs, endMs: s.text.length ? s.endMs : s.startMs, text: s.text.trim(), speaker: s.speaker,
  }));
  await db.query(`INSERT INTO transcript_segments (meeting_id, start_ms, end_ms, speaker, text)
                  SELECT $1, x.start, x."end", x.speaker, x.text
                  FROM jsonb_to_recordset($2::jsonb) AS x(start bigint, "end" bigint, speaker text, text text)`,
                 [meetingId, JSON.stringify(segments)]);
  return segments;
}
```

**When to use / when not to.** Use multimodal input when the *visual or audio structure itself*
carries meaning: screenshots of errors, scanned forms, charts, voice tone. Use text extraction +
RAG when the content is prose you need to search, quote, or cite precisely. Never feed a 200-page
PDF into a context window and hope.

**Trade-offs.** Images cost tokens roughly proportional to area, so a 4K screenshot can cost as much
as several thousand words. PDFs sent as images cost you searchability and increase latency.
Extraction pipelines add complexity (parsers fail, layout is messy, tables are nightmares). A hybrid â€”
extract for search, send page images for the specific pages retrieval selected â€” is the mature answer.

**Production considerations.**
- Validate and re-encode uploads server-side; never trust client MIME types. Strip EXIF from photos.
- Virus-scan documents. They're untrusted input that a parser will open.
- Cap per-request image count and total bytes; these are the cheapest denial-of-wallet vectors.
- Store original file, derived images, and extracted text with separate retention policies.
- Citation granularity: for documents, cite page + bounding region; for audio, cite timestamps.

**Common mistakes.**
- Sending screenshots at full resolution and blowing the token budget.
- Naive PDF text extraction that interleaves columns, destroying table structure.
- Storing a single transcript blob with no timestamps, making citation impossible.
- Trusting the client's declared content type for file uploads.
- Deleting the original document because you extracted text â€” you now cannot answer visual questions.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you handle a user uploading a 100-page PDF?"
- "How do you cite a source in a model's answer?"
- "When would you send an image directly instead of extracting text?"

---

---
## 13.4 RAG Architecture

### 13.4.1 The naive RAG pipeline and why it underperforms

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Naive RAG: embed the corpus, embed the query, take the top *k* by cosine similarity,
paste into a prompt, ask a question. It works on demos and disappoints in production because
retrieval quality â€” not generation quality â€” dominates answer quality.

**Mechanics.** The naive pipeline:

```
ingest:   docs -> chunk (fixed 512 tokens) -> embed -> insert into vector index
query:    question -> embed -> ANN search top 8 -> concat -> "answer from context" -> answer
```

Why it underperforms:

1. **Chunking destroys meaning.** Fixed-size splits cut mid-sentence, separate a heading from its
   content, and orphan a table from its caption. The embedded vector then represents a fragment.
2. **One query, one search.** Real questions are multi-part ("compare plan A and plan B pricing over
   3 years") or use vocabulary absent from the corpus ("how do I stop being charged twice" vs. the
   doc's "duplicate invoice generation").
3. **Similarity is not relevance.** Cosine on a generic chunk ("Overview of billing") matches almost
   any billing question. The most similar chunks are often the least informative.
4. **No ordering strategy.** Chunks pasted in arbitrary similarity order cause "lost in the middle":
   models attend most reliably to the beginning and end of long context.
5. **No reranking.** The top 8 by embedding similarity typically contains 2â€“4 truly relevant chunks
   and 4â€“6 near-misses that crowd out good context and inject noise.
6. **Top-k is arbitrary.** Too few misses the answer; too many adds noise and burns tokens.
7. **No evaluation.** Without a labelled queryâ†’relevant-chunk set, every tuning decision is guesswork.
8. **Freshness gaps.** The index drifts from the source of truth and nobody notices until a user reads
   a deleted policy.
9. **No citations.** The user cannot verify, and you cannot detect hallucination.
10. **Retrieval silently fails.** When nothing relevant is retrieved, the model still answers fluently
    from priors. This is the most damaging failure because it is invisible.

**The mental model that fixes most of it.** Treat it as a **cascade with error budgets**:

```
query understanding -> candidate retrieval -> rerank -> assembly -> generation
     ~95%              recall@50 ~90-97%      top 3-6    ordered     grounded
```

Each stage has a measurable metric, and you improve them in order. Improving the prompt while
retrieval recall@20 is 40% is wasted effort.

**When to use / when not to.** Use RAG when the answer must reflect data that changes, data you may
not train on, data you can cite, or a corpus larger than the context window. Do **not** use RAG when
the answer is stable, short, and well-known to the model; when the corpus is tiny (put it in the
prompt or use keyword search); or when a structured query answers it exactly (aggregations, filters,
"my last 5 invoices").

**Trade-offs.** RAG adds a whole subsystem: ingestion, indexing, evaluation, freshness, and an
observability surface. For many products, structured search over a relational or search index beats
RAG decisively. Decide per feature, not per company.

**Production considerations.**
- Instrument retrieval separately from generation: log retrieved chunk IDs, their scores, and the
  final answer. Without this you cannot debug.
- Have an explicit "no relevant context" signal that makes the model say so.
- Index freshness as an SLO, not an afterthought.

**Common mistakes.**
- Tuning the prompt while retrieval is broken.
- No labelled query set, so chunk size and top-k are folklore.
- Assuming the vector index is current.
- No fallback when retrieval returns nothing relevant.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Why does naive RAG underperform?"
- "How do you debug a wrong RAG answer?"

---

### 13.4.2 Embeddings: what they are, how to choose, and how to evaluate

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** An embedding model maps text to a fixed-dimensional vector such that semantically
similar texts are close and dissimilar texts are far under a learned metric. Retrieval quality is
bounded by embedding quality; no amount of prompt work fixes a bad embedding model for your domain.

**Mechanics.**
- **Models.** Small general-purpose (hundreds of millions of parameters, 384â€“768 dims, very cheap) vs.
  domain-specific or multilingual vs. asymmetric (separate query/document encoders). Diminishing
  returns beyond ~1024 dims for most retrieval tasks; higher dims cost index size and search time.
- **Similarity metrics.** Cosine for normalized embeddings (the usual choice); dot product for
  unnormalized vectors (requires comparable magnitude, useful with trained dimension-truncation
  schemes); Euclidean on normalized vectors is monotonically related to cosine and equivalent for
  ranking purposes.
- **Asymmetric models** encode a *query* and a *document* differently. Using the wrong one on either
  side is a silent, large quality loss. Check the model documentation; do not guess.
- **Preprocessing.** Lowercase? Strip punctuation? These matter more than you would expect for models
  trained with a specific normalisation. Prefixing each chunk with its breadcrumb ("Document title â†’
  Section â†’ text") reliably improves retrieval, because every chunk then carries its own context.
- **Query/document prefix instructions.** Some embedding models expect an instruction prefix on the
  *query* side only. Using it wrongly degrades results.

```ts
export interface EmbeddingModel {
  id: string;
  dims: number;
  maxInputTokens: number;
  isAsymmetric: boolean;
  queryPrefix?: string;
