```ts
  embed(inputs: string[], kind: "query" | "document"): Promise<Float32Array[]>;
}

export class CachedEmbedder implements EmbeddingModel {
  constructor(private readonly inner: EmbeddingModel, private readonly cache: KV) {}

  async embed(inputs: string[], kind: "query" | "document"): Promise<Float32Array[]> {
    const prefixed =
      kind === "document" || !this.inner.queryPrefix
        ? inputs
        : inputs.map((t) => this.inner.queryPrefix + t);
    const keys = prefixed.map((t) => `emb:${this.inner.id}:${fnv1a(t)}`);
    const cached = await this.cache.mget(keys);
    const missing: number[] = [];
    for (let i = 0; i < keys.length; i++) if (!cached[i]) missing.push(i);
    if (missing.length > 0) {
      const fresh = await this.inner.embed(missing.map((i) => prefixed[i]), kind);
      await this.cache.mset(missing.map((i, j) => [keys[i], encodeF32(fresh[j])] as [string, string]));
      for (let j = 0; j < missing.length; j++) cached[missing[j]] = encodeF32(fresh[j]);
    }
    return cached.map(decodeF32);
  }
}

export function cosine(a: Float32Array, b: Float32Array): number {
  let dot = 0, na = 0, nb = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
    dot += a[i] * b[i];
    na += a[i] * a[i];
    nb += b[i] * b[i];
  }
  return dot / (Math.sqrt(na) * Math.sqrt(nb) || 1);
}
```

**Choosing a model â€” empirically, on your own data.** This evaluation is worth more than any heuristic:

```ts
export interface EmbeddingBenchmark {
  name: string;
  queries: { text: string; relevantChunkIds: string[] }[];
}

export async function scoreEmbeddingModel(
  model: EmbeddingModel,
  bench: EmbeddingBenchmark,
  index: ChunkIndex,
): Promise<{ mrr: number; recallAt5: number; recallAt20: number }> {
  let rrSum = 0, r5 = 0, r20 = 0;
  for (const q of bench.queries) {
    const [qv] = await model.embed([q.text], "query");
    const hits = await index.search(qv, 20);
    const ranks = q.relevantChunkIds
      .map((id) => hits.findIndex((h) => h.id === id))
      .filter((r) => r >= 0);
    if (ranks.length > 0) {
      rrSum += 1 / (Math.min(...ranks) + 1);
      if (ranks.some((r) => r < 5)) r5++;
      r20++;
    }
  }
  const n = bench.queries.length;
  return { mrr: rrSum / n, recallAt5: r5 / n, recallAt20: r20 / n };
}
```

**Practical rules.**
- Build a labelled set of 100â€“300 real queries with the chunks a human says answer them. Ten hours of
  labelling that pays back forever.
- Evaluate 2â€“4 candidate models on it. Choose on MRR/recall, then on cost and latency.
- Changing the embedding model **invalidates the entire index**. Budget a full reindex and plan a
  dual-index migration (write both, compare recall, cut over).
- Check the model's input limit. A chunk exceeding it is silently truncated.

**When to use / when not to.** Use embeddings for semantic matching where keywords fail: synonyms,
paraphrase, cross-lingual, "described but not named" queries. Do not use them where exact lexical
match matters â€” part numbers, error codes, legal citations, names. That is what BM25 and hybrid search
are for (13.4.5), and skipping them is the most common RAG mistake.

**Trade-offs.** Embedding models have real per-token cost and add index-rebuild coupling. Changing one
is a project, not a config change. Dimensionality trades accuracy against storage and query latency.

**Production considerations.**
- Batch embed calls; per-chunk calls cost 10â€“50x more in wall-clock time and often in dollars.
- Cache document embeddings by content hash so re-ingesting unchanged documents is nearly free.
- Version the embedding model in index metadata and on every chunk row, so mixed-index bugs are visible.
- Queue and rate-limit embedding work at ingest; it is the most token-heavy part of building RAG.

**Common mistakes.**
- Choosing a model by popularity rather than by benchmark on your data.
- Applying a query prefix to documents, or omitting a required one.
- Re-embedding the whole corpus every time the chunker changes, with no way to identify stale chunks.
- Ignoring that oversized chunks are silently truncated by the embedding model.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you choose an embedding model?"
- "What happens to your index when you change the embedding model?"

---

---
### 13.4.3 Chunking strategies: fixed, semantic, hierarchical, and by-structure

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Chunking decides what the retrieval unit is. It is the highest-leverage,
lowest-attention part of RAG, because retrieval can only return chunks that exist.

**Mechanics â€” the strategies.**

**Fixed-size with overlap.** Simple, predictable, universally applicable. Specify chunk size in
*tokens*, not characters (a character count silently mis-sizes with code and non-Latin scripts).
Overlap of 10â€“20% prevents facts from being cut in half.

```ts
export function chunkByTokens(
  text: string,
  { size = 400, overlap = 60, tokenizer }: { size?: number; overlap?: number; tokenizer: Tokenizer },
): string[] {
  const ids = tokenizer.encode(text);
  const chunks: string[] = [];
  const stride = size - overlap;
  for (let start = 0; start < ids.length; start += stride) {
    const slice = ids.slice(start, start + size);
    if (slice.length < 20) break; // skip trailing slivers
    chunks.push(tokenizer.decode(slice));
    if (start + size >= ids.length) break;
  }
  return chunks;
}
```

**Semantic chunking.** Split where embedding distance between adjacent sentences spikes â€” a topic
boundary. More faithful, but adds a full embedding pass over the corpus at ingest and produces
variable-length chunks that complicate budgeting.

```ts
export async function semanticChunks(
  sentences: { text: string; start: number }[],
  embed: EmbeddingModel,
  { percentile = 85, minSentences = 3 }: { percentile?: number; minSentences?: number } = {},
): Promise<string[]> {
  if (sentences.length <= minSentences) return [sentences.map((s) => s.text).join(" ")];
  const vecs = await embed.embed(sentences.map((s) => s.text), "document");
  const sims: number[] = [];
  for (let i = 1; i < vecs.length; i++) sims.push(cosine(vecs[i - 1], vecs[i]));
  const threshold = percentileOf(sims, percentile);

  const chunks: string[] = [];
  let cur: string[] = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < sentences.length; i++) {
    cur.push(sentences[i].text);
    if (i < sentences.length - 1 && sims[i] <= threshold && cur.length >= minSentences) {
      chunks.push(cur.join(" "));
      cur = [];
    }
  }
  if (cur.length > 0) chunks.push(cur.join(" "));
  return chunks;
}
```

**Hierarchical (parent-child, "small-to-big").** Index *small* chunks for precise retrieval, but
return the *parent section* as context. This is the best general answer for structured documents, and
it decouples retrieval precision from context completeness.

```ts
export interface HierarchyNode {
  id: string;
  parentId: string | null;
  level: "section" | "paragraph" | "sentence";
  text: string;
  embeddable: boolean; // only leaves are embedded
  ordinal: number;
}

export async function buildHierarchy(doc: ParsedDocument): Promise<HierarchyNode[]> {
  const nodes: HierarchyNode[] = [];
  for (const heading of doc.headings) {
    const sectionId = `${doc.docId}:${heading.level}:${heading.text}`;
    nodes.push({
      id: sectionId, parentId: null, level: "section",
      text: `${doc.title} > ${heading.text}`, embeddable: false, ordinal: 0,
    });
    for (const p of sectionParagraphs(doc, heading)) {
      nodes.push({
        id: `${sectionId}:p${nodes.length}`, parentId: sectionId, level: "paragraph",
        text: p, embeddable: true, ordinal: nodes.length,
      });
    }
  }
  return nodes;
}

export async function retrieveWithParentExpansion(
  queryVector: Float32Array, index: ChunkIndex, k: number, fetch: (ids: string[]) => Promise<HierarchyNode[]>,
): Promise<{ leafIds: string[]; contextNodes: HierarchyNode[] }> {
  const hits = await index.search(queryVector, k);
  const parents = await fetch([...new Set(hits.map((h) => parentIdOf(h.id)))]);
  return { leafIds: hits.map((h) => h.id), contextNodes: dedupeAndOrder(hits, parents) };
}
```

**By-structure.** The best option when the source has structure: Markdown headings, HTML sections,
JSON objects, API endpoint definitions, code files (split by function or class), database rows (one
chunk per row with column names as context), table rows (header row prepended to each chunk â€”
otherwise the row is meaningless), and Q&A pairs.

```ts
// Row-per-chunk with a rendered header line. The header is what makes the row interpretable.
export function chunkTable(table: ParsedTable): string[] {
  const header = table.columns.join(" | ");
  return table.rows.map(
    (r, i) =>
      `<table name="${escapeAttr(table.name)}" caption="${escapeAttr(table.caption ?? "")}">\n` +
      `${header}\n${r.cells.join(" | ")}\n<!-- row ${i + 1} of ${table.rows.length} -->\n</table>`,
  );
}

// Code chunking: never split a function body.
export function chunkCode(src: SourceFile, lang: string): { text: string; symbol?: string; file: string }[] {
  const symbols = parseSymbols(src.text, lang); // tree-sitter or a language server
  return symbols.map((s) => ({
    symbol: s.name,
    file: src.path,
    text: `// file: ${src.path}\n// symbol: ${s.kind} ${s.name}\n${s.text}`,
  }));
}
```

**Chunks that carry context.** Prefix each chunk with breadcrumb metadata. This measurably improves
retrieval because the embedding no longer represents an orphan.

```ts
export const toChunkText = (n: HierarchyNode, docTitle: string): string =>
  `${docTitle} â€º ${breadcrumb(n)}\n\n${n.text}`;
```

**When to use / when not to.**
- **Fixed with overlap:** default; unstructured sources; fastest to build.
- **By-structure:** whenever structure exists (docs, wikis, code, tickets, tabular data). Always
  better when available.
- **Hierarchical:** long-form documents and reports, anywhere a small precise match must be returned
  inside a complete section.
- **Semantic:** long narrative prose with no headings (transcripts, books), when you can afford the
  ingest embedding pass.

**Trade-offs.** Chunk size is a genuine trade-off curve: small chunks raise precision and lose context;
large chunks do the reverse. There is a broad optimum around 200â€“500 tokens for QA-style retrieval
with parent expansion, and it must be measured on your data. Variable-size chunks complicate context
budgeting â€” select top-k by score, compute the token total, then drop the lowest-scoring chunk if you
exceed budget.

**Production considerations.**
- Store `chunkerVersion` on every chunk; changing the chunker invalidates every embedding.
- Version-control chunker configuration alongside prompts. It is code.
- Re-chunking a 100k-document corpus is an expensive background job with a dual-index migration.
- Evaluate chunking by measuring recall@k against labelled queries, directly, not by intuition.

**Common mistakes.**
- Chunking by characters.
- Splitting a table without its header row.
- No overlap, so facts straddle chunk boundaries.
- Changing chunk size without re-running retrieval evals.
- Embedding chunks larger than the embedding model's input limit.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you choose chunk size?"
- "What is parent-child retrieval and why does it help?"

---

### 13.4.4 Indexing: vector stores, pgvector, HNSW, and the recall/latency trade-off

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** The vector index is the data structure that finds the *k* nearest neighbours among
millions of vectors fast enough to sit in a request path. Every choice is a position on the
**recall vs. latency vs. memory vs. operational complexity** curve.

**Mechanics.**

**Exact search (flat).** Brute-force cosine over all vectors. Perfect recall. Latency grows linearly
with corpus size. This is the *baseline you must always measure against*, and it is genuinely fine up
to roughly 10^5â€“10^6 vectors depending on dimensions and hardware. Many teams deploy a vector database
when flat search on their actual corpus size would be faster and simpler.

**HNSW (hierarchical navigable small world).** A layered proximity graph. Search starts on a sparse
top layer, greedily descends to denser layers, then beam-searches near the query. Parameters:

- `m` (edges per node, 8â€“64): higher = better recall, more memory.
- `ef_construction` (100â€“500+): higher = better graph quality, slower indexing.
- `ef_search` (query-time beam width, 40â€“200): **the runtime recall/latency dial.** Set it per query.

```
Recall@10:  0.92  (ef_search=40)    4ms
            0.96  (ef_search=80)    7ms
            0.98  (ef_search=200)  15ms
```

Set `ef_search` from a recall target measured on your labelled set, and raise it for hard queries.

**IVF (inverted file).** Partition space into Voronoi cells via k-means; search probes only the
nearest `nprobe` cells. Faster to build, larger memory, and recall depends on `nprobe`. Useful for very
large corpora with fast-changing data, but suffers from data drift unless periodically rebuilt.

**Product filters versus ANN.** This is the operational crux. Filtering by tenant, date, or document
type alongside ANN search is hard: pre-filtering then searching a small set can be slow when the
filter is highly selective; post-filtering can return fewer than *k* results because the nearest
neighbours were all filtered out. Mature engines solve this with filtered ANN (partitioned
namespaces, per-tenant indexes, iterative scan). With pgvector you express the filter in the WHERE
clause and accept that the index scan may be less selective.

**pgvector.** Postgres-native. The right default when your corpus lives in the same database as your
business data, when you need transactional consistency between document metadata and chunks, when you
need real relational filtering and joins, and when the corpus is moderate (up to a few million
vectors with tuning).

```sql
CREATE TABLE chunks (
  id            uuid PRIMARY KEY,
  doc_id        uuid NOT NULL,
  tenant_id     text NOT NULL,
  ordinal       int  NOT NULL,
  chunker_ver   text NOT NULL,
  embed_model   text NOT NULL,
  content       text NOT NULL,
  heading_path  text[] NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',
  token_count   int  NOT NULL,
  embedding     vector(768) NOT NULL,
  indexed_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  deleted_at    timestamptz
);

-- HNSW with cosine distance. The operator class must match how you compare.
CREATE INDEX chunks_embedding_hnsw ON chunks
  USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
  WITH (m = 16, ef_construction = 128);

-- Filtered search. ORDER BY distance is required for the index to be used.
-- ef_search is set per session: that is the recall/latency dial.
SELECT c.id, c.doc_id, c.content, 1 - (c.embedding <=> $1::vector) AS score
  FROM chunks c
  JOIN documents d ON d.id = c.doc_id
 WHERE c.tenant_id = $2
   AND c.deleted_at IS NULL
   AND c.indexed_at <= $3              -- as-of snapshot, for freshness correctness
   AND ($4::text[] IS NULL OR c.heading_path && $4::text[])
 ORDER BY c.embedding <=> $1::vector
 LIMIT $5;
```

```ts
// Two-phase filtering for selective filters: over-fetch, filter, then trim.
export async function filteredSearch(
  q: Float32Array, tenantId: string, opts: { tags?: string[]; k: number },
): Promise<SearchHit[]> {
  const over = opts.tags && opts.tags.length > 0 ? opts.k * 8 : opts.k;
  const rows = await sql`
    SELECT id, doc_id, content, heading_path, 1 - (embedding <=> ${toPgVector(q)}::vector) AS score
      FROM chunks
     WHERE tenant_id = ${tenantId} AND deleted_at IS NULL
     ${opts.tags && opts.tags.length > 0 ? sql`AND heading_path && ${opts.tags}` : sql``}
     ORDER BY embedding <=> ${toPgVector(q)}::vector
     LIMIT ${over}`;
  const filtered =
    opts.tags && opts.tags.length > 0
      ? rows.filter((r) => r.heading_path.some((h) => opts.tags!.includes(h)))
      : rows;
  return filtered.slice(0, opts.k);
}
```

**Dedicated vector stores.** A separately deployed ANN engine with its own scaling story. Choose when
the corpus exceeds what Postgres indexes comfortably, when you need multi-tenant sharding at scale,
when you need region-local indexes, or when you need filtered-ANN tuning knobs. Accept: a second data
store, consistency lag between Postgres metadata and the vector index, no cross-store transactions, and
another on-call surface.

**When to use / when not to.**
- **Flat/exact:** up to ~200k vectors, or whenever you cannot tolerate recall loss.
- **pgvector HNSW:** the default for most product teams with moderate corpora.
- **Dedicated ANN service:** very large corpora, high QPS, or multi-region/tenant sharding needs.
- Consider **no vector index at all** if the corpus is small enough to scan or if lexical search does
  the job â€” see 13.4.5.

**Trade-offs.** HNSW gives excellent recall/latency at the cost of memory (vectors plus graph links)
and non-trivial build times; high-rate inserts eventually degrade the graph and require maintenance.
IVF indexes faster, but recall is more parameter-sensitive and suffers from data drift.

**Production considerations.**
- HNSW builds are CPU- and memory-heavy. Build large indexes in a background job with generous
  resources, not during a migration window on the primary.
- Plan reindexing: changing `m`, dimensions, or the embedding model requires a full rebuild. Use
  dual-write, compare recall, then cut over.
- Monitor index build duration, `ef_search` distribution, recall against a shadow labelled set, and
  filter-selectivity rate (queries where filtering removed most candidates â€” a sign your index design
  does not fit your query shape).
- Set `ef_search` per query class: broad or summary queries higher, precise lookups lower.
- Soft-delete plus periodic vacuum, or your index carries tombstones and degrades.

**Common mistakes.**
- Comparing ANN recall against nothing â€” never against exact search.
- Using IVF on a corpus that is still growing (k-means centroids become stale).
- Creating the HNSW index without an operator class matching the distance function.
- Assuming `LIMIT k` with a filter returns k rows.
- Rebuilding the index synchronously during a deploy.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Explain HNSW parameters and how you'd tune them."
- "pgvector or a dedicated vector database â€” how do you decide?"
- "How do you handle filters alongside ANN search?"

---

---
### 13.4.5 Retrieval: hybrid search, BM25 plus vectors, and reranking

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** **Hybrid search** combines lexical (BM25) and dense (vector) retrieval, usually with
reciprocal rank fusion. **Reranking** runs a cheap-to-query, expensive-to-compute relevance model over
the candidate set to reorder it precisely.

**Mechanics â€” why lexical search is not obsolete.** Embeddings are weak at exactly what users query
for literally:

- Error codes (`ERR_PAYMENT_4021`), part numbers, SKU codes, function names, API endpoints.
- Proper nouns and rare terms the model never saw in pretraining (your product's feature names).
- Exact phrases ("duplicate invoice", "not received").
- Legal citations, version strings, dates.

BM25 handles all of these natively. Conversely, BM25 fails on paraphrase and cross-lingual queries.
Hybrid gets both.

**BM25 essentials.** Score = Î£ over query terms of IDF(q) Â· (f(q,d)Â·(k1+1)) /
(f(q,d) + k1Â·(1 âˆ’ b + bÂ·|d|/avgdl)). `k1` (term-frequency saturation, ~1.2â€“2.0) and `b` (length
normalization, ~0.75) are the two knobs. Practical needs beyond the formula: per-field weighting (a
term in a title should count more than in the body), synonym expansion, and corpus-tuned stopwords.

```sql
-- Postgres full-text baseline (rank_cd is not true BM25 but is a solid lexical signal).
-- For true BM25 use a dedicated search engine or the BM25 extension.
WITH q AS (SELECT websearch_to_tsquery('english', $2) AS tsq)
SELECT c.id,
       c.doc_id,
       ts_rank_cd(to_tsvector('english', c.heading_path_text || ' ' || c.content), q.tsq) AS score
  FROM chunks c, q
 WHERE c.tenant_id = $1
   AND c.deleted_at IS NULL
   AND to_tsvector('english', c.heading_path_text || ' ' || c.content) @@ q.tsq
 ORDER BY score DESC
 LIMIT $3;
```

**Reciprocal Rank Fusion.** Rank-based, scale-free, no tuning of relative score magnitudes â€” which is
why it is the default:

```
RRF(d) = Î£_over_lists  weight_i / (k + rank_i(d)),   k â‰ˆ 60 typical
```

```ts
export interface RankedHit {
  id: string;
  score: number;
  source: string;
  meta: Record<string, unknown>;
}

export function reciprocalRankFusion(
  lists: { name: string; hits: RankedHit[]; weight: number }[],
  k = 60,
): RankedHit[] {
  const acc = new Map<string, { hit: RankedHit; rrf: number; sources: Set<string> }>();
  for (const list of lists) {
    // Dedupe within a list first: a document appearing twice is not twice as relevant.
    const seen = new Set<string>();
    let rank = 0;
    for (const h of list.hits) {
      if (seen.has(h.id)) continue;
      seen.add(h.id);
      rank++;
      const cur = acc.get(h.id) ?? { hit: h, rrf: 0, sources: new Set<string>() };
      cur.rrf += list.weight / (k + rank);
      cur.sources.add(list.name);
      acc.set(h.id, cur);
    }
  }
  return [...acc.values()]
    .sort((a, b) => b.rrf - a.rrf)
    .map((x) => ({ ...x.hit, score: x.rrf, source: [...x.sources].join("+") }));
}
```

**Weights.** Start dense 1.0 / lexical 1.0 and tune on the labelled set. Rarely need more than three
lists (dense, lexical, and optionally a metadata/filtered prior). If queries skew toward error codes,
raise lexical weight; if they skew toward conceptual questions, raise dense weight.

**Reranking.** A cross-encoder reads (query, passage) *together* and outputs a relevance score. Far
more accurate than bi-encoder embeddings because attention runs across both, and far more expensive â€”
so it only runs on the top 50â€“200 candidates.

```
embed-then-rerank:  embed all chunks at ingest (cheap)  ->  ANN top 200  ->  rerank  ->  top 5
```

```ts
export interface Reranker {
  id: string;
  maxDocs: number;
  maxTokensPerPair: number;
  rerank(query: string, docs: { id: string; text: string }[]): Promise<{ id: string; score: number }[]>;
}

export interface RetrievalDiagnostics {
  reranked: boolean;
  rerankerId?: string;
  lexicalTopIds: string[];
  denseTopIds: string[];
  finalIds: string[];
  scores: Record<string, number>;
  rankDelta: Record<string, number>;
}

export async function retrieveWithRerank(
  query: string,
  tenantId: string,
  opts: { lexicalK: number; denseK: number; finalK: number; rerank?: Reranker },
): Promise<{ hits: RankedHit[]; diagnostics: RetrievalDiagnostics }> {
  const dense = await vectorSearch(toVector(query), tenantId, opts.denseK);
  const lexical = await lexicalSearch(query, tenantId, opts.lexicalK);
  const fused = reciprocalRankFusion([
    { name: "dense", hits: dense, weight: 1 },
    { name: "lexical", hits: lexical, weight: 1 },
  ]);

  if (!opts.rerank) {
    const hits = fused.slice(0, opts.finalK);
    return {
      hits,
      diagnostics: {
        reranked: false,
        lexicalTopIds: lexical.slice(0, 10).map((h) => h.id),
        denseTopIds: dense.slice(0, 10).map((h) => h.id),
        finalIds: hits.map((h) => h.id),
        scores: Object.fromEntries(hits.map((h) => [h.id, h.score])),
        rankDelta: {},
      },
    };
  }

  const cands = fused.slice(0, opts.rerank.maxDocs);
  const reranked = await opts.rerank.rerank(
    query,
    cands.map((c) => ({ id: c.id, text: truncateToTokens(c.content, opts.rerank!.maxTokensPerPair) })),
  );
  const byId = new Map(cands.map((c) => [c.id, c]));
  const final = reranked
    .sort((a, b) => b.score - a.score)
    .slice(0, opts.finalK)
    .map((r) => ({ ...byId.get(r.id)!, score: r.score, source: "rerank" }));

  const fusedRank = new Map(fused.map((h, i) => [h.id, i]));
  return {
    hits: final,
    diagnostics: {
      reranked: true,
      rerankerId: opts.rerank.id,
      lexicalTopIds: lexical.slice(0, 10).map((h) => h.id),
      denseTopIds: dense.slice(0, 10).map((h) => h.id),
      finalIds: final.map((h) => h.id),
      scores: Object.fromEntries(final.map((h) => [h.id, h.score])),
      rankDelta: Object.fromEntries(
        final.map((h) => [h.id, fusedRank.get(h.id)! - final.indexOf(h)]),
      ),
    },
  };
}
```

**When to use / when not to.** Use hybrid whenever your corpus contains identifiers, codes, or proper
nouns, or your users' vocabulary differs from your writers'. Use reranking when you have more than a
few candidates and precision matters â€” it is the single highest-return quality improvement in most RAG
systems. Skip reranking when the corpus is tiny (< 50 chunks: just put all of them in context) or when
your latency budget cannot absorb the extra hop.

**Trade-offs.** Reranking adds a full extra model call per query, typically 30â€“200ms and a real per-
query cost. It also introduces another model to version and can be domain-sensitive: an off-the-shelf
reranker may underperform on specialised vocabulary, and fine-tuning or training a domain reranker is
more work than most teams take on. Hybrid fusion adds two index queries instead of one.

**Production considerations.**
- Always log which chunks came from which list. When a user reports a bad answer, the
  lexical-vs-dense comparison tells you immediately whether the problem is vocabulary mismatch or
  ranking.
- Cache the rerank result keyed on (query hash, candidate id set hash) â€” reranked queries repeat far
  more than you expect in a support or docs product.
- Set `finalK` from a token budget, not a magic number: assemble by score until you hit budget.
- Rerankers can be swapped per query class; a code-search corpus may need a different reranker than
  a policy corpus.

**Common mistakes.**
- Vectors only, no lexical leg, so error-code queries fail.
- Reranking a candidate set of 5, which cannot reorder anything meaningfully.
- Fusing scores directly (`Î±Â·dense + Î²Â·lexical`) instead of ranks, then tuning Î± and Î² endlessly.
- Not capping `maxTokensPerPair`, so one long chunk blows the reranker's context.
- Using `topK` before reranking rather than after, defeating the purpose.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Why do you need lexical search if you have embeddings?"
- "What is reranking and where does it sit in the pipeline?"
- "How do you tune the fusion weights?"

---

### 13.4.6 Query transformation: rewrite, decomposition, HyDE

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Query transformation improves retrieval by changing the query *before* searching:
**rewrite** (fix vocabulary and phrasing), **decomposition** (split a multi-part question into
independent sub-queries), and **HyDE** (hypothetical document embedding â€” embed a plausible fake
answer, then retrieve with that).

**Mechanics.**

**Rewrite.** The user's question may be conversational, contain pronouns, be in another language, or
use vocabulary the corpus lacks. A cheap model rewrites it into a standalone keyword-rich query.

```ts
export async function rewriteQuery(original: string, history: ChatMessage[]): Promise<string> {
  const res = await client.complete({
    model: FAST_MODEL,
    maxOutputTokens: 80,
    temperature: 0,
    system:
      "Rewrite the user's question as a standalone search query. Resolve pronouns using the " +
      "conversation. Keep proper nouns, error codes, and identifiers verbatim. " +
      "Output the query only. No quotes, no explanation.",
    messages: [...history.slice(-6), { role: "user", content: original }],
  });
  if (res.stopReason !== "end_turn") return original; // rewrite failure must not break retrieval
  const out = textOf(res.content).trim();
  return out.length > 0 ? stripQuotes(out) : original;
}
```

Guardrails: never let the rewrite drop identifiers (regex-extract codes and re-append them if
missing); measure rewrite quality separately; and always keep the original as a fallback leg in a
fusion so a bad rewrite cannot destroy recall.

**Decomposition.** Multi-hop and comparative questions ("difference between plan A and B", "how do I
get X and also fix Y") retrieve poorly in one shot. Decompose, retrieve per sub-query, fuse, then let
the generator synthesize across all evidence.

```ts
export interface SubQuery { text: string; kind: "primary" | "sub"; }

export async function decompose(question: string): Promise<SubQuery[]> {
  const res = await client.complete({
    model: FAST_MODEL,
    maxOutputTokens: 200,
    temperature: 0,
    responseSchema: {
      type: "object",
      additionalProperties: false,
      required: ["needsDecomposition", "subQueries"],
      properties: {
        needsDecomposition: { type: "boolean" },
        subQueries: { type: "array", maxItems: 4, items: { type: "string", minLength: 3 } },
      },
    },
    messages: [
      {
        role: "system",
        content:
          "Decide whether the question requires separate searches. Decompose only if it " +
          "contains multiple independent facts, a comparison, or a sequential task. " +
          "Each sub-query must be independently searchable. Maximum 4.",
      },
      { role: "user", content: question },
    ],
  });
  const parsed = InvoiceDisputeLikeSchema.safeParse(tryParseJson(textOf(res.content)));
  if (!parsed.success || !parsed.data.needsDecomposition) return [{ text: question, kind: "primary" }];
  return [
    { text: question, kind: "primary" },
    ...parsed.data.subQueries.map((t) => ({ text: t, kind: "sub" as const })),
  ];
}

export async function retrieveMultiHop(question: string, tenantId: string): Promise<RankedHit[]> {
  const subs = await decompose(question);
  const lists: { name: string; hits: RankedHit[]; weight: number }[] = [];
  for (const [i, sq] of subs.entries()) {
    const hits = await retrieveWithRerank(sq.text, tenantId, {
      lexicalK: 40, denseK: 40, finalK: 15,
    });
    // Primary query gets more weight; sub-queries still contribute.
    lists.push({ name: `q${i}`, hits: hits.hits, weight: sq.kind === "primary" ? 1.5 : 1 });
  }
  const fused = reciprocalRankFusion(lists, 60);
  return rerankOnce(question, fused, tenantId, 6);
}
```

**HyDE (hypothetical document embeddings).** Retrieval queries and answers live in different regions
of embedding space. HyDE asks a cheap model to *write a plausible answer*, embeds that answer, and
retrieves with it. Because a hypothetical answer resembles real documents more closely than a
question does, recall improves â€” sometimes substantially on short, vague queries.

```ts
export async function hydeRetrieve(question: string, tenantId: string, k: number): Promise<RankedHit[]> {
  const hypo = await client.complete({
    model: FAST_MODEL,
    maxOutputTokens: 180,
    temperature: 0.3,
    system:
      "Write a short passage that would plausibly appear in a product knowledge base and that " +
      "would answer the question. Do not hedge, do not mention that it is hypothetical. " +
      "One paragraph.",
    messages: [{ role: "user", content: question }],
  });
  // Fuse the hypothetical answer WITH the original query: pure HyDE loses exact-term matching.
  const [v1, v2] = await Promise.all([
    embedder.embed([question], "query"),
    embedder.embed([textOf(hypo.content)], "query"),
  ]);
  return reciprocalRankFusion([
    { name: "q", hits: await vectorSearch(v1[0], tenantId, k), weight: 1.0 },
    { name: "hyde", hits: await vectorSearch(v2[0], tenantId, k), weight: 0.8 },
  ]).slice(0, k);
}
```

**Other transformations worth knowing.** Multi-query fan-out (generate 3â€“5 paraphrases, retrieve for
each, fuse â€” robust but costs *k* searches); step-back prompting (ask for a general question to retrieve
general context alongside specifics); and routing (classify the question type and send it to a
different retrieval strategy: structured query, code search, semantic search).

**When to use / when not to.** Rewrite: almost always, when the query comes from a conversational UI
and conversation state exists. Decompose: only when questions genuinely have multiple parts â€”
detecting decomposition wrongly splits simple questions and hurts precision. HyDE: when queries are
short and vague, or in domains where question/answer embedding mismatch is measurable. Skip all three
for a search box over a small, well-titled corpus.

**Trade-offs.** Every transformation adds a model call (100â€“400ms, real cost) and can only lose
information. The safest architecture is *additive*: transform, then fuse the transformed query's
results with the original query's results, so a bad transformation degrades gracefully rather than
catastrophically.

**Production considerations.**
- Measure each transformation independently on your labelled query set: does it improve recall@20
  over no transformation? Keep only what measurably wins.
- Cache rewrites keyed on (question hash, last few message ids) â€” repeated questions in support
  products are common.
- Log the original query, the transformed query, and the retrieved sets side by side. This is the
  single most useful debugging artifact in a RAG system.
- Set `maxOutputTokens` low (60â€“200) on transformations; long rewrites are usually a symptom of a bad
  prompt.

**Common mistakes.**
- Replacing the original query instead of fusing with it.
- Running decomposition on every question "just in case."
- Not preserving error codes and identifiers through a rewrite.
- Assuming HyDE always helps; on corpora with lots of exact identifiers it hurts.
- Recursive transformations (rewrite the rewrite) with no benefit.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "Your RAG gives bad answers for follow-up questions. Why?"
- "What is HyDE and when would you not use it?"
- "How would you debug a retrieval failure?"

---

### 13.4.7 Context assembly: how many chunks, in what order, with what citations

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Context assembly is turning a ranked hit list into the exact text block you place in
the prompt. It decides how much context, in what order, deduplicated how, with what citations â€” and
every choice interacts with "lost in the middle."

**Mechanics â€” the decisions, and what to do.**

**How many chunks.** Fill a token budget, not a chunk count. Reserve budget for the system prompt,
tools, the question, and the output. Then add chunks by score until the remaining budget is consumed.

```ts
export interface AssemblyBudget {
  totalContextTokens: number;
  reservedForOutput: number;
  systemTokens: number;
  toolTokens: number;
  questionTokens: number;
  safetyMargin: number;
}

export function remainingBudget(b: AssemblyBudget): number {
  return b.totalContextTokens - b.reservedForOutput - b.systemTokens
       - b.toolTokens - b.questionTokens - b.safetyMargin;
}

export function assembleByBudget(
  ranked: RankedHit[],
  budgetTokens: number,
  tokenizer: Tokenizer,
  { maxChunks = 12, perChunkCap = 800 }: { maxChunks?: number; perChunkCap?: number } = {},
): { included: RankedHit[]; excluded: RankedHit[]; usedTokens: number } {
  const included: RankedHit[] = [];
  const excluded: RankedHit[] = [];
  const seenDocs = new Set<string>();
  let used = 0;

  for (const hit of ranked) {
    if (included.length >= maxChunks) { excluded.push(hit); continue; }
    // Diversity cap: at most 2 chunks from one document, so one long doc cannot fill the window.
    if (seenDocs.has(hit.meta.docId as string) && countFor(hit.meta.docId as string, included) >= 2) {
      excluded.push(hit);
      continue;
    }
    const text = truncateToTokens(hit.meta.text as string, perChunkCap);
    const t = tokenizer.encode(text).length + 12; // 12 â‰ˆ per-chunk delimiters
    if (used + t > budgetTokens) { excluded.push(hit); continue; }
    included.push({ ...hit, meta: { ...hit.meta, text } });
    seenDocs.add(hit.meta.docId as string);
    used += t;
  }
  return { included, excluded, usedTokens: used };
}
```

**Order.** Empirically, models attend most reliably to the start and end of long context. Two good
strategies:

1. **Highest score last** (closest to the question). Good for question-answering.
2. **Highest score first** (most prominent). Good for long generations where the beginning sets the
   frame.

For Q&A, put the *best* chunk closest to the question and diversify the rest. Also group chunks by
document so related context is contiguous, and always put the question *after* the context so the
model's final attention lands on the task.

**Citations.** Emit machine-readable citations so the UI can render them and so you can verify them.

```ts
export function numberSources(hits: RankedHit[]): { context: string; idToLabel: Map<string, number> } {
  const idToLabel = new Map<string, number>();
  const blocks = hits.map((h, i) => {
    const label = i + 1;
    idToLabel.set(h.id as string, label);
    return [
      `<source id="${label}" doc="${escapeAttr(String(h.meta.title))}" url="${escapeAttr(String(h.meta.url))}"` +
      `${h.meta.pageNumber ? ` page="${h.meta.pageNumber}"` : ""} updated="${escapeAttr(String(h.meta.updatedAt))}">`,
      h.meta.text,
      `</source>`,
    ].join("\n");
  });
  return { context: blocks.join("\n\n"), idToLabel };
}

export const CITATION_INSTRUCTION = [
  "Cite every factual claim using the source numbers in square brackets, e.g. [2].",
  "Place the citation immediately after the sentence it supports.",
  "If no source supports a claim, omit the claim entirely. Never cite a source that does not contain it.",
  "If the sources do not answer the question, say so plainly and do not speculate.",
].join("\n");
```

**Deduplication and near-duplicate suppression.** Chunks from adjacent sections frequently overlap.
Use an n-gram or MinHash Jaccard check against already-included chunks and drop near-duplicates, plus
embedding similarity above a threshold (e.g. 0.97).

```ts
export function isNearDuplicate(candidate: string, included: string[], jaccardThreshold = 0.6): boolean {
  const c = shingles(candidate, 8);
  return included.some((inc) => jaccard(c, shingles(inc, 8)) >= jaccardThreshold);
}
```

**Handling insufficient context.** If the fused and reranked results fall below a relevance threshold,
do *not* pad the prompt with weak chunks and hope. Pass an explicit "no sufficient context" signal so
the model can decline honestly.

```ts
export function shouldDecline(hits: RankedHit[], { minScore, minCount }: { minScore: number; minCount: number }): boolean {
  const strong = hits.filter((h) => h.score >= minScore);
  return strong.length < minCount;
}
```

**When to use / when not to.** Budget-based assembly with a relevance floor is the default for
user-facing Q&A. For a tiny corpus (under ~20 chunks total, fitting comfortably), skip retrieval
entirely and put everything in context â€” retrieval then only adds failure modes.

**Trade-offs.** More context raises recall of the answer and lowers precision, plus increases latency
and cost linearly in context tokens. The relevance floor is the lever that keeps precision from
collapsing. Ordering strategies trade a little accuracy for a lot of robustness at small context
sizes.

**Production considerations.**
- Log exactly what was assembled (chunk ids, labels, order, token count) on every request. Without
  this, "the model ignored the right document" is undebuggable.
- Report `context_fill_ratio` and `chunks_excluded_by_budget` as metrics; if you routinely exclude the
  top chunk, your budget or ordering is wrong.
- Make `perChunkCap` and `maxChunks` config, not constants, and run them through your eval set.
- Preserve stable source numbering across retries so citations remain valid.

**Common mistakes.**
- Fixed `topK = 5` regardless of budget or document length.
- Similarity order with the best chunk buried in the middle.
- Asking the question *before* the context.
- No relevance floor, so the model answers from four irrelevant chunks with total confidence.
- Citations the model can invent, because you never gave it resolvable source ids.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you decide how much context to put in the prompt?"
- "How do you handle the case where retrieval finds nothing relevant?"
- "How do you verify citations?"

---

---
### 13.4.8 Evaluating RAG: retrieval metrics and groundedness

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** RAG has two independently measurable halves. **Retrieval metrics** measure whether the
right chunks were found (deterministic, given labels). **Groundedness** measures whether the answer is
supported by what was retrieved (requires judgement). Teams that measure only the second half tune
prompts forever while the real bug sits in chunking.

**Mechanics â€” retrieval metrics.**

- **Recall@k**: fraction of queries where at least one (or all) relevant chunk appears in the top k.
  Recall@50 for the candidate stage; recall@5 or recall@8 for the final stage.
- **Precision@k**: fraction of retrieved chunks that are relevant. Diagnostic for noise.
- **MRR (mean reciprocal rank)**: rewards putting a relevant chunk high. Best single-number proxy for
  "will the generator see the answer."
- **nDCG@k**: graded relevance with position discount. Best when you have graded labels.
- **Context recall / context precision** (generation-oriented framing): did the retrieved set contain
  the sentences needed to write a correct answer?

```ts
export interface RetrievalCase {
  id: string;
  query: string;
  relevantChunkIds: string[];
  gradedRelevance?: Record<string, number>; // chunkId -> 0..3
}

export function retrievalMetrics(
  cases: RetrievalCase[],
  runs: Map<string, RankedHit[]>,
  ks = [1, 3, 5, 10, 20, 50],
): Record<string, number> {
  const out: Record<string, number> = {};
  for (const k of ks) {
    let recall1 = 0, recallAll = 0, precision = 0, mrr = 0;
    for (const c of cases) {
      const top = (runs.get(c.id) ?? []).slice(0, k);
      const ids = new Set(top.map((h) => h.id as string));
      const hits = c.relevantChunkIds.filter((id) => ids.has(id));
      recall1 += hits.length > 0 ? 1 : 0;
      recallAll += hits.length / c.relevantChunkIds.length;
      precision += top.length > 0 ? hits.length / top.length : 0;
      const first = top.findIndex((h) => c.relevantChunkIds.includes(h.id as string));
      mrr += first >= 0 ? 1 / (first + 1) : 0;
    }
    const n = cases.length;
    out[`recall_at_${k}`] = recall1 / n;
    out[`context_recall_at_${k}`] = recallAll / n;
    out[`precision_at_${k}`] = precision / n;
    out[`mrr_at_${k}`] = mrr / n;
  }
  for (const c of cases) {
    const top = runs.get(c.id) ?? [];
    out[`ndcg_at_10`] = (out[`ndcg_at_10`] ?? 0) + ndcgAt(top.slice(0, 10), c);
  }
  out[`ndcg_at_10`] /= cases.length;
  return out;
}

function ndcgAt(top: RankedHit[], c: RetrievalCase): number {
  if (!c.gradedRelevance) {
    const dcg = top.reduce((s, h, i) => s + (c.relevantChunkIds.includes(h.id as string) ? 1 / Math.log2(i + 2) : 0), 0);
    const ideal = c.relevantChunkIds.slice(0, top.length)
      .reduce((s, _, i) => s + 1 / Math.log2(i + 2), 0);
    return ideal > 0 ? dcg / ideal : 0;
  }
  const gain = (id: string) => Math.pow(2, (c.gradedRelevance![id] ?? 0)) - 1;
  const dcg = top.reduce((s, h, i) => s + gain(h.id as string) / Math.log2(i + 2), 0);
  const ideal = Object.values(c.gradedRelevance!).sort((a, b) => b - a).slice(0, top.length)
    .reduce((s, g, i) => s + (Math.pow(2, g) - 1) / Math.log2(i + 2), 0);
  return ideal > 0 ? dcg / ideal : 0;
}
```

**Groundedness.** Two families:

**Deterministic proxies** (free, always run):
- Citation resolution: does every cited id exist in the assembled sources?
- Entailment by substring: for factual claims with numbers or identifiers, does the number appear in
  the cited source?
- Sentence-level coverage: fraction of source sentences that are referenced by the answer (too high
  means the answer is a bland summary; too low means it wandered).

**Model-based judgement** (costs money, needed for real claims):
- **Claim decomposition â†’ per-claim entailment.** Split the answer into atomic claims, then for each,
  ask a judge model whether the retrieved sources entail it. This is far more reliable than a single
  holistic judgement because it localizes failures.
- **LLM-as-judge rubric scoring.** One call, a numeric rubric, calibrated against human labels.
- **Human review on a sample.** Ground truth for your judge. 50â€“200 hand-scored answers, periodically
  re-scored.

```ts
export interface GroundednessReport {
  totalClaims: number;
  supported: number;
  unsupported: { claim: string; bestSourceId?: string; reason: string }[];
  coverage: number;
  judgeModel: string;
}

export async function scoreGroundedness(
  answer: string,
  sources: Map<string, string>,
  judge: JudgeFn,
): Promise<GroundednessReport> {
  const claims = await decomposeClaims(answer);
  const unsupported: GroundednessReport["unsupported"] = [];
  let supported = 0;

  // Batch claims to keep judge calls cheap; source ids keep the judge honest.
  const verdicts = await judge(`
You are a strict fact-checker. For each numbered claim, decide whether the SOURCES entail it.
Answer "supported" only if a specific source contains the information. Partial support is not support.
Disagreement or a plausible-sounding but absent fact is "unsupported".

SOURCES
${[...sources.entries()].map(([id, text]) => `<source id="${id}">${text}</source>`).join("\n\n")}

CLAIMS
${claims.map((c, i) => `${i + 1}. ${c}`).join("\n")}

Return JSON: {"verdicts": [{"index": 1, "verdict": "supported"|"unsupported", "sourceId": "...",
 "reason": "one clause"}]}
`, { schema: VERDICTS_SCHEMA });

  verdicts.verdicts.forEach((v, i) => {
    if (v.verdict === "supported") supported++;
    else unsupported.push({ claim: claims[i], bestSourceId: v.sourceId, reason: v.reason });
  });

  const cited = new Set(extractCitations(answer));
  const coverage = sources.size > 0 ? [...cited].filter((id) => sources.has(id)).length / sources.size : 0;
  return {
    totalClaims: claims.length,
    supported,
    unsupported,
    coverage,
    judgeModel: judge.modelId,
  };
}
```

**Judge calibration.** A judge model with no calibration is a random number generator with good manners.
Calibrate: score 100â€“200 human-labelled answers, measure agreement (target: high agreement on the
binary supported/unsupported decision), then use disagreement cases as judge eval cases. Also
**position-bias controls**: randomize claim order between runs and check the verdict distribution is
stable; and prefer pairwise comparisons over absolute scales, which LLMs handle far more reliably.

**End-to-end correctness.** The metric that matters to product is "did the user get a right answer,"
which requires a task-specific definition: for a support bot, did the reply resolve the ticket; for
extraction, did the extracted field match ground truth. Track a small set of these, and treat
retrieval metrics as *leading indicators* you can iterate on cheaply.

**When to use / when not to.** Build the retrieval eval set before you build anything else â€” it is the
single highest-leverage hour in a RAG project. Skip LLM-judge groundedness in early prototyping; use
citation resolution and manual spot checks until you have labelled answers to calibrate against.

**Trade-offs.** Labelled datasets cost human time and go stale as the corpus changes. LLM judges cost
money per run and can be gamed by a prompt optimized against that specific judge. Mitigations: judge
with a different model family than the generator, rotate judges periodically, and keep a permanent
human-labelled anchor set.

**Production considerations.**
- Run retrieval evals in CI on every chunker, embedding model, or index change. They are fast and
  deterministic.
- Run generation evals nightly, not per-commit, since they cost money and are nondeterministic.
- Log per-stage metrics so you can attribute a quality drop to a specific stage change.
- Track eval coverage: what fraction of production query shapes is your eval set actually
  representative of? Cluster production queries weekly and diff against your eval distribution.

**Common mistakes.**
- Measuring only end-to-end answer quality, so you cannot tell retrieval from generation.
- An uncalibrated LLM judge treated as ground truth.
- Building an eval set entirely from synthetic queries the model helped write, then optimizing for it.
- Judging with the same model that generated the answer.
- Not re-running retrieval evals after a chunker change.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you evaluate a RAG system?"
- "How do you know whether a bad answer is a retrieval problem or a generation problem?"
- "How do you calibrate an LLM judge?"

---

### 13.4.9 RAG failure modes and how to debug them

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** RAG has a small number of recurring failure modes with distinct signatures. Debugging is
a matter of learning to read the signature and then walking the pipeline in order.

**Mechanics â€” the failure catalogue and its fixes.**

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Answer confidently wrong, cites nothing | Retrieval found nothing relevant; model answered from priors | Relevance floor + explicit decline path |
| Answer correct but cites the wrong source | Answer was right by chance, or citation numbers guessed | Citation resolution check; give resolvable ids |
| Cites a source that does not contain the claim | Model conflated similar sources | Per-claim entailment judge in CI; stronger citation instruction |
| Great answer for short queries, poor for long | Budget assembly truncating or excluding | Budget-based assembly; decomposition for long questions |
| Good on exact terms, poor on paraphrase | Vectors only, no lexical leg | Hybrid search |
| Great on paraphrase, poor on error codes | Sanitisation stripped codes, or no lexical leg | Preserve codes; hybrid search |
| Perfect offline, poor in production | Eval set not representative | Cluster production queries; measure coverage |
| Degrades over time | Index stale | Incremental indexing (13.4.10) |
| Degrades after a deploy | Prompt or model change | Regression suite; recipe-level rollback |
| Same query, different answer each time | No caching, no pinning, nondeterministic sampling | Cache; lower temperature; pin model |
| Answer from an old policy | Deletions not propagated | Soft-delete propagation + index sweep |
| Correct answer from an irrelevant-looking chunk | Chunk contains the fact but not in isolation | Breadcrumb prefixes; parent expansion |

**The debugging procedure.** Always walk the pipeline in order, and stop at the first broken stage.

```ts
export async function debugRagQuery(query: string, tenantId: string): Promise<RagDebugReport> {
  const stages: any[] = [];

  // 1. Retrieval candidates
  const dense = await vectorSearch(toVector(query), tenantId, 50);
  const lexical = await lexicalSearch(query, tenantId, 50);
  stages.push({ stage: "candidates", dense: dense.length, lexical: lexical.length,
    denseTop: dense.slice(0, 5).map((h) => ({ id: h.id, score: h.score, preview: preview(h.meta.text) })) });

  // 2. Fusion
  const fused = reciprocalRankFusion([
    { name: "dense", hits: dense, weight: 1 },
    { name: "lexical", hits: lexical, weight: 1 },
  ]);
  stages.push({ stage: "fused", n: fused.length,
    onlyInDense: fused.filter((f) => f.source === "dense").length,
    onlyInLexical: fused.filter((f) => f.source === "lexical").length });

  // 3. Rerank
  const { hits, diagnostics } = await retrieveWithRerank(query, tenantId,
    { lexicalK: 50, denseK: 50, finalK: 8, rerank: reranker });
  stages.push({ stage: "rerank", diagnostics });

  // 4. Assembly
  const asm = assembleByBudget(hits, remainingBudget(budget), tokenizer);
  stages.push({ stage: "assembly", usedTokens: asm.usedTokens,
    excludedByBudget: asm.excluded.length, included: asm.included.map((h) => h.id) });

  // 5. Generation
  const res = await generate(query, asm.included, recipe);
  stages.push({ stage: "generation", stopReason: res.stopReason,
    outputTokens: res.usage.outputTokens });

  // 6. Verification
  const ground = await scoreGroundedness(textOf(res.content), sourceMap(asm.included), judge);
  stages.push({ stage: "verify", unsupportedClaims: ground.unsupported.length,
    unresolvedCitations: ground.unsupported.filter((u) => !u.bestSourceId).length });

  return { query, stages, verdict: diagnose(stages) };
}

function diagnose(stages: any[]): string {
  const [c, f, r, a, g, v] = stages;
  if (r.diagnostics.finalIds.length === 0) return "RETRIEVAL_EMPTY: no candidates survived filtering";
  if (c.dense === 0 && c.lexical === 0) return "INDEX_EMPTY: filters likely too restrictive";
  if (v.unsupportedClaims > 0 && a.excludedByBudget > 0)
    return "ASSEMBLY_TRUNCATED: relevant chunk likely dropped by budget";
  if (f.onlyInLexical > f.onlyInDense * 3) return "LEXICAL_DOMINANT: consider hybrid weights";
  if (v.unsupportedClaims > 0 && r.diagnostics.reranked) return "GENERATION_HALLUCINATION: evidence was present";
  if (g.stopReason === "max_tokens") return "TRUNCATION: raise budget or chunk the task";
  return "UNKNOWN: inspect stages";
}
```

**The three highest-yield debugging techniques.**
1. **Show the prompt.** Literally paste the assembled prompt for the failing case. Half of all "the
   model is dumb" reports dissolve here.
2. **Swap stages.** Replace retrieval with "the one chunk you know is right." If the answer is still
   wrong, it is a generation or prompt problem, not retrieval.
3. **Check the chunk, not the document.** Read the actual retrieved chunk text. Frequently the fact is
   absent because chunking cut it out.

**When to use / when not to.** Use this procedure whenever you have a *specific* reported failure with
a stored request. For aggregate quality monitoring, use the dashboards and sampling from 13.8 instead â€”
you cannot debug 400 failures individually.

**Trade-offs.** Rich debug tooling costs storage (storing full prompts and retrieved chunk text) and
engineering time. Mitigate by sampling: keep full prompt snapshots for 1% of traffic plus 100% of
flagged failures, and keep chunk text references (ids) for everything so it can be rehydrated.

**Production considerations.**
- One-click "explain this answer" in your internal tooling. It pays for itself in the first week.
- Store the retrieved chunk ids *and* the assembled prompt hash on every request.
- When users report a bad answer (a feedback button), capture the full debug report automatically.
- Watch for failure modes that change shape over time: index rot, prompt drift, and model updates all
  look like "it used to work better."

**Common mistakes.**
- Debugging by re-running the query and getting a different answer (nondeterminism masquerading as a
  bug).
- Blaming the model when the right chunk was retrieved but never included in the prompt.
- Not storing chunk text with the trace, so you cannot reconstruct what the model saw.
- Fixing retrieval for a query type that represents 0.1% of traffic.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "A user says the answer was wrong. Walk me through your debugging process."
- "How do you tell whether a bad answer is retrieval or generation?"

---

### 13.4.10 Data freshness and incremental indexing

ðŸ”Ÿ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Freshness is the guarantee that the index reflects the source of truth within a stated
window. It is an SLO ("p95 staleness under 15 minutes for changed documents, under 1 hour for new
documents") and it requires an explicit change-propagation design.

**Mechanics â€” the ingestion pipeline with change tracking.**

```ts
export interface IngestJob {
  source: SourceRef;
  sourceVersion: string;      // etag, hash, or updated_at from the source
  mode: "full" | "incremental";
}

export async function ingest(job: IngestJob): Promise<IngestReport> {
  const existing = await db.query(
    `SELECT source_version, indexed_at FROM document_sources WHERE source = $1`, [job.source.id]);
  if (existing.rows[0] && existing.rows[0].source_version === job.sourceVersion) {
    return { status: "skipped", reason: "unchanged" };
  }

  const docs = await fetchChangedDocuments(job.source, job.sourceVersion);
  const report: IngestReport = { status: "ok", added: 0, updated: 0, deleted: 0, unchanged: 0 };

  for (const doc of docs) {
    const contentHash = sha256(doc.text);
    const prev = await db.query(
      `SELECT id, content_hash, chunker_ver, embed_model FROM documents
        WHERE source = $1 AND external_id = $2`, [job.source.id, doc.externalId]);

    if (prev.rows.length === 0) {
      await indexDocument({ ...doc, contentHash, mode: "insert" });
      report.added++;
      continue;
    }
    const p = prev.rows[0];
    if (p.content_hash === contentHash) { report.unchanged++; continue; }

    // Content changed. Re-chunk and re-embed, but reuse embeddings for unchanged chunks
    // when the chunk text is byte-identical.
    const { chunks, reusedEmbeddingIds } = await chunkAndDiff(doc, p);
    const toEmbed = chunks.filter((c) => !c.textHashes.has(p.id));
    const vecs = toEmbed.length > 0 ? await embedder.embed(toEmbed.map((c) => c.text), "document") : [];

    await db.transaction(async (tx) => {
      await tx.query(`UPDATE documents SET content_hash = $1, updated_at = now() WHERE id = $2`,
        [contentHash, p.id]);
      // Soft-delete old chunks; they become visible to vacuum, not to search.
      await tx.query(`UPDATE chunks SET deleted_at = now() WHERE doc_id = $1 AND deleted_at IS NULL`, [p.id]);
      await insertChunks(tx, p.id, chunks, vecs);
    });
    report.updated++;
  }

  // Hard-delete handling: sources that removed documents must propagate.
  const seen = new Set(docs.map((d) => d.externalId));
  const stale = await db.query(
    `SELECT id, external_id FROM documents WHERE source = $1`, [job.source.id]);
  for (const s of stale.rows) {
    if (seen.has(s.external_id)) continue;
    await softDeleteDocumentTree(s.id);      // documents AND chunks AND parent nodes
    report.deleted++;
  }

  await db.query(
    `UPDATE document_sources SET source_version = $1, indexed_at = now() WHERE source = $2`,
    [job.sourceVersion, job.source.id]);
  return report;
}
```

**Soft delete and vacuum.** Never hard-delete chunks you may still need to debug; mark them and exclude
them from search. But tombstones degrade ANN quality and waste space, so run a periodic sweep that
physically removes rows deleted more than N days ago, ideally during low traffic.

```sql
-- Vacuum: remove tombstones older than 7 days, in bounded batches.
WITH dead AS (
  SELECT id FROM chunks WHERE deleted_at < now() - interval '7 days' LIMIT 5000
)
DELETE FROM chunks c USING dead WHERE c.id = dead.id;
```

**Trigger strategies.**
- **Webhook / event-driven:** fastest, but you must handle missed and duplicated events. Keep a
  periodic reconciliation sweep regardless, because webhooks are always lossy.
- **Polling on change tokens:** simple and reliable for sources with an `updated_at` or etag. Poll at
  a frequency matching your staleness SLO.
- **Full reindex on a schedule:** the safety net. Weekly or nightly for critical corpora.

**Chunk-level diffing.** Re-embedding an entire document on every keystroke-save is expensive. Compute
chunk text hashes and reuse the stored vector for any chunk whose text is byte-identical. For a 200-chunk
document where 3 chunks changed, that is 3 embeddings instead of 200.

```ts
export async function chunkAndDiff(
  doc: SourceDocument, prev: DocumentRow,
): Promise<{ chunks: ChunkDraft[]; reusedEmbeddingIds: Set<string> }> {
  const chunks = buildChunks(doc, CHUNKER_CONFIG);   // current version
  const prevChunks = await db.query(
    `SELECT id, text_hash, embedding FROM chunks WHERE doc_id = $1 AND deleted_at IS NULL`, [prev.id]);
  const prevByHash = new Map(prevChunks.rows.map((r) => [r.text_hash, r]));

  const reused = new Set<string>();
  const drafts: ChunkDraft[] = chunks.map((c) => {
    const h = sha256(c.text);
    const p = prevByHash.get(h);
    if (p) { reused.add(p.id); prevByHash.delete(h); }
    return { ...c, textHash: h, reuseId: p?.id };
  });
  // Unmatched previous chunks are gone: their ids stay in `reused` and must not be re-inserted.
  return { chunks: drafts, reusedEmbeddingIds: reused };
}
```

**Handling embedding-model or chunker-version change.** This is a full reindex. Do it as a **dual-index
migration**, never a drop-and-rebuild:

```
1. Create chunks_v2 alongside chunks_v1.
2. Backfill in the background, prioritized by query traffic (hot documents first).
3. Shadow-query: run both indexes for real traffic, log recall differences on labelled queries.
4. Cut over reads at a config flag.
5. Keep v1 warm for one rollback window, then drop.
```

**When to use / when not to.** Any corpus that changes while users read it needs a freshness design. If
your corpus is a versioned artifact â€” a documentation snapshot, a release, a quarterly filing â€” you can
use immutable snapshots and a pointer swap, which is far simpler and gives perfect consistency for
as-of questions.

**Trade-offs.** Freshness costs write throughput, index churn, and dual-write complexity during
migrations. Aggressive freshness (continuous reindex) can dominate your embedding bill; lazy freshness
(weekly) is cheap and occasionally embarrassingly wrong. The SLO should reflect what your users
actually need: a policy document needs hours; a changelog needs seconds.

**Production considerations.**
- Expose index freshness in your own status dashboard alongside the model provider's status.
- Alert on ingestion failures and on `staleness_p95` breaching the SLO.
- Make ingestion idempotent â€” jobs retry, events duplicate, and the pipeline must survive both.
- Serialize ingestion per source with a lock so two runs cannot interleave deletes and inserts.
- Chunk-level diffing is the single biggest ingest cost optimization; implement it early.

**Common mistakes.**
- Only reindexing on a schedule and never reacting to deletes, so removed policies remain answerable.
- Re-embedding everything on every change.
- Dropping and rebuilding the index in place during a deploy, with no rollback.
- Trusting webhooks alone, with no reconciliation sweep, and drifting further out of sync every week.
- Forgetting that soft-deleted documents still appear in *search results UI* if you filter only in the
  vector query and not in the metadata query.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you keep a RAG index fresh?"
- "What happens to your index when you change the embedding model?"
- "How do you handle a document being deleted?"

---

---
## 13.5 Tool and Function Calling

### 13.5.1 The contract: name, description, JSON schema

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Tool calling is how a model requests that *your* code do something. The model never
executes anything; it emits a structured request naming a tool and providing arguments, and your server
decides whether to run it. The tool definition **is** the API surface you are designing.

**Mechanics.** Three fields, each doing a specific job:

1. **Name.** A verb-noun identifier, stable across prompt versions. Models match names to descriptions
   far more reliably than humans expect; renaming a tool is a breaking change to behaviour, not just
   to code. Prefer one clear name over two overlapping ones â€” ambiguity between similar tools is the
   top cause of wrong-tool selection.
2. **Description.** The model's *only* documentation. It must say what the tool does, when to use it,
   when **not** to use it, what each argument means including units and formats, and what the return
   value is. This is the highest-leverage text in your entire system.
3. **Input schema.** A JSON Schema for the arguments, with `required`, `additionalProperties: false`,
   enums, and descriptions on every property. The schema is both documentation (the model reads the
   `description` fields) and, where supported, a decoding constraint.

```ts
export interface ToolSpec {
  name: string;
  description: string;
  input_schema: Record<string, unknown>;
  // Optional, your own metadata â€” never sent to the model.
  meta?: {
    destructive?: boolean;
    requiresApproval?: boolean;
    idempotent?: boolean;
    scopes: string[];              // authz scopes the CALLER must hold
    costClass?: "free" | "cheap" | "expensive";
    timeoutMs?: number;
  };
}

export const searchOrders: ToolSpec = {
  name: "search_orders",
  description: [
    "Search the current customer's orders by status, date range, or free-text query.",
    "Use this when the user asks about their own orders, shipments, or order status.",
    "Returns up to 25 orders, newest first, as {id, status, placedAt, totalCents, itemCount}.",
    "If the user asks about pricing or plan features rather than specific orders, use list_plans instead.",
    "Never use this to search the product catalogue; use search_catalog for that.",
  ].join(" "),
  input_schema: {
    type: "object",
    additionalProperties: false,
    required: ["query"],
    properties: {
      query: {
        type: "string",
        description:
          "Free-text search terms, e.g. 'sneakers', or a status like 'pending'. May be empty string to list all orders.",
      },
      status: {
        type: "string",
        enum: ["pending", "paid", "shipped", "delivered", "cancelled", "refunded"],
        description: "Optional exact status filter. Omit to search across all statuses.",
      },
      placedAfter: {
        type: "string",
        description: "ISO-8601 date, inclusive lower bound, e.g. '2026-01-15'. Omit for no lower bound.",
      },
      placedBefore: {
        type: "string",
        description: "ISO-8601 date, exclusive upper bound. Omit for no upper bound.",
      },
      limit: {
        type: "integer",
        minimum: 1,
        maximum: 25,
        description: "Maximum results to return. Defaults to 10. Hard cap 25.",
      },
    },
  },
  meta: {
    destructive: false,
    requiresApproval: false,
    idempotent: true,
    scopes: ["orders:read"],
    costClass: "cheap",
    timeoutMs: 3_000,
  },
};
```

**Output shape for tool results.** Return a compact, structured, *already-formatted* result. The model
reads tool output and either reuses it or summarizes it, so verbose JSON wastes tokens and invites
misreading. Return exactly the fields needed, plus a stable `ok` discriminator, and cap size.

```ts
export function formatToolResult(ok: boolean, data: unknown, err?: { code: string; message: string }): string {
  const payload = ok
    ? { ok: true, data: truncateDeep(data, MAX_RESULT_CHARS) }
    : { ok: false, error: { code: err?.code ?? "unknown", message: err?.message ?? "tool failed" } };
  return JSON.stringify(payload);
}
```

Errors are results, not exceptions. An error tool result lets the model recover ("the date was invalid,
let me retry") instead of aborting the whole turn.

**When to use / when not to.** Use tool calling when the model needs information you have (a database,
an internal API, live state) or the ability to take an action with side effects. Do not use it when a
deterministic function call would do â€” putting a tool around `add(a, b)` adds latency, tokens, and a
failure mode for nothing. Keep the tool count small: past roughly 15â€“20 tools in a single request,
selection accuracy degrades sharply. Split into multiple tool groups selected by a router.

**Trade-offs.** Tools cost prompt tokens (schemas are usually a few hundred to a couple thousand tokens
each) on **every** request. Schema strictness improves argument quality but makes the model fail
harder on unusual inputs. Descriptions cost tokens too, and verbose descriptions compete with each
other for the model's attention.

**Production considerations.**
- Version tool schemas alongside prompts; a schema change alters model behaviour.
- Validate arguments against the schema **server-side** before executing, even when the provider claims
  to enforce it. Never trust the model's arithmetic, ranges, or enum values.
- Bound every result. A tool returning 2MB of JSON will blow the context and cost.
- Tool definitions must be part of the cached prefix (13.7.2) or you pay for them on every turn.

**Common mistakes.**
- Vague descriptions ("Gets data") with no units or format hints.
- Two tools whose descriptions overlap, so the model picks wrong.
- No `additionalProperties: false`, letting the model invent parameters you then reject.
- Returning raw database rows with internal fields and IDs you did not intend to expose.
- Throwing on tool failure instead of returning an error result.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you write a good tool description?"
- "How do you decide how many tools to expose?"
- "What do you do when the model passes invalid arguments?"

---

### 13.5.2 Tool selection accuracy and description engineering

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Tool selection accuracy is the probability the model picks the right tool given the
request. It is measurable with a labelled set, and it is improved primarily by **description
engineering**, not by model upgrades.

**Mechanics â€” the levers, in order of impact.**

**1. Disambiguate boundaries explicitly.** State what each tool is for *and* what to use instead. Models
choose by contrast.

```
- search_orders: Use for the customer's own orders. NOT for product availability â€” use search_catalog.
- search_catalog: Use for products, stock, and pricing. NOT for a customer's order history.
```

**2. One tool per concept.** If you have `get_user` and `get_user_with_orders`, you have created a
choice. Prefer one tool with an optional `include` parameter.

**3. Put argument constraints in the description.** Models respect prose constraints surprisingly well:
"Date must be ISO-8601. Do not pass relative dates like 'last month'."

**4. Include the shape of the return value.** It reduces hallucinated assumptions about what comes back.

**5. Name the trigger conditions.** "Use when the user says 'refund', 'return', or 'money back'."

**6. Reduce the tool count via routing.** Two-stage dispatch: a cheap classifier picks a tool group,
then only that group's tools are exposed.

```ts
export interface ToolGroup { id: string; label: string; tools: ToolSpec[]; examples: string[]; }

export async function selectToolGroup(
  userText: string, groups: ToolGroup[], classifier: ModelFn,
): Promise<ToolGroup> {
  const catalogue = groups
    .map((g) => `<group id="${g.id}" examples="${escapeAttr(g.examples.join(" | "))}">\n${g.label}\n</group>`)
    .join("\n");
  const res = await classifier({
    model: FAST_MODEL,
    maxOutputTokens: 40,
    temperature: 0,
    responseSchema: {
      type: "object",
      additionalProperties: false,
      required: ["group"],
      properties: { group: { type: "string", enum: groups.map((g) => g.id) } },
    },
    messages: [
      {
        role: "system",
        content:
          "Route the request to exactly one tool group. Output the group id only.\n\n" +
          "GROUPS\n" + catalogue,
      },
      { role: "user", content: userText },
    ],
  });
  const parsed = JSON.parse(textOf(res.content));
  return groups.find((g) => g.id === parsed.group) ?? groups[0];
}
```

**Measuring selection accuracy.** Build a labelled set: for each of 100â€“200 realistic requests, the tool
a human says *should* be called (and the arguments). Then measure:

```ts
export interface ToolSelectionCase {
  id: string;
  userText: string;
  history?: ChatMessage[];
  expectedTool: string;
  expectedArgs?: Record<string, unknown>;
  forbiddenTools?: string[];   // tools that would cause harm or cost if chosen
}

export interface SelectionReport {
  accuracy: number;
  toolAccuracy: number;         // right tool
  argAccuracy: number;          // right tool AND right args
  confusion: Record<string, Record<string, number>>;
  forbiddenChosen: number;      // must be zero
}

export async function evaluateSelection(
  cases: ToolSelectionCase[], run: (c: ToolSelectionCase) => Promise<{ tool: string; args: unknown }>,
): Promise<SelectionReport> {
  const confusion: SelectionReport["confusion"] = {};
  let toolOk = 0, argOk = 0, forbidden = 0;

  for (const c of cases) {
    const got = await run(c);
    confusion[c.expectedTool] ??= {};
    confusion[c.expectedTool][got.tool] = (confusion[c.expectedTool][got.tool] ?? 0) + 1;
    if (got.tool === c.expectedTool) {
      toolOk++;
      if (c.expectedArgs === undefined || deepEqual(got.args, c.expectedArgs)) argOk++;
    }
    if (c.forbiddenTools?.includes(got.tool)) forbidden++;
  }

  const n = cases.length;
  return {
    accuracy: toolOk / n,
    toolAccuracy: toolOk / n,
    argAccuracy: argOk / n,
    confusion,
    forbiddenChosen: forbidden,
  };
}
```

**Reading the confusion matrix.** Errors are rarely random. Typical patterns: `search_orders` and
`get_order` confused (fix: merge or sharpen boundaries); `search_orders` chosen for product questions
(fix: add the contrast line); a rare tool never chosen (fix: add trigger words to its description);
`no_tool` cases where the model invents a call (fix: add an explicit "if none apply, respond directly").

**Description A/B testing.** Because descriptions are just prompt text, treat them as experiments. Run
the selection eval against candidate descriptions, gate on accuracy and on `forbiddenChosen`, and
record the winner in the prompt changelog.

**When to use / when not to.** Always measure on more than a handful of tools â€” selection accuracy
degrades non-linearly as the tool set grows. If you have under five well-differentiated tools, careful
descriptions are usually enough and you do not need routing. Add routing when you exceed ~15 tools or
when accuracy is high on the common cases but poor on the long tail.

**Trade-offs.** Every sentence of description costs tokens on every request, including turns where the
tool is irrelevant. Explicit contrast lines are worth it; full prose documentation is not. Routing adds
a model call and a failure mode (wrong group) in exchange for a much smaller tool list.

**Production considerations.**
- Keep the selection eval in CI when you change any description or add a tool.
- Track `tool_call_rate`, `unknown_tool_rate`, `invalid_args_rate`, and `no_op_tool_rate` (tool called
  and result ignored) in production. A rising no-op rate means descriptions or the prompt are off.
- Log every tool selection with its request text so new confusions are discoverable from real traffic.
- Version descriptions and keep the eval labels in sync.

**Common mistakes.**
- Judging tool quality by whether it "works when called" rather than whether it gets called.
- Adding tools without adding eval cases.
- Making descriptions long and abstract instead of short and operational.
- Not measuring argument accuracy separately from tool accuracy â€” a right tool with wrong arguments is
  usually still a bug.

**Interviewer probes.**
- "How do you improve tool-calling accuracy?"
- "How many tools is too many?"
- "How do you test tool selection?"

---

### 13.5.3 Parallel tool calls and orchestration

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Definition.** Models can request several tool calls in one turn (parallel calls), and they can be
driven across multiple turns (sequential orchestration). The orchestration layer is deterministic code
you own; the model only ever *proposes* the next action.

**Mechanics â€” the loop.** The invariant that keeps agentic systems safe and debuggable: **the loop is
your code.** You decide the tools, the iteration cap, the authorization, the timeouts, and when to
stop. The model only emits the next action.

```ts
export interface AgentStep {
  index: number;
  modelOutput: ModelResponse;
  toolCalls: { id: string; name: string; input: unknown }[];
  toolResults: { toolUseId: string; content: string; isError: boolean }[];
  usage: Usage;
  latencyMs: number;
}

export interface AgentResult {
  steps: AgentStep[];
  finalText: string;
  stop: "end_turn" | "max_steps" | "budget_exceeded" | "cancelled" | "no_progress";
  totalUsage: Usage;
  transcript: ChatMessage[];
}

export async function runAgentLoop(
  initial: ChatMessage[],
  tools: ToolRegistry,
  opts: {
    system: string;
    maxSteps?: number;
    maxToolCalls?: number;
    deadlineMs?: number;
    signal?: AbortSignal;
    onEvent?: (e: AgentEvent) => void;
  },
): Promise<AgentResult> {
  const maxSteps = opts.maxSteps ?? 8;
  const maxToolCalls = opts.maxToolCalls ?? 24;
  const transcript: ChatMessage[] = [
    { role: "system", content: opts.system },
    ...initial,
  ];
  const steps: AgentStep[] = [];
  let toolCallCount = 0;
  let totalUsage: Usage = { inputTokens: 0, outputTokens: 0, cachedInputTokens: 0 };
  const toolSignatures: string[] = [];

  for (let i = 0; i < maxSteps; i++) {
    if (opts.signal?.aborted) return finish("cancelled");
    const res = await client.complete({
      model: MODEL, maxOutputTokens: 1500, messages: transcript,
      tools: tools.specs(), toolChoice: { type: "auto" },
    }, { signal: opts.signal, timeoutMs: opts.deadlineMs ?? 90_000 });

    totalUsage = addUsage(totalUsage, res.usage);
    const toolUses = res.content.filter(isToolUse);

    const step: AgentStep = {
      index: i, modelOutput: res, toolCalls: [], toolResults: [],
      usage: res.usage, latencyMs: 0,
    };

    if (toolUses.length === 0) {
      steps.push(step);
      return { steps, finalText: textOf(res.content), stop: "end_turn", totalUsage, transcript };
    }

    toolCallCount += toolUses.length;
    if (toolCallCount > maxToolCalls) return finish("budget_exceeded");

    // Loop detection: identical tool+args twice in a row means we are stuck.
    const sig = toolUses.map((t) => `${t.name}:${stableStringify(t.input)}`).sort().join("|");
    toolSignatures.push(sig);
    if (i > 0 && toolSignatures[i] === toolSignatures[i - 1]) return finish("no_progress");

    // Execute tool calls. Independent calls run concurrently; dependent ones cannot.
    const executions = await executeToolCalls(toolUses, tools, {
      signal: opts.signal,
      onEvent: opts.onEvent,
      // Never execute a tool the loop has not seen and authorized.
      maxConcurrency: 6,
    });

    for (const e of executions) {
      step.toolCalls.push({ id: e.id, name: e.name, input: e.input });
      step.toolResults.push({ toolUseId: e.id, content: e.content, isError: e.isError });
      opts.onEvent?.({ type: "tool_result", name: e.name, ok: !e.isError });
    }
    steps.push(step);

    // Append assistant turn (with tool_use blocks) then the tool results.
    transcript.push({ role: "assistant", content: res.content });
    transcript.push({ role: "tool", content: executions.map((e) => ({
      type: "tool_result", tool_use_id: e.id, content: e.content, is_error: e.isError,
    })) });
  }
  return finish("max_steps");

  function finish(stop: AgentResult["stop"]): AgentResult {
    const last = steps[steps.length - 1];
    return {
      steps,
      finalText: last ? textOf(last.modelOutput.content) : "",
      stop, totalUsage, transcript,
    };
  }
}
```

**Parallel versus sequential.** If the model emits multiple tool uses in one turn, they are
*intentionally* independent â€” the model has already decided it needs both. Execute them concurrently
with a concurrency cap, but be careful: "independent" means the model thinks so, not that they are.
`transfer_funds` and `check_balance` emitted together are a real hazard if the model expected the
balance first. Mitigations: mark mutating tools as non-parallelizable in metadata; serialize tools with
a side-effect lock per resource; and require approval for destructive tools regardless.
