```ts
console.log(" attempt to change the amount:", JSON.stringify(run.resolve(transfer.awaiting, bob, "edit",
  { args: { amountCents: 250_000 }, reason: "correcting the amount" })));

console.log("\n== bob approves the real thing ==");
const approved = run.resolve(transfer.awaiting, bob, "approve");
console.log(JSON.stringify({ ok: approved.ok, status: approved.proposal?.status,
  result: approved.result }, null, 2));

console.log("\n== idempotency: replaying execution ==");
console.log(JSON.stringify(execute(db.proposals.get(transfer.awaiting), db.proposals.get(transfer.awaiting).args, bob)));

console.log("\n== rejection path feeds back a reason ==");
const d = propose({ conversationId: "c1", tool: "issue_refund", caller: bob,
  args: { orderId: "ORD-9", amountCents: 5000, currency: "USD", reason: "duplicate charge" } });
const rej = run.resolve(d.proposal.id, bob, "reject", { reason: "order was already refunded via the returns desk" });
console.log("status:", rej.proposal.status, "| feedback:", rejectionFeedback(rej.proposal).slice(0, 120));

console.log("\n== expiry ==");
const e = propose({ conversationId: "c1", tool: "cancel_order", caller: bob, args: { orderId: "ORD-3", itemCount: 2 } });
db.proposals.get(e.proposal.id).expiresAt = Date.now() - 1;
console.log(JSON.stringify(run.resolve(e.proposal.id, bob, "approve")));

console.log("\n== audit trail ==");
console.table(db.audit.map((a) => ({ event: a.event, tool: a.tool ?? "", by: a.by ?? a.reviewer ?? "" })));
```

**Run it.** `node hitl.mjs` â†’ observe the four refusals (self-approval, wrong tenant, stale auth,
argument tampering) all failing for *structural* reasons, then the correct approval executing exactly the
proposed action, then idempotent replay, then the rejection feedback, then expiry.

**What to notice.**
- Confirmation copy comes from server-side templates over validated arguments. A prompt-injected model
  cannot change what the reviewer is shown.
- `argsHash` makes approval binding: any edit to the arguments invalidates the confirmation and forces a
  fresh approval.
- Four-eyes is enforced for financial and security actions, plus re-auth within 5 minutes and MFA for
  security actions.
- The status transition to `executing` happens before execution, and execution is keyed by
  `tool:argsHash`, so double-clicks and resumed runs deduplicate.
- Rejections produce structured feedback so the model changes approach instead of retrying.
- Expired proposals fail closed.
- Auto-approval is gated on measured intervention rate and never applies to security actions.

**Trade-offs.** HITL converts latency you control into latency you do not, and creates a queue with its
own SLAs and a rubber-stamping risk. Measure intervention rate (edited-or-rejected Ã· total) as the real
safety metric: near zero on high-risk actions means your reviewers are not reading, and your control is
theatre. Progressive autonomy is the way out â€” start conservative, then relax per tool per tenant as
evidence accumulates.

---

---
# PHASE 13 DEBUGGING SCENARIOS (12)

> Each scenario is a realistic production failure, presented as: symptom â†’ how to diagnose â†’ root cause
> â†’ fix. Several are deliberately subtle.

---

## Scenario 1 â€” The Chat UI froze after a deploy

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** Streaming chat worked in staging. In production, users click send and the full response
appears at once after a long pause. Locally it streams correctly.

**Diagnosis.** Compare response headers between environments.

```
local:      content-type: text/event-stream
production: content-type: text/event-stream
```

Headers match, so it is not the app. Check *when* the first byte arrives:

```bash
curl -N -s -X POST https://prod/api/chat -d '{"conversationId":"c1","text":"hi"}' \
  -w '\nTTFB=%{time_starttransfer} TOTAL=%{time_total}\n'
```

TTFB â‰ˆ total time. So the server is not emitting early. Two candidates: (a) a reverse proxy is
buffering, (b) the server is not actually streaming.

**Instrument.**

```ts
const t0 = Date.now();
const res = await client.stream(req);
console.log("first provider event after", Date.now() - t0, "ms");
res.on("delta", () => { if (!firstSentAt) { firstSentAt = Date.now(); console.log("first byte to client", firstSentAt - t0, "ms"); } });
```

The first provider event arrives at 40ms but the first byte to the client arrives at 2.8s â€” the delay is
*after* your handler.

**Root cause.** Nginx (or an ingress) buffers the upstream response. `proxy_buffering on` with a
buffer size larger than your response accumulates the whole stream before forwarding.

**Fix.**

```nginx
location /api/chat {
  proxy_pass http://app;
  proxy_http_version 1.1;
  proxy_set_header Connection '';
  proxy_buffering off;              # do not buffer SSE
  proxy_cache off;
  chunked_transfer_encoding on;
  proxy_read_timeout 300s;
  gzip off;                          # gzip re-buffers
}
```

Plus the response headers from 13.2.5: `cache-control: no-cache, no-transform`,
`x-accel-buffering: no`. The `no-transform` directive also stops intermediate proxies transforming the
stream.

**Lesson.** Streaming is a property of the whole path, not just your handler. Add a CI smoke test that
asserts first-byte latency is well under total latency, or this will regress silently.

---

## Scenario 2 â€” Costs tripled overnight with no traffic increase

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** Daily spend went from $180 to $540. Request volume flat. No deploy on the model side.

**Diagnosis.** Group the ledger by dimension.

```sql
SELECT date_trunc('hour', at) h, model, feature, count(*) calls,
       sum(input_tokens) in_tok, sum(cached_tokens) cached, sum(output_tokens) out_tok,
       round(sum(cost_cents),2) cents
  FROM spend_records
 WHERE at > now() - interval '3 days'
 GROUP BY 1,2,3 ORDER BY 1 DESC, 6 DESC LIMIT 20;
```

Two findings: `cached_tokens` dropped to near zero for `support.reply`, and `output_tokens` per call rose
~2.5x.

**Investigate the cache collapse first.** A `cacheHitRate` dashboard shows a step change at 03:00 UTC.

```ts
// Diff the rendered prefix between a hit and a miss.
const a = renderSupportPrompt(fixedInput);
const b = renderSupportPrompt(fixedInput);
console.log(cachePrefixHash(a.messages), cachePrefixHash(b.messages));   // differ!
console.log(JSON.stringify(a.messages[0]).slice(0, 200));
console.log(JSON.stringify(b.messages[0]).slice(0, 200));
```

The system prompt now starts with a tenant name â€” a personalization change shipped at 03:00. The prefix
is no longer stable across requests, so nothing caches.

**Root cause (two bugs).**
1. **Personalization moved into the system prompt**, which sits in the cached prefix. The user's name
   must go in the tail.
2. **Output growth**: the prompt edit also added "provide a thorough, detailed response" while a separate
   change added three more few-shot examples. Output tokens rose accordingly.

**Fix.**
- Move identity into the volatile region; keep system + tools static.
- Add the CI assertion from 13.3.4: `expect(cachePrefixHash(render(fixedInput))).toEqual(CACHE_PREFIX_HASH)`.
  This would have caught it before deploy.
- Revert the verbosity instruction; set `maxOutputTokens` per feature rather than relying on prose.

**Lesson.** Cost regressions are usually prompt-layout regressions. Track cache hit rate as a first-class
SLO per feature, and snapshot-test the prefix hash.

---

## Scenario 3 â€” Truncated JSON crashes the parser

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** ~4% of extraction requests fail with `Unexpected end of JSON input`. The failure rate is
higher on long documents.

**Diagnosis.** Look at the failed requests' `stop_reason`.

```sql
SELECT stop_reason, count(*), round(avg(output_tokens))
  FROM message_observations
 WHERE created_at > now() - interval '1 day' AND output_schema_ok = false
 GROUP BY 1;
```

100% of failures are `max_tokens`. Not a parser bug. The response is being cut mid-object because the
output budget is smaller than the schema requires.

The schema has 14 fields, several with `maxLength: 2000`, and `maxOutputTokens` is 1,200. A maximal
answer needs well over 4,000 tokens.

**Root cause.** The output budget was set for the old 6-field schema and never revisited.

**Fix â€” in order of preference.**

1. **Raise the budget from the schema**, with headroom measured from the observed distribution:

```ts
const required = estimateMaxTokensFromSchema(schema);       // sum of maxLengths + overhead
const budget = Math.ceil(required * 1.15);
// and derive the actual max from p99 of observed output, not a guess:
const p99 = percentile(outputTokensByFeature, 0.99);
const maxOutputTokens = Math.max(budget, p99 * 1.3);
```

2. **Detect it and fail loudly**, not with a parse error:

```ts
if (res.stopReason === "max_tokens") {
  metrics.count("truncation", { feature });
  throw new TruncationError({ feature, outputTokens: res.usage.outputTokens, required });
}
```

3. **Reduce what must be generated**: split the schema into a small "primary" object plus a detail array
   with a cap, so a complete answer always fits.

**Lesson.** `stop_reason` is the field that tells you the truth. Never detect truncation by string
heuristics, and never set `maxOutputTokens` without deriving it from something.

---

## Scenario 4 â€” RAG answers cite sources that do not contain the answer

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** Users report "it made up a citation." The answer looks reasonable; the cited chunk is
topically related but does not contain the claim.

**Diagnosis.** Build a per-claim check over a sample.

```ts
const sample = await db.query(
  `SELECT id, content, citations FROM messages
    WHERE feature = 'rag.answer' AND status = 'complete' AND created_at > now() - interval '2 days'
    ORDER BY random() LIMIT 200`);
const sources = await loadSourcesFor(sample.rows);
const bad = [];
for (const m of sample.rows) {
  const cited = extractCitations(m.content);
  for (const n of cited) {
    const src = sources.get(m.id)?.[n - 1];
    if (!src) { bad.push({ id: m.id, why: "unresolved", n }); continue; }
    const claims = splitClaims(m.content).filter((c) => c.citations.includes(n));
    const support = judgeSupport(claims, src);
    if (support.unsupported > 0) bad.push({ id: m.id, why: "unsupported", n, support });
  }
}
console.log("bad citation rate:", bad.length / sample.rows.length);
```

Rate is 11%. Now check *which* source numbers are being cited.

```sql
SELECT (regexp_match(content, '\[(\d+)\]'))[1] AS cite, count(*)
  FROM messages WHERE feature='rag.answer' AND created_at > now() - interval '2 days'
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC;
```

Almost every citation is `[1]`. But look at the assembly: chunks were ordered by score, and the prompt
lists them in that order â€” yet the *generator* is citing index 1 far more than chance would predict.

**Root cause.** Two problems stacked.
1. **Ordering**: chunks were inserted best-last (closest to the question), so `[1]` was the *weakest*
   chunk while the strongest was `[5]`. The model over-weighted the start of the context and cited it.
2. **Chunk truncation**: `perChunkCap` truncated chunks mid-table, so the source the model saw did not
   contain the row it cited.

**Fix.**
- Order highest-relevance-first for Q&A, and log the mapping from label â†’ chunk id so you can verify
  independently of the generator's citation.
- Raise `perChunkCap`; never truncate below a complete sentence or row.
- Add the deterministic output check from 13.6.5: citations must resolve, and numbers must appear in the
  cited source. That alone would have blocked all 11%.

```ts
const v = validateOutput(raw, { sources: assembledSources, policy: { requireCitations: true } });
if (!v.allow) return { ok: false, code: v.code, detail: v.detail };
```

**Lesson.** Citation correctness is verifiable without a judge. Make it a blocking check, not a nice
to-have.

---

## Scenario 5 â€” The agent loops forever on one failing tool

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** A support agent burns its full token budget (~$3) on about 2% of conversations, always the
same shape: 24 tool calls, all `get_order`, then `max_steps`.

**Diagnosis.** Read a trace.

```ts
const trace = await loadTrace(runId);
const calls = trace.steps.flatMap((s) => s.toolCalls);
console.log(calls.length, [...new Set(calls.map((c) => c.name))]);
console.log(calls.slice(0, 6).map((c) => c.args));
```

Every call is `get_order({orderId: "ORD-9999"})`. That order does not exist. The tool returns:

```json
{"ok": false, "error": {"code": "not_found", "message": "order not found"}}
```

The model reads that, and calls the same tool with the same id.

**Root cause.** The error result did not tell the model what to do next, and nothing stopped repetition.
The loop had a step cap (which is why it terminated) but no loop detection and no *remedy* in the error.

**Fix â€” three layers.**

1. **Make the error actionable and terminal for that argument.** Errors should carry a `retryable: false`
   and a `remedy` hint:

```ts
if (!order) {
  return toolResult(false, null, {
    code: "not_found",
    message: "No order ORD-9999 exists for this account.",
    retryable: false,
    remedy: "Do not retry this order id. Either call search_orders to find the correct id, or ask the user which order they mean.",
  });
}
```

2. **Detect repetition in the loop and fail with a useful error**, so the user gets a sentence rather
   than a timeout:

```ts
const fingerprint = calls.map((c) => `${c.name}:${canonical(c.args)}`).sort().join("|");
if (fingerprints.includes(fingerprint)) {
  return { stop: "no_progress", userMessage:
    "I got stuck trying to look up that order. Could you give me the order number, or should I search your recent orders?" };
}
```

Also track per-tool failure counts and stop after 2 consecutive failures of the same tool.

3. **Bound the damage**: a per-tool call cap (`get_order: 4`) inside the overall budget.

```ts
const perTool = new Map();
for (const c of res.toolCalls) {
  const n = (perTool.get(c.name) ?? 0) + 1;
  perTool.set(c.name, n);
  if (n > TOOL_LIMITS[c.name]) return { stop: "tool_limit", tool: c.name };
}
```

**Lesson.** Loop detection is not a safety net, it is a UX feature. The real fix is making tool errors
instructive: an error is a message to the model, so write it as one.

---

## Scenario 6 â€” One tenant sees another tenant's data

âš« Expert

**Symptom.** A customer reports that a document search returned a chunk from a different company. Rare
(3 reports in a week), always in the "compare my invoices to last quarter's" flow.

**Diagnosis.** Reproduce with the tenant-switching code path.

```ts
const caller = { userId: "u1", tenantId: "acme", scopes: new Set(["docs:read"]) };
const input = { query: "invoices", topK: 5 };
const hits = await retrieve(input.query, { topK: input.topK });   // <-- no tenant filter!
```

Confirmed: one retrieval helper takes only a query and searches the whole corpus. Two other helpers do
filter by tenant.

**Root cause.** A missing tenant filter in one code path â€” and a red-team case that only tested the tool
layer, not retrieval. The prompt also *contained* the other tenant's content, so even with per-call
tool authorization the model had already been shown it.

**Fix â€” three layers.**

1. **Make unfiltered retrieval impossible by construction.** Tenant scoping is not an argument; it comes
   from a request-scoped context:

```ts
class RequestContext {
  constructor(readonly caller: CallerIdentity) {}
  // tenantId is not a parameter, so it cannot be forgotten.
  async searchCorpus(qv: Float32Array, k: number) {
    return db.query(
      `SELECT id, content FROM chunks
        WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND deleted_at IS NULL
        ORDER BY embedding <=> $2::vector LIMIT $3`, [this.caller.tenantId, toPgVector(qv), k]);
  }
}
```

2. **Post-hoc filter on retrieved content.** Defence in depth, because SQL filters get refactored away:

```ts
const hits = results.filter((h) => h.tenant_id === caller.tenantId);
if (hits.length < results.length) audit.append({ event: "cross_tenant_candidate_dropped", count: results.length - hits.length });
```

3. **An output guardrail for identifiers.** Every invoice/customer/document id in the answer must exist
   in the caller's data. This catches the case where the model somehow knows an id it should not.

```ts
const leaked = extractIdentifiers(answer).filter((id) => !knownIdsFor(caller).has(id));
if (leaked.length) return { ok: false, code: "unknown_identifier", detail: leaked };
```

4. **A red-team case** in CI: cross-tenant retrieval via query phrasing, not via tool arguments.

**Lesson.** Tenant isolation must be structural â€” a capability the code cannot express, rather than a
parameter a code path can forget. Any function that reads data should get identity from a scope, not
arguments.

---

## Scenario 7 â€” The eval score improved and production got worse

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** A prompt change lifted the offline score from 0.71 to 0.79 on a 200-case golden set.
Two weeks later, escalation rate doubled and users complain the answers are longer but less useful.

**Diagnosis.** Line up the timeline and compare slices.

```ts
const cand = await runEval("support.v4");
const base = baselines.get("support.v3");
console.log("overall", base.overall, "â†’", cand.overall);
console.table(slices(base).map((s) => ({ slice: s.slice, base: s.acc, cand: sliceOf(cand, s.slice).acc, n: s.n })));
```

Overall improved. But per slice:

| slice | base | cand | n |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| short_factual | 0.95 | 0.96 | 60 |
| multi_tenant | 0.61 | 0.66 | 8 |
| long_context | 0.52 | 0.79 | 4 |

The entire improvement comes from `long_context`, which is 4 cases out of 200. That slice gained 0.27
Ã— 4 = 1.1 "case points" out of 200 Ã— ~0.1 = 20 aggregate points gained. **More than half the reported
improvement is four cases.**

Then check production:

```sql
SELECT recipe_id, count(*), round(avg(edit_distance_ratio),3) edits,
       round(avg(accept)::numeric,3) accept, round(avg(escalated)::numeric,3) escalate,
       round(avg(output_tokens)) out_tok
  FROM interaction_events
 WHERE feature='support.reply' AND at > now() - interval '30 days'
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;
```

`support.v4` has higher acceptance but 40% higher output tokens and much higher edit distance. Users
accepted and then rewrote it. The eval's rubric rewarded "thoroughness"; users wanted brevity.

**Root cause.** Three compounding issues: (a) the eval set is unrepresentative â€” 4 long-context cases
dominating a headline metric; (b) the rubric had a "completeness" dimension that rewarded length and was
never calibrated against human preference; (c) the eval set contains no cases drawn from the segments
that escalated.

**Fix.**
1. **Report weighted-and-per-slice, never the aggregate alone.** Make the CI gate evaluate per slice with
   a minimum *n*, and refuse to promote on a slice with n < 20 without a human sign-off.
2. **Recalibrate the rubric against real human edits.** Take 200 accepted-then-edited answers, compute
   edit distance, and score them with the judge. Where edit distance is high, the judge should have
   scored low. It did not, so the rubric is wrong.
3. **Sample production clusters weekly** and diff against the eval distribution:

```ts
const prodClusters = await clusterProductionQueries(7);
const coverage = reportCoverage(prodClusters, CASES);
console.log(coverage.uncoveredClusters);   // e.g. "refund_for_partial_year: 3.1% of traffic, 0 cases"
```

4. **Add guardrail metrics to the gate**: output tokens, edit distance proxy, escalation â€” so a quality
   gain that doubles length is visible at promotion time.

**Lesson.** An offline score is a hypothesis about production, not a measurement of it. The two failure
modes to fear are an unrepresentative dataset and an uncalibrated judge, and per-slice reporting is the
cheapest defence against both.

---

## Scenario 8 â€” `context_length_exceeded` after the history feature launched

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** New failures, all on conversations over ~30 turns: the provider rejects the request outright.

**Diagnosis.**

```sql
SELECT count(*), round(avg(input_tokens)) avg_in, max(input_tokens) max_in,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE error_code = 'context_length_exceeded') errs
  FROM message_observations WHERE feature='chat' AND created_at > now() - interval '1 day';
```

`max_in` is above the model's context window. The window for this model is 200k, so `max_in` around
201k means the last turn pushed it over â€” the *next* request would too, but it fails first.

**Root cause.** History is projected with `maxChars` (13.2.8) but the budget was expressed in
*characters*, while the limit is in *tokens*. For conversations containing code or non-Latin text,
characters-per-token is well above 4. A 900k-character budget is ~250k tokens.

**Root cause (second).** No headroom was reserved for the output. `budget = window - maxOutputTokens`
was computed with `maxOutputTokens = 8000`, but long-form answers sometimes need more, and the provider
checks input + requested output against the window.

**Fix.**

```ts
// 1. Measure with the tokenizer, not characters.
const budgetTokens = tokenizer.encode(serialize(projected.messages)).length;

// 2. Reserve output plus a real margin.
const usable = windowTokens - req.maxOutputTokens - SAFETY_MARGIN_TOKENS;   // margin ~2k

// 3. Enforce it at send time, not at projection time.
if (budgetTokens > usable) {
  // Deterministic, not a model call: compact, then re-measure.
  await maybeCompact(store, conversationId, { windowTokens: usable, triggerFraction: 0.8, keepRecentTurns: 8, maxSummaryTokens: 600 });
  const reprojected = projectHistory(await store.history(conversationId), await store.summary(conversationId), budget);
  if (tokenCount(reprojected.messages) > usable) throw new ContextBudgetExceeded({ needed: tokenCount(reprojected.messages), usable });
}
```

Also: **fail gracefully.** Catch `context_length_exceeded` specifically and compact-and-retry *once*,
rather than surfacing a 500.

```ts
catch (e) {
  if (e.code === "context_length_exceeded") {
    await forceCompact(conversationId, { aggressive: true });
    return await attempt(1);          // one retry, deliberately
  }
  throw e;
}
```

**Lesson.** Token limits are token limits. Character-based budgeting is a bug waiting for a user with a
code-heavy conversation, and you must reserve output headroom or you will fail on the request *after*
the one that filled the window.

---

## Scenario 9 â€” A prompt edit broke nothing detectable and cost 15%

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** Prompt `classify.v6` shipped. All evals passed with a comfortable margin (0.88 â†’ 0.86,
threshold 0.05). Two weeks later someone notices non-English inputs are much worse. Nobody noticed at
launch because the eval set is 100% English.

**Diagnosis.** Slice the golden set by language â€” there is only one slice:

```ts
console.log([...new Set(CASES.map((c) => c.metadata.slice))]);
// [ 'billing', 'account', 'incident', 'adversarial' ]
```

No `non_english` slice exists, so a total collapse there is invisible to the aggregate.

Re-run manually on a quick Spanish and German set:

```ts
const probe = ["necesito un reembolso", "ich kann mich nicht anmelden", "no puedo iniciar sesiÃ³n"];
for (const t of probe) {
  const r = await runOnce("classify.v6", t);
  console.log(t, "â†’", r.out);
}
```

The v6 prompt removed a line that said "respond in the language of the input." The model now emits the
canonical label with a translated reason field, or worse, mis-labels.

**Root cause.** Not a regression by the gate's definition â€” the gate measured what the dataset contains.
The real root cause is **eval coverage**: the dataset has no coverage of a dimension the product supports.

**Fix.**
1. **Extend the dataset** to cover languages the product claims to support, weighted by traffic.
2. **Make coverage a checked property, not a hope.** Tag every case with dimensions and assert coverage in
   CI:

```ts
export function assertCoverage(required: { slice: string; minCases: number }[]) {
  for (const { slice, minCases } of required) {
    const n = CASES.filter((c) => c.metadata.slice === slice).length;
    if (n < minCases) throw new Error(`eval coverage gap: ${slice} has ${n}/${minCases} cases`);
  }
}
```

3. **Compare the prompt diff against the removed lines.** A diff of `v5 â†’ v6` shows the deletion; that
   is a two-minute review that would have caught this. Add a prompt-diff reviewer checklist item: "list
   every capability or language removed."
4. **Sample production traffic by language** and add cases when a cluster is uncovered:

```ts
const byLang = await db.query(
  `SELECT language_hint, count(*) FROM interaction_events
    WHERE feature='classify' AND at > now() - interval '14 days' GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC`);
```

**Lesson.** A gate can only catch what the dataset measures. Coverage gaps are silent, permanent, and
invisible in aggregates. Treat "what does my eval set *not* cover?" as a first-class review question.

---

## Scenario 10 â€” Streaming responses occasionally arrive out of order

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** In a chat UI, some answers have the first and last sentences in the wrong order, or a
paragraph repeats. Only under load. Never in staging.

**Diagnosis.** Log the sequence numbers the server sends and the order the client applies them.

```ts
// server
let seq = 0;
for await (const ev of providerStream) {
  send("delta", { seq: seq++, text: ev.text, turnId });
}
// client
const seen = new Map();
for await (const ev of stream()) {
  if (ev.type === "delta") {
    if (seen.has(ev.data.seq)) continue;         // duplicate
    seen.set(ev.data.seq, ev.data.text);
    bubble.textContent = [...seen.keys()].sort((a,b)=>a-b).map(k=>seen.get(k)).join("");
  }
}
```

With the sequence map applied, the display is correct â€” confirming the bug is **duplicate frames**,
not reordering.

Where do duplicates come from? A proxy with automatic retry on a stalled upstream replays the buffered
prefix. Or: the app is deployed with two instances behind a load balancer, and a client reconnect
(retry) re-attaches mid-stream to a second instance.

**Root cause.** The SSE response is being retried by an intermediary. SSE is a long-lived response, and
anything that buffers-and-retries will replay. The app also has no `turnId`, so a reconnect cannot tell a
resumed stream from a new one.

**Fix.**
1. **Disable proxy retries/buffering for the SSE route** (`proxy_next_upstream off` for that location,
   `proxy_buffering off`).
2. **Add a `turnId` and `seq` to every frame**, and make the client idempotent per `seq` â€” which is
   correct regardless of who is duplicating.
3. **Make reconnects explicit.** A client that reconnects sends `Last-Event-ID`; the server resumes from
   a stored cursor, or restarts the turn cleanly with a new `turnId` and a `reset` frame the client acts
   on.

```ts
// client: on reconnect
if (ev.type === "reset") { seen.clear(); bubble.textContent = ""; }
// turn ids are scoped so a stale stream from a previous turn can never append to the current bubble
if (ev.data.turnId !== currentTurnId) continue;
```

**Lesson.** Assume anything between you and the browser will duplicate, reorder, and buffer. Design the
frame protocol to be idempotent, and treat the transport as untrusted. This is the same lesson as
13.2.6's idempotency, applied to a stream.

---

## Scenario 11 â€” The safety classifier is blocking legitimate security questions

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** Support ticket volume from the security team spiked with "assistant unavailable" messages.
Users asking things like "how do I rotate an API key" and "show me the auth logs for tenant X" are being
blocked.

**Diagnosis.** Inspect the blocks by category and by user segment.

```sql
SELECT segment, category, count(*) blocks, count(*) FILTER (WHERE appealed) appeals
  FROM guardrail_blocks WHERE at > now() - interval '7 days' GROUP BY 1,2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;
```

100% of blocks are in one segment (internal security engineers) and one category (`injection_pattern`).
Appeals are 0 â€” nobody appealed, they just gave up and used the terminal.

The specific rule firing is `/you\s+are\s+now\s+/i` plus a keyword heuristic on "auth logs". A legitimate
question â€” "you are now asking me to prove I own the account, what do you need?" â€” trips it.

**Root cause.** Two design mistakes. (a) A keyword heuristic on security vocabulary, which is exactly the
vocabulary a legitimate security team uses. (b) No appeal path, so false positives are invisible: they
look like product breakage, not like a guardrail problem.

**Fix.**

1. **Scope rules by trust and by segment.** The injection heuristic should be strongest on public,
   untrusted input and relaxed for an internal, authenticated, already-authorized segment â€” while still
   structurally enforced (least privilege) regardless.

```ts
const POLICY = {
  public:      { injection: "block", secretPatterns: "block" },
  partner:     { injection: "quote", secretPatterns: "allow" },
  internal:    { injection: "quote", secretPatterns: "allow" },
};
// Structural controls â€” least privilege, tenant scoping, egress restrictions â€” are unchanged for all.
```

2. **Replace the keyword heuristic with the structural control it was standing in for.** The reason we
   blocked "show me auth logs" was fear of unauthorized data access. That is an *authorization* problem;
   solve it by checking the caller's scopes, not by pattern-matching their vocabulary.

```ts
if (needsTenantData(args, caller)) {
  const missing = requiredScopes(args).filter((s) => !caller.scopes.has(s));
  if (missing.length) return deny("missing_scope", missing);
}
```

3. **Add an appeal path** and measure false-positive rate from it:

```ts
await notifications.enqueue({ type: "guardrail_appeal", userId: caller.userId, blockId, reason });
```

Track `block_rate` **by segment** and alert on divergence between segments â€” that divergence is your
false-positive detector.

4. **Never remove the structural controls.** Relaxing *prompt-level* heuristics under pressure is
   acceptable; relaxing tenant scoping or least privilege is not.

**Lesson.** Guardrails need a measured false-positive rate and an escape hatch that is itself audited.
A guardrail that silently blocks a whole customer segment is an outage you caused deliberately, and
because no one appeals, your dashboards will never tell you.

---

## Scenario 12 â€” The "smart" reranker made retrieval worse

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

**Symptom.** After switching rerankers (a smaller, faster, domain-fine-tuned-on-other-data model), recall@5
on the labelled set went *down* from 0.86 to 0.79, but p95 latency improved by 180ms. Someone shipped it
anyway because the latency win looked good in a dashboard.

**Diagnosis.** Compare per-query, not per-aggregate.

```ts
const base = baselines.get("rerank.a");
const cand = await evalRetrieval("rerank.b");
const moved = cand.perQuery.map((q, i) => ({
  query: q.query, before: base.perQuery[i].rr, after: q.rr, delta: q.rr - base.perQuery[i].rr,
})).filter((m) => Math.abs(m.delta) > 0.2);
console.table(moved.slice(0, 12));
```

A pattern emerges: every regression has a long chunk (>400 tokens) or contains a table. The new reranker
has a shorter effective input window and was trained on short passages.

**Root cause.** The reranker's `maxTokensPerPair` is 512 and it is silently truncating long candidates.
With short candidates it wins (hence better on the cases where it did not regress), so the aggregate on a
randomly-worded eval looked acceptable before the last gate was bypassed.

There is also a process failure: **the latency gate had no corresponding quality floor**, and someone had
authority to override the quality gate. Both are structural problems.

**Fix.**

1. **Fix the truncation.** Give the reranker complete candidates:

```ts
const candidates = fused.slice(0, 100);
const pairs = candidates.map((c) => ({
  id: c.id,
  // No silent truncation: over-length candidates are dropped and logged, not cut in half.
  tokens: tokenizer.encode(c.meta.text).length,
  text: c.meta.text,
})).filter((p) => p.tokens <= reranker.maxTokensPerPair);
const dropped = candidates.length - pairs.length;
if (dropped > 0) metrics.count("rerank_dropped_overlength", { n: dropped });
```

2. **Add a per-query regression gate**, not just an aggregate one:

```ts
const regressions = cand.perQuery
  .map((q, i) => ({ query: q.query, before: base.perQuery[i].rr, after: q.rr }))
  .filter((m) => m.before - m.after > 0.2);
if (regressions.length > 4) { console.error("gate FAIL: 5+ queries regressed materially"); process.exit(1); }
```

3. **Make latency improvements conditional on quality.** The gate should express "latency may improve
   **up to** X, quality may not regress at all":

```ts
if (qualityRegressed || latencyGain > maxAllowedLatencyGainForThisQualityDrop) fail();
```

4. **Characterise a reranker before adopting it** â€” token limits, effective context, training
   distribution â€” and record that in the config next to the model id.

**Lesson.** Every model in the pipeline has its own input limits and its own distribution. "Smaller and
faster" is a hypothesis; measure per query, watch for silent truncation, and never allow a latency gain
to be purchased with unmeasured quality loss.

---

---
# PHASE 13 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

---

**Q1. What is a stop reason, and why do you care about every value?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

The field that tells you *why* generation stopped. `end_turn` is the model finished naturally.
`max_tokens` means it was truncated â€” a silent correctness failure, not an error, and the single most
common cause of "the model ignores the second half of my instructions." `tool_use` means it wants a tool
executed. `stop_sequence` means a custom delimiter fired, which is usually intentional and occasionally a
bug where a delimiter appeared in the data. `refusal` means safety filtering or your own instructions
blocked it. I branch on it explicitly: truncation gets a different strategy (raise the budget, shrink the
prompt, or split the task), refusal gets routed or transformed, and `tool_use` enters the tool loop.
Retrying identically on truncation cannot help, and retrying a refusal never does.

---

**Q2. How would you design a provider abstraction?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

One narrow interface for the core: `complete(ChatRequest) â†’ ModelResponse` and
`stream(ChatRequest) â†’ AsyncIterable<StreamEvent>`, where my own types are messages, content blocks, stop
reason, and usage. Then a `capabilities` object per provider, because features genuinely differ: native
JSON-schema mode, strict tool schemas, parallel tool calls, explicit versus automatic prompt caching,
logprobs, reasoning modes, batch endpoints. Application code reads capabilities and branches; it never
sees a vendor field name. I add escape hatches for provider-specific features rather than
lowest-common-denominator everything â€” an abstraction that erases prompt caching controls is worse than
no abstraction. And I write a normalization test per provider so a vendor response-shape change fails in
CI rather than in production.

---

**Q3. How do you guarantee the model returns valid JSON?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Four layers, strongest last. One: the schema is the source of truth, and I generate the validator, the
TypeScript type, and the prompt's output section from it, so they cannot drift. Two: use native schema
mode where available, keeping the schema inside the provider's supported feature subset. Three: validate
server-side with the generated validator regardless, because schema mode guarantees shape, not truth. Four:
a bounded repair loop â€” on failure I resend the original request plus a short, specific list of the failing
paths, at most once, and then degrade to a documented failure path. I also include an explicit escape
hatch in the contract, like an `insufficient_evidence` variant, because a schema with no way to say "I
don't know" forces the model to invent data to fill required fields. And detection is a metric:
`schema_validation_failed` with the failing path, not the payload.

---

**Q4. How do you keep conversation state?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Server-side, always. Providers are stateless; every request resends the full history, so the canonical
state lives in my database and I *project* a sendable view from it. The browser sends only the latest
user input plus a conversation id â€” if the client owned history, refresh would lose it, I could not
summarize or compact, I could not enforce retention, and two tabs would diverge. Projection uses
windowing, plus summarization for long conversations: a cumulative, factual, entity-focused summary
replacing the oldest span, with recent turns kept verbatim. Messages form a tree with a `parentId`, because
"edit and resend" and regenerate are much cheaper to support from the start than to retrofit. Every
request stores the exact messages array or its hash, so any bad turn is reproducible.

---

**Q5. How do you cancel a model call, and what happens to partial output?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Three layers. The browser aborts the `fetch` with an `AbortController`; the server forwards the signal to
the provider request, which closes the socket and stops generation *and* stops billing. Partial output
that I already buffered is mine, and I persist it marked `partial` â€” never deleted, never replayed as a
complete turn. The third layer is the one people forget: aborting the stream does not roll back side
effects. If the model already wrote to the database mid-loop, I need an explicit transaction boundary or
a compensation step. I distinguish retry semantics by situation: user-cancelled means no auto-retry,
offer "continue" or "discard"; deadline before any output means safe to retry; deadline mid-stream means
offer resume rather than transparent retry, because the user has already read some text.

---

**Q6. Explain prompt caching and how you structure prompts for it.**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Prompt caching reuses computation for a byte-identical prefix, billed at a much lower rate, so the layout
of my prompt is a cost-control mechanism. The rule: everything static at the front â€” system prompt, tool
schemas, tenant policy â€” and everything volatile at the back â€” retrieved evidence, the user question. Chat
history sits after the static prefix and before the volatile tail, so early turns stay cached across a
session. The failure modes are all subtle: a timestamp or user name in the system prompt, non-deterministic
tool-schema ordering, history placed before the prefix, or a per-request parameter change. I snapshot-test
the prefix hash in CI, which is a five-line test that would have caught a real threefold cost increase we
hit from putting tenant personalization in the system prompt. Cache writes cost a premium, so caching pays
only when the prefix is reused; and the suffix is always full price.

---

**Q7. How would you debug a wrong RAG answer?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

I walk the pipeline in order and stop at the first broken stage. First, retrieval candidates: does the
right chunk appear in the dense list, the lexical list, or neither? If neither, it's chunking, embeddings,
or query mismatch. Second, fusion and reranking: is it in the top 20 but pushed out of the top 5? That is
a ranking problem, fixable with weights or a better reranker. Third, assembly: was it in the final list
but excluded by the token budget or the relevance floor? I log chunk ids, labels, order, and token counts
on every request, so this is answerable rather than a guess. Fourth, generation: with the right chunk
present and in the prompt, is the answer still wrong? Then it's a prompt or contract problem. Two techniques
make this fast: showing the literal assembled prompt, and swapping retrieval for one known-good chunk â€” if
the answer is still wrong, retrieval was never the problem.

---

**Q8. When is a semantic cache dangerous?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

When the answer varies with something the embedding does not capture. Two concrete failures. Cross-user:
"what's my invoice total?" embeds fine but must never serve another user's answer â€” so the cache key must
contain tenant identity structurally, not probabilistically. Temporally: "how many tickets are open?" is
semantically stable but factually volatile, so a high-similarity hit serves a wrong number with total
confidence. Safe uses are stable, non-personalized answers: FAQ, documentation lookups, classification
labels, canned responses. I would never cache anything with side effects, and never a tool call that wrote.
The threshold is the risk dial, and you tune it against *negative* pairs â€” near-identical questions that
require different answers â€” and require the cache to miss all of them. And I hide cache hits from users
honestly for anything time-sensitive, because silently serving a stale answer destroys trust the first
time someone notices.

---

**Q9. Explain HNSW and how you'd tune it.**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

HNSW is a layered proximity graph: search starts on a sparse top layer, greedily descends to denser layers,
then beam-searches near the query. Three parameters. `m` is edges per node â€” higher gives better recall and
more memory. `ef_construction` is build-time graph quality â€” higher is better and slower to build.
`ef_search` is the query-time beam width, and that is the dial I actually operate: raising it improves
recall and costs latency, and I set it from a recall target measured on my labelled set, higher for hard
queries. Critically, I always measure ANN recall against exact flat search, because without that baseline
you cannot tell whether a low recall is the index or your expectations. Practical notes: the operator
class must match your distance function or the index may not be used; filtered ANN is hard, so filter
selectivity is a real design constraint; and builds are CPU- and memory-heavy, so they belong in a
background job with a dual-index migration, not a deploy.

---

**Q10. Why do you need lexical search if you have embeddings?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Because embeddings are weak at exactly what users type literally: error codes like `ERR_PAYMENT_4021`,
part numbers, function names, API endpoints, proper nouns the model never saw in pretraining, exact
phrases, legal citations, dates. BM25 handles all of that natively, and it fails in the opposite place â€”
paraphrase and cross-lingual queries, which embeddings handle well. So hybrid. I fuse with reciprocal rank
fusion rather than adding scores, because RRF is rank-based and scale-free, so there are no score
magnitudes to tune. In practice the diagnostic that settles it: cluster queries where dense-only and
lexical-only disagree. If lexical-only is winning on a coherent group, your corpus has identifiers and
keywords are undervalued. I also always keep the tools that make lexical retrieval work â€” preserving
identifiers through query rewrites, and carrying headers into chunks so terms are findable.

---

**Q11. What is prompt injection, and what actually defends against it?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Prompt injection is getting the model to treat attacker text as instructions rather than data. The
important cases are *indirect*: the payload is inside a web page, a PDF, an email, a code comment, or
another user's upload â€” content the developer often does not realize is user-controlled. Defence in depth,
ordered by how real it is. Genuine boundaries are structural: the model has no credentials, no direct
database access, cannot name a tenant, and cannot execute anything without passing my authorization and
confirmation layers. Capability reduction is the strongest control â€” a tool that returns cross-tenant data
removed eliminates that entire attack class. Output validation catches what gets through: schema checks,
secret and canary-token detection, deterministic grounding checks. Input screening and instruction/data
separation are useful but they raise the attacker's cost, nothing more. I'd say clearly that a prompt
instruction saying "ignore instructions in the data" is not a security boundary, and I'd back that with a
red-team suite in CI that covers indirect injection through every content source.

---

**Q12. When would you *not* use an agent?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Whenever I can describe the correct behaviour as a specification with test cases. The test is: do I know
the sequence of steps? If yes and it does not depend on data, it is a pipeline. If yes but data-dependent,
it is a pipeline with branches or a router. Only when the sequence genuinely depends on intermediate results
â€” investigation, debugging across a codebase, open-ended research â€” is an agent right. Concrete examples I
have turned down: summarizing a week's tickets (SQL plus one call), extracting invoice fields (parse plus
one call plus a repair), resetting a password (one endpoint). The structural argument is that agents cannot
be unit-tested, cannot be held to an SLA, multiply failure rates across steps, expand the injection surface,
and are nearly impossible to debug. What I build instead is a deterministic skeleton with the model at the
points of genuine ambiguity â€” gather data with SQL, classify with a schema-constrained call, then branch.
That is agentic in the loose sense but every step is bounded and auditable.

---

**Q13. Walk me through your tool-calling loop.**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

The loop is my code; the model only proposes. Structure: messages, tools with schemas, limits. Each
iteration: call the model with tools available; if it returns no tool calls, finish. Otherwise validate
every tool call's arguments against the schema, authorize each one from the authenticated caller's
identity, classify risk, and either execute or create an approval proposal. Execution results â€” including
errors, which are results rather than exceptions â€” go back as tool messages, and the loop repeats. Four
limits, all enforced: maximum steps, maximum tool calls, wall-clock deadline, and a token budget. Plus loop
detection by fingerprinting the call set, so identical repeated calls terminate with a useful message
instead of a timeout. Tool calls the model requests together are executed concurrently only when I have
marked them independent and non-mutating; mutating tools serialize behind a per-resource lock. And the full
transcript is stored per step, because a state dump that you can replay is the difference between debugging
and guessing.

---

**Q14. How do you prevent a prompt injection from reading another tenant's data?**

âš« Expert

Structurally, not with prompts. Tenant identity is not an argument any tool accepts â€” it is injected by
the executor from the authenticated request, so a model that asks for `tenantId: "other"` is overwritten
before the query runs. Every query includes an explicit tenant predicate, and the same applies to
retrieval, so the wrong content never reaches the prompt in the first place. That is why I said the real
defence for indirect injection is capability reduction. Then defence in depth: retrieval results are
post-filtered by tenant, with a metric and an audit line if any candidate is dropped, because SQL filters
get refactored away eventually. Output side: identifiers in the answer must exist in the caller's data, so
even a leak is caught at the boundary. And my authorization layer never authorizes the loop once at the
start â€” it re-checks on every call, because a loop can run for many steps with different arguments while
identity can change underneath it. A red-team case in CI exercises cross-tenant attempts through query
phrasing, not just tool arguments.

---

**Q15. How do you evaluate a RAG system?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Two halves, measured separately, because improving the wrong one wastes weeks. Retrieval metrics are
deterministic given labels: recall@k on the candidate set, recall and precision at the final k, MRR, and
nDCG if I have graded relevance. I build those labelled queries first â€” a hundred to three hundred real
queries with the chunks a human says answer them â€” because chunk size and top-k are otherwise folklore.
Then the generation metrics: schema pass rate, groundedness, and task-specific success. Groundedness has
cheap deterministic proxies I always run (do citations resolve, do numbers appear in the cited source) and
a model-based judge for the rest, where I decompose the answer into atomic claims and ask a judge whether
each is entailed by the retrieved sources â€” per-claim is far more reliable than one holistic call, and it
localizes the failure. The judge is calibrated against human labels before I trust it, and I keep a small
human-audited anchor set in every run. The end-to-end metric that matters to product is task success, and
retrieval metrics are the cheap leading indicators I iterate on.

---

**Q16. How do you decide which model handles a request?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Three strategies, in increasing sophistication. Static routing by task is the default and captures most of
the savings: classification and extraction on a small model, synthesis and code on a large one. Difficulty
routing classifies the request and sends it up or down â€” signals like input length, whether reasoning or
code is needed, tool count, retrieval confidence, tenant tier. Cascading runs the small model and escalates
only when verification fails, which is the best of both but is a bet on the verifier: a bad verifier means
you pay for both calls and accept the bad answer anyway. Self-consistency is a cheap special case â€” run the
fast model twice with different seeds, accept if the structured outputs agree, else escalate. I track the
routing decision and its reason on every request, because a router silently sending everything to the
expensive model is invisible until the bill arrives, and I maintain a routing confusion matrix in the eval
set the same way I do for tools. And I re-evaluate whenever models change, because the tiers' relative
strengths change under me.

---

**Q17. How do you reduce the perceived latency of an AI feature?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

I separate three numbers: time to first token, total completion time, and p99 under load. They have
different fixes. For perceived latency, streaming is the biggest lever by far â€” often 3â€“5x perceived
speed with zero change to server latency â€” and it only applies to prose-shaped output; partial JSON helps
nobody. Second, move work off the critical path: pre-embedding, pre-summarizing conversations in the
background, reranking the previous turn's query while the user types the next. If a computation is not
needed for the first token, it does not belong before the first token. Third, prompt caching, which removes
the prefill for the stable prefix â€” often the largest single component of time-to-first-token with a long
system prompt. Fourth, reduce output tokens, because output dominates completion time and output tokens are
priced higher. Fifth, progressive disclosure: stream a plan or a skeleton so there is always something on
screen. And sixth, designed degradation tiers with an explicit budget â€” full quality, reduced context, fast
model, deterministic fallback â€” so a slow provider is a degradation rather than an outage.

---

**Q18. What happens when the model refuses?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

First, distinguish the cause, because the responses differ. A provider-side safety block is not
retryable and not prompt-fixable in general â€” that is a product decision, so route to a human or offer a
rephrased alternative. A refusal caused by *my own instructions* is a prompt bug, and it is more common
than people expect: "never state a price" plus evidence containing no price produces a refusal rather
than reporting missing facts. So my contracts need a graceful path â€” an `insufficient_evidence` variant
with a `missing` list â€” rather than a refusal path. And over-broad safety training hits legitimate
requests, particularly security questions, which is a product problem I address with framing and, where
appropriate, an authorized scope rather than by blanket-refusing. Operationally: measure refusal rate per
feature, alert on spikes â€” because refusals usually mean a prompt change or a data problem, not user
behaviour â€” and never retry a refusal.

---

**Q19. How do you know a prompt change is safe to ship?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

It has to pass a gate that I cannot argue with. Prompts are versioned code with a changelog and an
identity that travels with the request, and a "recipe" identifier binds prompt plus model plus schema plus
parameters plus retrieval config, because changing any of them changes behaviour. In CI: deterministic
tests including a snapshot of the rendered prompt and an assertion on the cache prefix hash, then the full
golden set with multiple samples per case compared against a recorded baseline with per-metric thresholds
and per-case regression checks, because the aggregate hides the broken slice. Thresholds include cost and
latency, so a quality gain that doubles token count has to be justified. Locally: offline eval, then shadow
traffic, then a canary at 1â€“5% with a kill switch that is a config flip. Because models are not
bit-stable, I compare distributions across samples and record the model version with the baseline, so a
silent provider change does not read as my regression. And every change adds a case, because a fix that
leaves no test behind will be undone.

---

**Q20. What is a vector store's recall/latency trade-off in practice?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

It is a dial I set per query class rather than a decision I make once. Exact flat search has perfect
recall and latency linear in corpus size, and it is genuinely fine up to roughly 10^5â€“10^6 vectors
depending on dimensions and hardware â€” many teams deploy a vector database when flat search on their
actual corpus would be faster and simpler. HNSW gets excellent recall and latency at the cost of memory and
build time, and `ef_search` moves you along the curve at query time. IVF builds faster with lower memory
but recall depends on `nprobe` and suffers from data drift unless periodically rebuilt. The operational
constraint people underestimate is filters: filtering by tenant or date alongside ANN is hard, and either
your filter is unselective or you get fewer than k results. So filter selectivity is a real design
constraint that shapes whether you shard per tenant. And the discipline that matters: always measure ANN
recall against exact search, and treat a filter-selectivity metric as a signal that your index design does
not fit your query shape.

---

**Q21. How do you keep an AI feature under budget?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Attribution first, because without it every decision is a guess. Every call logs usage, cost, feature,
tenant, model, cache status, latency, and success â€” unconditionally, including failures and aborts, because
those tokens were billed. Then the levers, roughly in order of impact: prompt caching, because a stable
prefix is close to free money and most teams underuse it; prompt and schema shape, because tool schemas and
retrieved context are input tokens on every request forever; model routing; context trimming in chat,
which is quadratic in conversation length if you resend full history; and semantic caching for stable
non-personalized answers. Around that, hard controls: per-tenant and global caps enforced *before* the
call with an estimate, a kill switch, and budget-aware routing that degrades to a smaller model rather
than failing. And the metric that actually governs decisions is cost per successful outcome, not cost per
call â€” a prompt change that raises tokens 40% to lift success from 80% to 92% is a good trade, and the
same change for 92% to 95% usually is not.

---

**Q22. Explain few-shot examples and when they beat instructions.**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Few-shot beats adjectives because adjectives are underspecified. "Be concise" has many satisfying
implementations, and the model picks one arbitrarily; examples pin down the format, the length, the tone,
and â€” most importantly â€” the decision boundary on judgement calls. Three well-chosen examples beat twenty
instructions. What matters more than count is selection: cover the typical case, the hardest edge case,
and a negative case where the correct behaviour is a specific non-answer. Beyond hand-picking, you can
retrieve the k nearest labelled examples by embedding at request time, which is cheap and reuses your RAG
machinery; or take cluster medoids for a fixed set; or select greedily for diversity subject to covering
known failure modes. The trade-off is tokens â€” examples compete with retrieved context for window â€” and
overfitting, which is why the eval set must contain no examples and must use different phrasings. And every
discovered failure should become a permanent example with a note saying why it exists, or it will be
deleted by a future maintainer.

---

**Q23. What is HyDE, and when would you not use it?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Hypothetical document embeddings: you ask a cheap model to *write a plausible answer*, embed that answer
rather than the question, and retrieve with it. The intuition is that queries and answers live in different
regions of embedding space, and a hypothetical answer resembles real documents more closely than a
question does, so recall improves. It helps on short, vague queries and in domains with a measurable
question/answer mismatch. I would not use it when the corpus is full of exact identifiers â€” error codes,
SKUs, part numbers â€” because the hypothetical answer paraphrases them away and lexical retrieval would
have nailed it. I also would not use it alone: I fuse the hypothetical answer's results *with* the original
query's results, so a bad hypothesis degrades gracefully. And it costs an extra model call, so I require it
to beat plain retrieval on my labelled set before keeping it. Related transformations I know: multi-query
fan-out, which is robust but multiplies searches, and step-back prompting for general context alongside
specifics.

---

**Q24. How do you keep index freshness?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

I set a staleness SLO and build a change-propagation path to meet it, because "we reindex nightly" is
not a design. Ingestion is idempotent and serialized per source, driven by webhooks where available plus a
polling reconciliation sweep on change tokens â€” webhooks are always lossy, so the sweep is not optional. The
big optimization is chunk-level diffing: compute a text hash per chunk and reuse the stored vector for any
chunk that is byte-identical, so editing one section of a 200-chunk document re-embeds 3 chunks instead of
200. Deletes propagate as soft deletes to documents, chunks, and parents, with a vacuum later to keep the
index clean â€” hard deletes immediately cost you the ability to debug "why did this come up?" Changing the
embedding model or chunker version is a full reindex, and I do it as a dual-index migration: write v2,
backfill in the background prioritizing hot documents, shadow-query both, compare recall, cut over, keep v1
warm for the rollback window. If the corpus is a versioned artifact, an immutable snapshot with a pointer
swap is much simpler and gives perfect as-of consistency.

---

**Q25. How do you make sure your LLM judge is any good?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

I treat it as untrusted until measured. Concretely: I hand-label 100 to 200 answers myself against the
rubric, then measure agreement with the judge on the binary supported/unsupported decision and on the
rubric scale, and I iterate on the rubric until agreement is high â€” the rubric is the artifact, the labels
follow from it. I use a different model family than the generator where possible, to avoid self-preference
bias. I run it at temperature zero with a fixed rubric and require it to quote the span justifying each
score, which makes it more consistent and auditable. I control position bias by randomizing dimension
order across runs and checking the verdict distribution is stable. Where I must choose between two prompts
I use pairwise comparison, which models do far more reliably than absolute scales. I cache judge results by
case, output hash, and judge model, because judge calls dominate eval cost. And I keep a human-audited
anchor subset in every run so judge drift is detectable. Uncalibrated, a judge is a random number
generator with good manners.

---

**Q26. What is speculative execution?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

You run a small, fast model to produce a draft, then verify it â€” ideally in a single forward pass â€” and
accept the draft if verification succeeds, giving large-model quality at small-model latency. The critical
constraint is that verification must be deterministic and essentially free. If the verifier is a model
call, you have paid for both and gained nothing; if it is a schema check, a numeric assertion, or a
deterministic rule, you have something real. It works well for classification, extraction, and simple
transformations, where acceptance rates above 50% make the arithmetic work. It fails badly on open
generation, where nothing cheap can verify the draft. The costs are real: rejected drafts are wasted
tokens, and you add a code path. So I measure acceptance rate, and if it is low I turn speculation off
rather than tuning it. A related trick that needs no extra machinery: self-consistency, running the fast
model twice at different temperatures and accepting only when the structured outputs agree.

---

**Q27. How do you handle tool-selection failures?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Description engineering first, because it is where the leverage is. The model gets exactly one document
per tool: your description. So disambiguate boundaries explicitly with contrast â€” "use for the customer's
own orders, NOT for product availability; use `search_catalog` for that" â€” because models choose by
contrast. Keep one tool per concept rather than `get_user` and `get_user_with_orders`. Put argument
constraints in the prose ("ISO-8601 dates only, do not pass 'last month'"), and describe the return
shape, which prevents hallucinated assumptions. Measure before and after: a labelled set of requests with
the tool a human would pick, scoring tool accuracy and argument accuracy separately, because a right tool
with wrong arguments is still a bug. Read the confusion matrix â€” errors are rarely random, and typical
patterns are diagnosable. And when the tool set grows past roughly fifteen, stop adding descriptions and
add a router that selects a tool group first, so the model only ever sees a small relevant list.

---

**Q28. What is your approach to an eval set?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Cases come from real traffic first, because that is the only distribution that does not drift. Then bug
reports, reviewer rejections from any approval queue â€” the model proposed something wrong there â€” expert
cases for rare high-stakes paths, red-team cases in a separate suite since they are adversarial rather than
representative, and synthetic cases last, because a model-generated case set encodes the generator's
biases. Each case stores the input frozen, expectations as *properties* rather than one exact string, a
free-form rubric, and metadata including the slice and the reason it was added. I build slices â€” by task
type, input length, tenant, risk, language â€” because aggregate scores hide the regressions that matter, and
I report per slice with confidence intervals so I do not over-read a four-case slice. And I treat the
dataset as a product with an owner: I never edit a failing case to make it pass, I only add, and I review
quarterly.

---

**Q29. How do you handle multi-agent systems?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Usually I would not build one. The honest accounting: context is not actually isolated in a way that pays,
because each subagent needs the goal, the background, and its own contract; error rates compound, so six
85%-reliable subagents give a workflow that succeeds about a third of the time; cost multiplies because
each re-reads the prompt and often the same documents; debugging becomes archaeology across six
nondeterministic origins; the routing and merge logic are themselves prompts needing versions and evals;
and parallelism is bounded by provider rate limits anyway. The three patterns that do earn their keep are
genuinely independent parallel work driven by a deterministic orchestrator, per-document context isolation
for very large corpora merged into a cited synthesis, and separate permission scopes so an
untrusted-content agent can never act. I require a named coordination problem and a named saving before
building one. Everything else should be one agent with a good tool set.

---

**Q30. How do you test code that calls a model?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Split into two worlds. Around the model, everything is normally testable and I test it normally: prompt
rendering is a pure function so it gets snapshot tests; retrieval, filtering, and indexing get deterministic
unit tests plus retrieval metrics against a fixture; schema validation, business rules, and authorization
get ordinary unit tests; streaming framing, cancellation, and budget enforcement get deterministic tests
against a fake provider â€” which I build, so the tool loop's unknown-tool path and the streaming protocol are
testable in milliseconds with zero cost. The model itself gets statistical evaluation: schema pass rate,
task success, groundedness, refusal and truncation rates, latency, and cost per success. And the statistical
side needs discipline: multiple samples per case compared as distributions, confidence intervals rather
than bare numbers, and a recorded model id and date, because hosted models are not bit-stable and a
provider change will otherwise read as my regression.

---

**Q31. How do you handle a long-running AI job?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

A queue, immediately. The handler enqueues and returns a job id; a worker processes with retries, and the
result arrives by notification. The worker contract is what matters: a deadline, heartbeats so a dead
worker is detected, incremental persistence of partial progress so a retry resumes rather than restarts, a
budget check *before* every billable operation, and error classification that distinguishes transient from
permanent so retries are not wasted. I use `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` for safe concurrent claims by many
workers, and I give interactive work a separate, smaller concurrency pool with higher priority, so a
tenant's bulk job cannot starve live traffic. Idempotency keys on enqueue, because user double-clicks and
at-least-once delivery both happen. Dead-letter handling with an on-call alert, since a silently stuck
batch is worse than a loud failure. And UX: a job id, real progress stages, a notification on completion,
and partial results visible â€” not a 20-minute spinner.

---

**Q32. What's the biggest risk in exposing tools to a model?**

âš« Expert

That you have confused "the model asked" with "the action is authorized." The model is untrusted input
that happens to be fluent. So authorization is re-derived from the authenticated caller's identity on
every call, using the tool's declared required scopes, and identity-bearing arguments â€” `userId`,
`tenantId`, `role`, `price` â€” are never accepted from the model; the executor injects them. That is the
confused-deputy problem: an injected model can be induced to specify arguments the real user could not.
Related surfaces: no generic file-read or HTTP-fetch tool, because each is an arbitrary-read primitive; no
tool that runs SQL the model wrote, or if unavoidable, a read-only role with a statement timeout, a row
limit, and no DDL; sandboxed execution for anything the model influences, with no ambient credentials and a
network egress allowlist; idempotency keys on mutating tools because retries and resumed loops re-issue
calls; and an immutable audit log that records refusals too, because the interesting attacks are the denied
ones. Confirmation with an argument hash for irreversible and financial actions.

---

**Q33. How do you use an AI coding tool on a real task?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Like managing a fast junior engineer. The brief has five parts: context â€” the relevant files, the
architecture, the conventions, the actual error, the existing patterns to follow; the goal stated as
observable behaviour; constraints â€” signatures to preserve, no new dependencies, error-handling
conventions, performance and security requirements; verification â€” the specific command to run and what
output to paste; and scope discipline â€” which files may change, and to stop and explain otherwise.
Sequencing matters more than the brief. For anything touching more than a couple of files I ask for a plan
first, review and correct it, then ask for the implementation; that converts a large unverifiable diff into
a small reviewable one. Then one change at a time, separating refactor from behaviour change. Tests
requested before the implementation for logic changes. Failures pasted verbatim, never paraphrased. And
then the verification discipline: I run everything myself rather than trusting the report, I break a test to
confirm it can fail, and I review the diff specifically for migrations, dependency changes, public API
changes, auth, and money.

---

**Q34. What kinds of bugs does AI-generated code produce most often?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

A very consistent catalogue. Plausible API hallucination â€” methods that sound right and do not exist, or
exist with different semantics. Off-by-one and boundary errors at 0, 1, nâˆ’1, n. Silently wrong logic that
returns the right type: wrong operator, inverted condition, null-versus-undefined, timezone, integer
division. Concurrency: unawaited promises, races, check-then-act. Swallowed errors, where `catch {}`
converts failures into wrong answers. Injection surface from string-built SQL and missing tenant filters.
Missing idempotency, so retries duplicate side effects. Naive datetime handling. Floating-point money.
Over-engineering with abstractions that have one implementation, and under-engineering with no validation,
no timeouts, no pagination. Copy-paste inconsistency, where one path in a near-identical block is missing
the filter. Tests that assert nothing. And comments that describe an earlier version. The two reviews that
matter most are the boundaries and error paths, and the security predicates â€” the happy path is usually
fine.

---

**Q35. How do you keep an AI feature's quality from regressing quietly?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Three defences. First, coverage: an eval set with slices and a CI assertion on minimum cases per slice, so
a language or task type you support but do not test is a visible gap rather than a silent one â€” the
failure I care about most is an aggregate that looks healthy while a four-case slice improved and a
single-digit slice collapsed. Second, the gate: per-metric thresholds, per-case regression checks, and cost
and latency in the same gate so a quality gain that doubles token count has to be argued for; plus a
cache-prefix hash snapshot, because prompt-layout regressions are the most common cost regression. Third,
online metrics: acceptance, regeneration, edit distance, escalation, and abandonment, sliced by recipe
id, sampled weekly against the eval distribution so drift is caught. And the process rule that makes it
work: every discovered failure becomes a permanent case, and no failing case is ever edited to pass. A
discovered failure that leaves no test behind will be reintroduced.

---

**Q36. When is RAG the wrong answer?**

ðŸŸ¡ Intermediate Â· **Must-know**

Four cases. When the answer is stable, short, and well-known to the model â€” nobody needs retrieval for
"what is OAuth." When the corpus is small enough to fit in context: a dozen documents go in the prompt,
because retrieval then only adds failure modes. When a structured query answers it exactly: aggregations,
filters, "my last five invoices" â€” that is SQL, and it is cheaper, faster, and correct. And when the
answer must be exact and verifiable, where a model plus a verifier beats a model alone but a verifier
alone beats both. The cost-shape argument is the one that persuades product teams: a model call costs
orders of magnitude more than a database query in both money and wall-clock time, so if the deterministic
version is two hundred lines it will be more correct, more debuggable, and more testable than any prompt
aiming at the same behaviour. Use the model for the genuinely fuzzy part â€” extracting structure from messy
text, summarizing, semantic matching â€” and determinism for everything with a spec.

---

**Q37. How do you make an agent safe?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

Three planes, and only some of it is prompt work. Input guardrails: length and shape limits, Unicode
normalization, stripping invisible and bidi characters, injection-pattern detection, encoding-blob
detection, and â€” the part people skip â€” trust labelling, so retrieved content is marked as untrusted data.
But input screening only raises the attacker's cost. Action guardrails are the real boundary: per-call
authorization from the authenticated caller, least-privilege tool sets per agent, confirmation bound to an
argument hash for anything irreversible, sandboxed execution with no ambient credentials and an egress
allowlist, idempotency keys, and hard caps on steps, calls, time, and tokens. Output guardrails: schema
validation, grounding checks, PII redaction, and safe markdown rendering with no raw HTML sink. And
human-in-the-loop checkpoints placed *before* irreversible effects and *before* drafts reach a customer,
with a real review queue that has SLAs, expiry that fails closed, and a diff so reviewers see what changed.
The discipline: design so that a fully compromised model still cannot exceed its intended authority.

---

**Q38. Explain context assembly and how you decide how much to include.**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

I fill a token budget, not a chunk count. I reserve budget for the system prompt, tool schemas, the
question, and the output, then add chunks by score until the remaining budget is consumed, with a
per-chunk cap and a diversity cap so one long document cannot consume the window. Ordering is a real
choice: for question answering the highest-relevance chunk goes *closest to the question*, since models
attend most reliably to the start and end of long context and the classic failure is the right chunk
buried in the middle; for long generation the strongest chunk goes first to set the frame. I dedupe
near-duplicates, since adjacent sections overlap heavily. Citations get machine-readable, resolvable
source ids, and I group chunks by document so related context is contiguous. And critically there is a
relevance floor: if nothing clears the threshold, I pass an explicit "no sufficient context" signal so the
model declines honestly instead of answering fluently from four irrelevant chunks. And I log the assembled
chunk ids, order, and token total on every request â€” without that, "the model ignored the right
document" is undebuggable.

---

**Q39. How do you decide between building and buying, or between models?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

The framing I use: default to the hosted frontier model for product features, because it is strongest and
fastest to ship, and add open-weight or self-hosted only for a specific reason â€” a hard data residency
requirement, very high steady volume where the GPU economics work, a need to fine-tune on your own
hardware, or a latency requirement that a distant endpoint cannot meet. Vendoring has real costs I
account for: a second data store, no cross-store transactions, operational burden, and a new on-call
surface. Vendor choice itself I decide by measurement, not reputation: build a labelled query set, run it
against two or three candidate models, and pick on quality metrics plus cost and latency. And I keep model
IDs in configuration with a promotion process, because changing a model is a regression event until evals
prove otherwise, and I run the eval suite against every model I might route to. The abstraction has to be
narrow with capability flags, and narrow enough that the escape hatches still work.

---

**Q40. What would you say to someone who thinks AI has made engineers less necessary?**

ðŸ”´ Advanced Â· **Must-know**

That the bottleneck moved, and it moved to the part that was always the hard part. Writing code was never
the scarce skill; knowing what to build, spotting the wrong abstraction, seeing that a retry needs an
idempotency key, that a timezone will bite, that this query needs a tenant predicate, that the eval set has
a coverage gap â€” that is the scarce skill, and AI does not do it. What AI does is widen the gap between
what you can produce and what you can verify, which makes verification the discipline that determines
whether you are faster or just less sure. Concretely in my own work: the review burden is now the
organizational constraint, so I respond by shrinking PRs, requiring the author to explain the change,
gating with automated checks, and reviewing hard on auth, money, migrations, and concurrency. And the
engineering that matters got *more* valuable, not less: a deterministic version of a feature is now often
the better engineering choice, because a model call costs orders of magnitude more and cannot be
tested, audited, or guaranteed.

---

---
# PHASE 13 RAPID-FIRE (55)

> One-line answers. Say the mechanism, then the reason.

---

1. **Stop reason?** Why generation ended; the only reliable truncation detector.
2. **`max_tokens` stop?** Truncated â€” raise budget, shrink prompt, or split the task. Never retry identically.
3. **Why normalize provider responses?** So a vendor shape change fails in CI, not production.
4. **Why capability flags, not lowest-common-denominator?** You would erase prompt caching and schema controls.
5. **Native schema mode?** Provider constrains decoding to satisfy your JSON Schema.
6. **Tool-call-as-output?** When no native schema mode: one tool whose parameters are your schema.
7. **Why a tolerant JSON parser?** Models emit fences, preambles, trailing commas, and prose.
8. **Why an `insufficient_evidence` branch?** A schema with no escape hatch forces the model to invent.
9. **Repair loop, max attempts?** One. Two at most, and never resend the whole original context.
10. **Cross-field business rules in a schema?** Impossible â€” enforce deterministically after parsing.
11. **SSE vs WebSockets?** Unidirectional, plain HTTP, auto-reconnect; use fetch + reader for POST bodies.
12. **Proxy breaks streaming?** Buffering â€” set `proxy_buffering off`, `no-transform`, `X-Accel-Buffering: no`.
13. **Keep partial output on abort?** Yes â€” mark it partial; deleting the user's work is unforgivable.
14. **TTFB budget separate from total?** Yes â€” patience ends at first byte, not at completion.
